A Unified Discretization Approach to Compute– Forward: From Discrete to Continuous Inputs
Adriano Pastore, Sung Hoon Lim, Chen Feng, Bobak Nazer, Michael Gastpar

Abstract
Compute–forward is a coding technique that enables receiver(s) in a network to directly decode one or more linear combinations of the transmitted codewords. Initial efforts focused on Gaussian channels and derived achievable rate regions via nested lattice codes and single-user (lattice) decoding as well as sequential (lattice) decoding. Recently, these results have been generalized to discrete memoryless channels via nested linear codes and joint typicality coding, culminating in a simultaneous-decoding rate region for recovering one or more linear combinations from K K users. Using a discretization approach, this paper translates this result into a simultaneous-decoding rate region for a wide class of continuous memoryless channels, including the important special case of Gaussian channels. Additionally, this paper derives a single, unified expression for both discrete and continuous rate regions via an algebraic generalization of Rényi’s information dimension.



I
Introduction

Consider a network information theory problem where one or more transmitters wish to communicate with one or more receivers, and our goal is to establish an achievable rate region for a wide class of memoryless sources and channels, both discrete and continuous. It is by now well-understood that there are many interesting network configurations where random codes with algebraic structure can be used to establish achievable rates that lie outside the rate regions available to i.i.d. random codes. This phenomenon was discovered by Körner and Marton in the context of a two-help source coding problem
[
1
]
where linear codes can be employed to compress the
sum
of dependent binary sources in a distributed fashion. Recent efforts have generalized this technique and applied it to a wide range of scenarios including multiple-access
[
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
,
7
]
, broadcast
[
8
]
, and interference channels
[
9
,
10
,
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
,
15
]
, physical-layer security
[
16
,
17
,
18
]
, distributed source coding
[
19
,
20
,
21
,
22
,
23
,
24
]
, and relay networks
[
25
,
26
,
27
,
28
,
29
,
30
,
31
,
32
]
. Broadly speaking, these efforts have handled discrete and continuous settings separately, employing (nested) linear codes
[
33
]
for the former and (nested) lattice codes
[
34
]
for the latter.

In this paper, we present a unified approach for handling discrete and continuous channels in the compute–forward framework. In this context, the goal is to reliably communicate one or more
linear combinations of codewords
across a multiple-access channel. This framework was initially proposed for Gaussian relay networks
[
27
]
, motivated by the potential gains of reliably implementing network coding directly on the physical layer. Subsequent efforts demonstrated that the compute–forward framework can serve as a building block in many other scenarios, such as multiple-antenna transceiver architectures
[
35
,
36
,
37
,
38
]
, interference alignment
[
12
]
, multiple-access strategies
[
12
,
36
,
39
,
40
]
, and distributed source coding
[
41
]
. The initial Gaussian compute–forward framework
[
27
]
utilized “single-user” decoding (i.e., decoding to the closest nested lattice codeword), and follow-up work generalized this framework to allow for sequential decoding
[
39
]
(i.e., utilizing recovered linear combinations as side information to reduce the effective noise). Naturally, one might expect further gains by generalizing the Gaussian compute–forward framework to allow for simultaneous decoding of multiple combinations. However, determining the simultaneous-decoding rate region for the Gaussian setting has remained an open problem, partly due to the challenge of characterizing this region via effective noise volume arguments for nested lattice codes. (The single-user and sequential arguments only require arguing about the effective noise variances.)

The foundations of network information theory can be derived using i.i.d. random encoding coupled with joint typicality decoding, as elegantly demonstrated in the textbook of El Gamal and Kim
[
42
]
. However, for schemes that exploit the algebraic structure of the code, it was until recently unclear how to establish achievability results via joint typicality. The work of Padakandla and Pradhan opened the door to such results by deriving joint typicality encoding and decoding methods for nested linear codes, and demonstrating gains for discrete memoryless multiple-access
[
5
]
, broadcast
[
8
]
, and interference channels
[
15
]
. Our own recent efforts have expanded this toolkit by developing a compute–forward framework for discrete memoryless channels that allows for simultaneous decoding, starting with the two-user setting
[
43
]
, and moving to the general case of
K
users and a receiver that wants
L
linear combinations
[
44
]
. One of the main ideas underlying these approaches is that, although the marginal distribution of a random linear codebook is uniform, multicoding (i.e., joint typicality encoding) can be used to select codewords with the desired type from a random nested linear codebook while preserving the overall algebraic structure. (This idea has also previously appeared in other contexts, such as trellis shaping
[
45
]
, nested lattice codes
[
46
]
, and sparse linear codes
[
47
]
.)

This paper presents a discretization approach that can translate the simultaneous-decoding, compute–forward rate region for the discrete memoryless case into a rate region for a wide class of continuous channels. The basic idea is to quantize the continuous channel inputs and outputs, evaluate the rate region for the resulting discrete channel, and finally take suitable limits of the quantization resolution in order to obtain a rate region for the continuous case. The primary obstacle that we need to surmount is that the simultaneous-decoding rate region for the discrete case is not expressed in terms of mutual information terms, but rather in terms of differences of discrete entropies. For mutual informations, the limits follow directly from standard arguments (see, e.g.,
[
42
, Sec. 3.4.1]
), whereas in our setting establishing the limits is considerably more challenging. To illustrate this point a bit more finely, consider a two-user discrete memoryless multiple-access channel with output
Y
where the
k^{\text{th}}
user transmits a (nested linear) codeword with marginal distribution
p(u_{k})
over the finite field
\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}
and the receiver attempts to decode the linear combination
a_{1}U_{1}+a_{2}U_{2}
(over the same finite field). From
[
43
]
, it follows that the following rate region is achievable via
single-user decoding
1
1

1

By single-user decoding, we mean that the receiver searches for a unique (nested linear) codeword that is jointly typical with the channel output and selects this codeword as its estimate of the linear combination. Simultaneous decoding yields a larger rate region, as described in detail in Section
V-A
, but the additional terms would obfuscate the simple contrast between discrete and differential entropies.

\displaystyle R_{k}<H(U_{k})-H\bigl(a_{1}U_{1}+a_{2}U_{2}|Y\bigr),~~~k=1,2.

(1)

Notice that these rates are unchanged if the receiver demands any other linear combination that lies in the span of the vector
[a_{1}~a_{2}]
. This is a desirable property, e.g., the receiver can simply obtain
2U_{1}+2U_{2}
by scaling
U_{1}+U_{2}
, and this should be reflected in the rate expression. However, as we move to the continuous case, we must keep in mind that differential entropies are not invariant under linear transformations. For instance, say that the channel inputs
U_{k}
are Gaussian and that the receiver wishes to decode the integer-linear combination
a_{1}U_{1}+a_{2}U_{2}
over
\mathbb{R}
. As a naïve first attempt, we might expect the (single-user decoding) rate region to take the form
R_{k}<h(U_{k})-h(a_{1}U_{1}+a_{2}U_{2}|Y)
. Of course, this fails to account for the fact that differential entropy is not scale invariant, e.g.,
h(2U_{1}+2U_{2})=h(U_{1}+U_{2})+\log(2)
. Overall, for the special case of decoding a single linear combination, this scaling issue can be handled by adding a correction term of the form
\log(\gcd(|a_{1}|,|a_{2}|))
where
\gcd
represents the greatest common divisor. It then follows from our prior work
[
43
]
that the following rate region is achievable via single-user decoding for the
two-user
Gaussian channel:

\displaystyle R_{k}<h(U_{k})-h(a_{1}U_{1}+a_{2}U_{2}|Y)+\log(\gcd(|a_{1}|,|a_{2}|))~~~k=1,2.

(2)

For decoding multiple linear combinations, the correction terms as well as the limit arguments become more complex.

To handle the general case of
K
users and
L
linear combinations, we introduce an
algebraic generalization
of Rényi’s information dimension and
d
-dimensional entropy
[
48
]
that allows us to express the simultaneous-decoding rate region for both the discrete and continuous setting via a single, compact expression. This leads to our main result in Theorem
3
: a simultaneous-decoding, compute–forward rate region for any continuous multiple-access channel of the form
Y=\sum_{k}h_{k}u_{k}+Z
as well as for any channel law with a finite input constellation. Notably, this theorem includes the Gaussian setting (with multiple antennas at the receiver) as a special case, and the resulting rate region is worked out in Corollary
1
, thus resolving the open problem described above. For completeness, we demonstrate how this framework recovers our earlier results for finite-field compute–forward in Theorem
1
and integer compute–forward in Theorem
2
. For comparison, we also derive sequential-decoding versions of these results in Theorems
4
,
5
, and
6
. The algebraic information dimension also enables us to organize and simplify the proofs of the required limit arguments, and may be useful in other settings, especially when attempting to map algebraic network information theory results from the discrete to the continuous case.

The paper is structured as follows: Section
II
defines notation, Section
III
describes the problem statement, Section
IV
introduces several notions (a generalized definition of entropy, matroids, lattices) that are needed to state the main results, Section
V
states our three main results, Section
VI
deals with particularizing our main results to some instructive special cases (sequential decoding, the two-user case, decoding a single linear combination, the Gaussian case), Section
VII
concludes our paper. The proofs of our main results are relegated to Appendices.

II
Notation

Several notational conventions differ from those in our previous publications
[
43
,
44
]
.

We denote the probability of an event
\mathcal{A}
by
\P\{\mathcal{A}\}
and use
P_{X}
,
p_{X}(\cdot)
,
f_{X}(\cdot)
, and
F_{X}(\cdot)
to denote the probability measure, the probability mass function (pmf), the probability density function (pdf), and the cumulative distribution function (cdf) of the random variable
X
, respectively.

The (positive) natural numbers, the integers, the real numbers, and prime numbers are denoted as
\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathbb{R}
, and
\mathbb{P}
, respectively. A field is generally denoted as
\mathbb{F}
and a finite field of order
\mathsf{q}
is denoted as
\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}
. Only finite fields of prime order are relevant in this publication, hence the symbol
\mathsf{q}
stands for a prime number throughout.

We denote column vectors with lowercase bold font whereas matrices are denoted with uppercase bold font. By convention, random vectors or matrices will be put in italic shape. For example, we would write
\P\{\bm{x}=\mathbf{x}\}
or
\P\{\bm{X}={\mathbf{X}}\}
.

For
{\mathbf{A}}
a matrix (or column vector) and
\mathcal{S}
a set of row indices,
[{\mathbf{A}}]_{\mathcal{S}}
denotes the submatrix of
{\mathbf{A}}
comprising only those rows indexed
\mathcal{S}
.

For any positive integer
p
, we define the bracket
[p]
as the set of integers
\{1,\dotsc,p\}
.
2
2

2

Note that we deviate from the convention in
[
43
,
44
]
, in which
[p]
stood for
\{0,\dotsc,p-1\}
.
For any odd positive integer
p
, we define the double bracket

\displaystyle\left\llbracket p\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\left\{-\frac{p-1}{2},\dotsc,-1,0,1,\dotsc,\frac{p-1}{2}\right\}.

(3)

In other words, for
p
an odd number,
\llbracket p\rrbracket
is obtained by shifting
[p]
to be centered around zero.

For any odd prime number
\mathsf{q}\geq 3
, we shall define the centered modulo-
\mathsf{q}
reduction

\mymod_{\mathsf{q}}(x)\triangleq x-\mathsf{q}\left\lfloor\frac{x}{\mathsf{q}}+\frac{1}{2}\right\rfloor.

(4)

In other words, for
x
an integer (or real scalar),
\mymod_{\mathsf{q}}(x)
is the unique element of
\llbracket\mathsf{q}\rrbracket
such that the difference
x-\mymod_{\mathsf{q}}(x)
is a multiple of
\mathsf{q}
. When endowed with the modulo-
\mathsf{q}
arithmetic, where addition ‘
\oplus_{\mathsf{q}}
’ and multiplication ‘
\otimes_{\mathsf{q}}
’ operations on two integers
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
are defined as

\displaystyle a\oplus_{\mathsf{q}}b

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mymod_{\mathsf{q}}(a+b)=\mymod_{\mathsf{q}}(\mymod_{\mathsf{q}}(a)+\mymod_{\mathsf{q}}(b))

(5a)

\displaystyle a\otimes_{\mathsf{q}}b

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mymod_{\mathsf{q}}(ab)=\mymod_{\mathsf{q}}(\mymod_{\mathsf{q}}(a)\mymod_{\mathsf{q}}(b)),

(5b)

the finite ring
(\llbracket\mathsf{q}\rrbracket,\oplus_{\mathsf{q}},\otimes_{\mathsf{q}})
is isomorphic to the finite field
\mathbb{Z}/\mathsf{q}\mathbb{Z}
. Therefore, we shall from now on identify the finite field
\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}
with

\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}=(\llbracket\mathsf{q}\rrbracket,\oplus_{\mathsf{q}},\otimes_{\mathsf{q}})

(6)

without loss of generality.
Additionally, we will denote as

\phi_{\mathsf{q}}\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}

(7)

the natural mapping that modulo-reduces an integer
x\in\mathbb{Z}
via the function
\mymod_{\mathsf{q}}
, and then maps
\mymod_{\mathsf{q}}(x)\in\llbracket\mathsf{q}\rrbracket
to its corresponding element in
\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}
.
The function
\phi_{\mathsf{q}}
(and occasionally, inverse images
\phi_{\mathsf{q}}^{-1}(\cdot)
) are invoked whenever it is important to explicitly distinguish between integer numbers and their finite-field counterparts. When applied on vectors or matrices, these functions act entrywise.

Finally, if
\mathbb{U}
denotes a ring and
\mathbb{A}\subseteq\mathbb{U}
denotes a discrete additive subgroup of
\mathbb{U}
. We shall denote the
lattice
over
\mathbb{A}
generated by the columns of the matrix
{\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{U}^{n\times d}
as

\Lambda_{\mathbb{A}}({\mathbf{Q}})=\bigl\{{\mathbf{Q}}\mathbf{v}\colon\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{A}^{d}\bigr\}.

(8)

For the common special case
\mathbb{U}=\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{Z}
(for which lattices are often defined) we will generally omit the subscript ‘
\mathbb{Z}
’ and simply write
\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}})
for notational brevity. Such lattices are called Euclidean lattices.

By contrast, if the coefficients
\mathbf{v}
are not restricted to a discrete additive subgroup
\mathbb{A}
, but instead run over the entire alphabet
\mathbb{U}
, then we refer to the resulting set as the
span
of
{\mathbf{Q}}
, and denote it as

\Span({\mathbf{Q}})=\bigl\{{\mathbf{Q}}\mathbf{v}\colon\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{U}^{d}\bigr\}.

(9)

In principle, whenever
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{U}
is discrete, the concepts of
span
and
lattice
could be used interchangeably, but we will generally invoke the notion of
lattice
to comply with standard terminology.

Norms are denoted using the widespread double bar notation. That is, for a real-valued matrix (or vector)
{\mathbf{A}}=[{\mathbf{A}}_{i,j}]
, the
p
-norm is denoted as
\lVert{\mathbf{A}}\rVert_{p}=(\sum_{i,j}{\mathbf{A}}_{i,j}^{p})^{(1/p)}
and the infinity norm is denoted as
\lVert{\mathbf{A}}\rVert_{\infty}=\max_{i,j}|A_{i,j}|
. The
n
-dimensional unit ball of a
p
-norm is denoted as
\mathscr{S}_{p}^{n}=\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\colon\lVert\bm{x}\rVert_{p}\leq 1\}
and the
n
-dimensional unit ball of the infinity norm is denoted as
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{n}=[-1,1]^{n}
.

Given an indexed collection of sets
\{\mathscr{A}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, the
inner set limit
(or limit inferior) and
outer set limit
(or limit superior) of this collection are defined respectively as

\displaystyle\varliminf_{n\to\infty}\mathscr{A}_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigcup_{\{n_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}}\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathscr{A}_{n_{i}}

(10a)

\displaystyle\varlimsup_{n\to\infty}\mathscr{A}_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigcap_{\{n_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}}\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathscr{A}_{n_{i}}

(10b)

where the union in (
10a
) and the intersection in (
10b
) are over all growing sequences
\{n_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of natural numbers. If inner and outer set limits coincide, we say that
\{\mathscr{A}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
has a set limit

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathscr{A}_{n}=\varliminf_{n\to\infty}\mathscr{A}_{n}=\varlimsup_{n\to\infty}\mathscr{A}_{n}.

(11)

In addition, note that for a double indexed collection

,

\displaystyle\varliminf_{n\to\infty}\bigcup_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathscr{A}_{n,m}

III
General problem statement of compute–forward

Consider the
K
-user memoryless multiple-access channel (MAC)

(\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{X}_{K},P_{Y|X_{1},\ldots,X_{K}},\mathcal{Y})

(13)

which consists of
K
sender alphabets
\mathcal{X}_{k}
,
k\in[K]
, one receiver alphabet
\mathcal{Y}
, and a conditional probability distribution
P_{Y|X_{1},\ldots,X_{K}}
.

[2^{nR_{1}}]\to\mathbb{U}^{n}
encoder
\mathbb{U}^{n}\to\mathcal{X}^{n}

modulation
mapping
[2^{nR_{K}}]\to\mathbb{U}^{n}
encoder
\mathbb{U}^{n}\to\mathcal{X}^{n}

modulation
mapping
P_{Y^{n}|X_{1}^{n},\dotsc,X_{K}^{n}}
decoder
M_{1}
M_{K}
U_{1}^{n}

Y^{n}
\hat{W}^{n}_{\bm{a}_{1}},\ldots,\hat{W}^{n}_{\bm{a}_{L}}

transmitter
1

transmitter
K

Fig. 1:
System model

Consider a ring
\mathbb{U}
and a subset
\mathbb{A}\subseteq\mathbb{U}
thereof. Let
\bm{a}_{1},\ldots,\bm{a}_{L}\in\mathbb{A}^{K}
denote
L
coefficient vectors, assume
L\leq K
, and define the coefficient matrix

{\mathbf{A}}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{a}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\bm{a}_{L}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{A}^{L\times K}.

(14)

A
(2^{nR_{1}},\dotsc,2^{nR_{K}},n)
code for compute–forward over
(\mathbb{U},\mathbb{A})
with coefficient matrix
{\mathbf{A}}
consists of

•

K
message sets
[2^{nR_{k}}]
,
k\in[K]
;

•

K
encoders, where encoder
k
maps a message
m_{k}\in[2^{nR_{k}}]
to a codeword
u^{n}_{k}(m_{k})\in\mathbb{U}^{n}
such that
u^{n}_{k}(m_{k})
is
one-to-one
;

•

K
modulation mappings
\mathbb{U}^{n}\to\mathcal{X}^{n}
that map
3
3

3

Modulation mappings may not necessarily be one-to-one mappings in general.
the entries of a codeword
u^{n}_{k}(m_{k})\in\mathbb{U}^{n}
to a
physical codeword

x^{n}_{k}(m_{k})\in\mathbb{U}^{n}
;

•

L
linear combinations (where
L\leq K
) for each message tuple
(m_{1},\ldots,m_{K})

\begin{bmatrix}w^{n}_{\bm{a}_{1}}(m_{1},\ldots,m_{K})\\
\vdots\\
w^{n}_{\bm{a}_{L}}(m_{1},\ldots,m_{K})\end{bmatrix}={\mathbf{A}}\begin{bmatrix}u^{n}_{1}(m_{1})\\
\vdots\\
u^{n}_{K}(m_{K})\end{bmatrix},

where additions and multiplications are defined over the vector space
\mathbb{U}^{n}
, and

•

a decoder that assigns estimates
(\hat{w}^{n}_{\bm{a}_{1}},\ldots,\hat{w}^{n}_{\bm{a}_{L}})\in\mathbb{U}^{n}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{U}^{n}
to each received sequence
y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}
.

Each message
M_{k}
is independently and uniformly drawn from
[2^{nR_{k}}]
. The average probability of error is defined as
P_{e}^{(n)}=\P\big\{(\hat{W}^{n}_{\bm{a}_{1}},\ldots,\hat{W}^{n}_{\bm{a}_{L}})\neq(W^{n}_{\bm{a}_{1}},\ldots,W^{n}_{\bm{a}_{L}})\big\}
. We say that a rate tuple
(R_{1},\ldots,R_{K})
is achievable for computing the linear combinations with coefficient matrix
{\mathbf{A}}
if there exists a sequence of
(2^{nR_{1}},\ldots,2^{nR_{K}},n)
compute–forward codes such that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}P_{e}^{(n)}=0
.

IV
Preliminaries

In this Section, we lay out some important concepts, notations and auxiliary results that will be needed in later Sections. These include

•

a new definition of entropy, dubbed
algebraic entropy
, that generalizes beyond the classic Shannon (discrete) entropy and differential entropy, and incorporates a matrix-valued parameter (Section
IV-A
);

•

a collection of definitions relative to Euclidean lattices and some classic fundamental results from the geometry of numbers (Section
IV-C
);

•

a definition of matroids and some key results from matroid theory (Section
IV-D
).

These preliminaries will be necessary for a complete understanding of the main Theorems presented in Section
V
, as well as their proofs. The present Section
IV
may be skipped at first reading.

IV-A

Algebraic entropy and information dimension

For a probability space
(\Omega,\Sigma,\mu)
defined on the reals
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and a reference measure
\rho\gg\mu
, we define the functional

D(\mu\|\rho)=\int_{\Omega}\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mathrm{d}\rho}\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mathrm{d}\rho}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\rho,

(15)

which is often referred to as the Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence, when
\rho
and
\mu
are probability measures.

If
\bm{u}\sim\mu
is discrete with support set
\mathcal{U}
and
\rho
is the counting measure, then
-D(\mu\|\rho)
becomes equal to the discrete entropy (also known as the Shannon entropy)

H(\bm{u})=-\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{U}}\P\{\bm{u}=\mathbf{u}\}\log\P\{\bm{u}=\mathbf{u}\}.

(16)

If instead
\bm{u}\sim\mu
is absolutely continuous and
\rho
is the Lebesgue measure, then
-D(\mu\|\rho)
becomes equal to the differential entropy

h(\bm{u})=-\int f_{\bm{u}}(\mathbf{u})\log f_{\bm{u}}(\mathbf{u})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u}

(17)

where
f_{\bm{u}}=\mathrm{d}\mu/\mathrm{d}\lambda
(
\lambda
denoting the Lebesgue measure) is referred to as the probability density function of
\bm{u}\sim\mu
.

In his 1959 paper
[
48
]
, Rényi elucidates some interesting connections between the discrete entropy of quantized variables and differential entropies. In particular, he introduces the concepts of
information dimension
and
d
-dimensional entropy
, which we recall in the following definitions.

Definition 1

(Information dimension and
d
-dimensional entropy)
.

For a random variable
\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the information dimension
d(\bm{u})
and the
d
-dimensional entropy
\mathcal{H}(\bm{u})
are defined as
[
48
]

\displaystyle d(\bm{u})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\frac{H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)}{\log(\nu)}

(18a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(\bm{u})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\Bigl\{H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)-d(\bm{u})\log(\nu)\Bigr\}

(18b)

provided the limits
d(\bm{u})
and
\mathcal{H}(\bm{u})
exist.

We now generalize these definitions by Rényi to the effect of incorporating a coefficient matrix as an additional parameter.

Definition 2

(Algebraic information dimension and algebraic entropy)
.

For a random vector
\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the algebraic information dimension
d_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u})
and the algebraic entropy
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u})
with coefficient matrix
{\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
are defined respectively as

\displaystyle d_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\frac{H({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)}{\log(\nu)}

(19a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\liminf_{\nu\to\infty}\Bigl\{H({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)-d_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u})\log(\nu)\Bigr\}

(19b)

if the limit
d_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u})
exists. As a general convention, whenever
{\mathbf{Q}}
is the identity matrix, we may omit the subscript and write
d(\bm{u})
and
\mathcal{H}(\bm{u})
instead, so as to recover the quantities from Definition
1
.

Some remarks are in order:

1.

The quantities
d_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u})
and
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u})
depend on the matrix
{\mathbf{Q}}
only via its row span, since for any square full-rank
{\mathbf{R}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
, we have
d_{{\mathbf{R}}{\mathbf{Q}}}(\bm{u})=d_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u})
and
\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{R}}{\mathbf{Q}}}(\bm{u})=\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u})
.

2.

The quantities
d(\bm{u})
and
\mathcal{H}(\bm{u})
from Definition
1
correspond to special cases of
d_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u})
and
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u})
when
m=n
and
{\mathbf{Q}}
is some square full-rank matrix (e.g., the identity matrix).

3.

Conversely,
d_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u})
and
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u})
can only ever be different from
d(\bm{u})
and
\mathcal{H}(\bm{u})
, respectively, if the row span of
{\mathbf{Q}}
is a strict subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, i.e., if
{\mathbf{Q}}
has rank less than its number of columns.
Whenever an information dimension or entropy in this paper is denoted with a subscript matrix like
{\mathbf{Q}}
, the latter typically stands for a strictly broad matrix with full row rank.

To complement Definition
2
, we further define conditional versions of the quantities
d_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u})
and
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u})
as follows.

Definition 3

(Conditional algebraic information dimension and entropy)
.

For a pair of random variables
(\bm{u},Y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathcal{Y}
, the conditional algebraic information dimension
d_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u}|Y)
and the conditional algebraic entropy
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u}|Y)
with coefficient matrix
{\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
are defined respectively as

\displaystyle d_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u}|Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\frac{H({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y)}{\log(\nu)}

(20a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u}|Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\liminf_{\nu\to\infty}\Bigl\{H({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y)-d_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u}|Y)\log(\nu)\Bigr\}

(20b)

if the limit
d_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u}|Y)
exists. If
\rank({\mathbf{Q}})=n
, we omit the subscript
{\mathbf{Q}}
and denote them as
d(\bm{u}|Y)
and
\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)
, respectively.

Definition 4

(Joint algebraic information dimension and entropy)
.

For a pair of random variables
(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}}
, the joint algebraic information dimension
d_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{1},{\mathbf{Q}}_{2}}(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2})
and the joint algebraic entropy
\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{1},{\mathbf{Q}}_{2}}(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2})
with coefficient matrices
{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}\times n_{1}}
and
{\mathbf{Q}}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{2}\times n_{2}}
are defined respectively as

\displaystyle d_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{1},{\mathbf{Q}}_{2}}(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\frac{H({\mathbf{Q}}_{1}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}_{1}\rfloor,{\mathbf{Q}}_{2}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}_{2}\rfloor)}{\log(\nu)}

(21a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{1},{\mathbf{Q}}_{2}}(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\liminf_{\nu\to\infty}\Bigl\{H({\mathbf{Q}}_{1}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}_{1}\rfloor,{\mathbf{Q}}_{2}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}_{2}\rfloor)-d_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{1},{\mathbf{Q}}_{2}}(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2})\log(\nu)\Bigr\}

(21b)

if the limit
d_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{1},{\mathbf{Q}}_{2}}(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2})
exists. If
\rank({\mathbf{Q}}_{1})=n_{1}
and
\rank({\mathbf{Q}}_{2})=n_{2}
, we omit the subscript
{\mathbf{Q}}_{1},{\mathbf{Q}}_{2}
and write
d(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2}|Y)
and
\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2}|Y)
instead, respectively.

In addition, the notion of joint and conditional algebraic information dimension, as well as joint and conditional algebraic entropy, are defined by straightforward combination of Definitions
3
and
4
. In the following two lemmata, we provide two elementary relationships which will be used in later proofs, namely, a chain rule of information dimension and entropy, as well as a connection to mutual information.

Lemma 1

(Chain rule for conditional algebraic information dimension and entropy)
.

For a triple of random variables
(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2},Y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}}\times\mathcal{Y}
such that the conditional information dimensions
d(\bm{u}_{1}|Y)
and
d(\bm{u}_{2}|Y,\bm{u}_{1})
exist and the mutual information
I(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2};Y)
is finite,
4
4

4

Note that, in addition to the existence of
d(\bm{u}_{1}|Y)
and
d(\bm{u}_{2}|Y,\bm{u}_{1})
, the finiteness assumption on
I(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2};Y)
is essential, as evidenced by Example 1 in
[
49
]
.
the conditional algebraic information dimension
d(\bm{u}|Y)
and the conditional algebraic entropy
\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)
obey the chain rule

\displaystyle d(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2}|Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(\bm{u}_{1}|Y)+d(\bm{u}_{2}|Y,\bm{u}_{1})

(22a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2}|Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}_{1}|Y)+\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}_{2}|Y,\bm{u}_{1}).

(22b)

Proof:

The proof is deferred to Appendix
A
.
∎

Lemma 2

(Algebraic entropy and mutual information)
.

For a pair of random variables
(\bm{u},Y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathcal{Y}
with finite mutual information
I(\bm{u};Y)
, we have

I(\bm{u};Y)=\mathcal{H}(\bm{u})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y).

(23)

Proof:

The proof is deferred to Appendix
B
.
∎

As we have already hinted, algebraic entropy is closely connected to discrete entropy (for discrete variables) and to differential entropy (for continuous variables). These connections are elicited in the following two subsections.

IV-A
1
Discrete distributions

The next lemma characterizes the algebraic information dimension and entropy of discretely supported distributions.

Lemma 3
.

For an integer matrix
{\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times n}
and an integer random variable
\bm{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
with finite discrete entropy
H(\bm{u})
, the algebraic information dimension and algebraic entropy are given by

\displaystyle d_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(24a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}).

(24b)

By convention, we extend (
24a
)–(
24b
) to also hold for vectors over a finite field
\bm{u}\in\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}^{n}
with a coefficient matrix
{\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}^{m\times n}
.
5
5

5

This can be justified by the fact that
\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}
is isomorphic to
(\llbracket\mathsf{q}\rrbracket,\oplus_{\mathsf{q}},\otimes_{\mathsf{q}})
or
\mathbb{Z}/\mathsf{q}\mathbb{Z}
, which live on finite (and thus discrete) subsets of the real field
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof:

The proof is deferred to Appendix
C
.
∎

IV-A
2
Continuous distributions

Prior to stating an important identity, which is the counterpart to Lemma
3
for continuous distributions, in that it relates algebraic entropy to differential entropy, we need some preliminary definitions.

Definition 5

(Unimodular matrix)
.

A square integer matrix
{\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n\times n}
is said to be
invertible
or
unimodular
if there exists an integer matrix
{\mathbf{Q}}^{-1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n\times n}
(called the
inverse
) such that
{\mathbf{Q}}{\mathbf{Q}}^{-1}={\mathbf{Q}}^{-1}{\mathbf{Q}}={\mathbf{I}}
.
The following statements are equivalent:

1.

{\mathbf{Q}}
is unimodular

2.

\left|\det({\mathbf{Q}})\right|=1
.

Proof:

If
{\mathbf{Q}}
is unimodular, then
{\mathbf{Q}}{\mathbf{Q}}^{-1}={\mathbf{I}}
and so
\det({\mathbf{Q}})\det({\mathbf{Q}}^{-1})=1
. Note that both
\det({\mathbf{Q}})
and
\det({\mathbf{Q}}^{-1})
are integers, since
{\mathbf{Q}}
and
{\mathbf{Q}}^{-1}
are integer matrices. This implies that
\left|\det({\mathbf{Q}})\right|=\left|\det({\mathbf{Q}}^{-1})\right|=1
. On the other hand, by Cramer’s rule, for any matrix
{\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
,
{\mathbf{Q}}^{-1}=(\det({\mathbf{Q}}))^{-1}\mbox{adj}({\mathbf{Q}})
, where
\mbox{adj}({\mathbf{Q}})
is the adjugate matrix of
{\mathbf{Q}}
. In particular, if
{\mathbf{Q}}
is an integer matrix, so is
\mbox{adj}({\mathbf{Q}})
by the definition of the adjugate matrix. Therefore, if
\left|\det({\mathbf{Q}})\right|=1
,
{\mathbf{Q}}^{-1}
is an integer matrix as well.
∎

Definition 6

(Right-invertible and left-invertible matrices)
.

A strictly broad integer matrix
{\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n\times m}
with
n<m
is said to be
right-invertible
if there exists a tall integer matrix
{\mathbf{Q}}^{\sharp}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times n}
(called the
right-inverse
) such that
{\mathbf{Q}}{\mathbf{Q}}^{\sharp}={\mathbf{I}}_{n}
.

Definition 7

(Smith normal form and elementary divisors)
.

For any integer matrix
{\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times n}
, there exists a non-negative integer diagonal matrix
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{Q}})=\diag(\sigma_{1}({\mathbf{Q}}),\sigma_{2}({\mathbf{Q}}),\ldots,\sigma_{r}({\mathbf{Q}}))
with
r=\rank({\mathbf{Q}})\leq\min\{n,m\}
such that
6
6
footnotemark:

6

\sigma_{1}({\mathbf{Q}})\mid\sigma_{2}({\mathbf{Q}})\mid\dotso\mid\sigma_{r}({\mathbf{Q}})
and

{\mathbf{Q}}=\tilde{{\mathbf{S}}}({\mathbf{Q}})\begin{bmatrix}{\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{Q}})&\mynull\\
\mynull&\mynull\end{bmatrix}\tilde{{\mathbf{T}}}({\mathbf{Q}})

(25)

for some unimodular matrices
\tilde{{\mathbf{S}}}({\mathbf{Q}})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times m}
and
\tilde{{\mathbf{T}}}({\mathbf{Q}})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n\times n}
.
Equivalently, there exists a left-invertible
{\mathbf{S}}({\mathbf{Q}})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times r}
and a right-invertible
{\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})\in\mathbb{Z}^{r\times n}
such that
{\mathbf{Q}}={\mathbf{S}}({\mathbf{Q}}){\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{Q}}){\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})
. Here,
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{Q}})
is called a
reduced Smith normal form
of
{\mathbf{Q}}
and its diagonal entries
\sigma_{i}({\mathbf{Q}})
are called
elementary divisors
. They can be computed as

\sigma_{i}({\mathbf{Q}})=\frac{d_{i}({\mathbf{Q}})}{d_{i-1}({\mathbf{Q}})}

(26)

where
d_{i}({\mathbf{Q}})
is the greatest common divisor of the
i\times i
minors
7
7

7

a\mid b
means
a
divides
b
.
of the matrix
{\mathbf{Q}}
and
d_{0}({\mathbf{Q}})=1
.

Note that for a square full-rank
{\mathbf{Q}}
, we have
\det({\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{Q}}))=\prod_{i=1}^{r}\sigma_{i}({\mathbf{Q}})=\left|\det({\mathbf{Q}})\right|
.
The reader is referred to
[
50
]
for additional details on Smith normal forms.

7
7
footnotetext:
A minor of a matrix
{\mathbf{Q}}
is the determinant of a square submatrix of
{\mathbf{Q}}
.

Using the Smith normal decomposition, it becomes straightforward to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 4
.

The following statements about a strictly broad integer matrix
{\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n\times m}
(with
n<m
) are equivalent:

1.

{\mathbf{Q}}
is right-invertible

2.

{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathsf{T}}
is left-invertible

3.

{\mathbf{Q}}
has elementary divisors equal to
\sigma_{1}({\mathbf{Q}})=\dotso=\sigma_{n}({\mathbf{Q}})=1

4.

{\mathbf{Q}}
can be completed to a unimodular matrix
\bigl[{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathsf{T}}\ \ {\mathbf{R}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bigr]
with some
{\mathbf{R}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{(m-n)\times m}
.

5.

The
n\times n
minors of
{\mathbf{Q}}
are coprime.

Proof:

The proof is deferred to Appendix
D
.
∎

Lemma 5
.

For an integer matrix
8
8

8

More generally, a matrix with rationally dependent entries. Since generalizing this lemma so as to cover
algebraic (in)dependence
is not the focus of our work, we limit the statement to integer matrices.

{\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times n}
and an absolutely continuous random vector
\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with finite differential entropy
h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u})
and finite
H(\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor)
, the algebraic information dimension and algebraic entropy with parameter
{\mathbf{Q}}
are given respectively by

\displaystyle d_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rank({\mathbf{Q}})

(27a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{Q}}}(\bm{u})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h({\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})\bm{u}).

(27b)

Given its central importance, and its reliance on several auxiliary lemmata, we provide the proof of Lemma
5
in a separate subsection further below (Section
IV-B
).
Note that if
{\mathbf{Q}}
has full row rank, i.e.,
{\mathbf{Q}}
is broad with
\rank({\mathbf{Q}})=m
, then we have that
{\mathbf{S}}({\mathbf{Q}})
is unimodular and
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{Q}})
is full-rank diagonal, in which case (
27b
) can be alternatively expressed in terms of its Smith normal form
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{Q}})
rather than
{\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})
, namely,

\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{Q}}}(\bm{u})=h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u})-\log\left|\det({\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{Q}}))\right|.

(28)

If in addition to having full row rank,
{\mathbf{Q}}
is also right-invertible, then
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{Q}})
is unimodular. In this case, (
28
) further simplifies to
\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{Q}}}(\bm{u})=h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u})
.
9
9

9

In fact, in this case one can set
{\mathbf{S}}({\mathbf{Q}})
and
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{Q}})
to be identity matrices, and
{\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})={\mathbf{Q}}
.

Remark 1
.

Note that the right-hand side of (
24b
) can be replaced without loss of generality by
H({\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})\bm{u})
. Doing so will make Lemma
3
(for discrete distributions) closely resemble the formulation of its continuous counterpart (Lemma
5
).

IV-B

Proof of Lemma
5

We will need the auxiliary Lemmata
6
,
7
,
9
,
8
and
10
, all stated below. Lemma
7
is mostly a classic result due to Rényi
[
48
, Thm. 1]
. The proof of Lemma
5
hinges mainly on Lemma
10
, which is a generalization of a recent result due to Makkuva and Wu
[
51
, Lem. 1]
.

Lemma 6
.

Let
\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
be an absolutely continuous random vector with finite entropies
h(\bm{u})
and
H(\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor)
. Consider a countable
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{Q}
on
\mathbb{R}^{K}
and let
\lceil\bm{u}\rfloor_{\mathcal{Q}}
denote a quantization of
\bm{u}
with respect to
\mathcal{Q}
(wherein the atoms of
\mathcal{Q}
represent the quantization cells). Assume that the atoms of
\mathcal{Q}
have Lebesgue measure uniformly upper-bounded by
\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}>0
. Then

\displaystyle h(\bm{u})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle H(\lceil\bm{u}\rfloor_{\mathcal{Q}})+\log(\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}).

(29)

Proof:

The proof is deferred to Appendix
E
.
∎

Lemma 7
.

Consider an absolutely continuous random vector
\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with finite entropies
h(\bm{u})
and
H(\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor)
. Let
\bm{z}
be uniformly distributed on the cube
[0,1)^{n}
and independent of
\bm{u}
. The sequence

\displaystyle S_{\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor+\bm{z})-n\log(\nu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)-n\log(\nu)

indexed by
\nu\in\mathbb{N}
is bounded as

h(\bm{u})\leq S_{\nu}\leq H(\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor),

(30)

satisfies
S_{m\nu}\leq S_{\nu}
for any
m,\nu\in\mathbb{N}
and has limit

\lim_{\nu\to\infty}S_{\nu}=h(\bm{u}).

(31)

Proof:

The proof is deferred to Appendix
F
.
∎

Lemma 8
.

For any full row-rank integer matrix
{\mathbf{T}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times n}
and any random vector
\bm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, it holds that

\bigl|H({\mathbf{T}}\lfloor\bm{v}\rfloor)-H(\lfloor{\mathbf{T}}\bm{v}\rfloor)\bigr|\leq m\log\left(\frac{\lVert{\mathbf{T}}\rVert_{1}}{m}\right).

(32)

Proof:

The proof is deferred to Appendix
G
.
∎

Lemma 9
.

For any absolutely continuous random vector
\bm{v}=[V_{1}\dotso V_{n}]^{\mathsf{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, assume that
H(\lfloor\bm{v}\rfloor)
and
h([\bm{v}]_{\mathcal{I}})
for all
\mathcal{I}\subset[n]
are finite. Then, for any full row-rank matrix
{\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
, the entropies
H(\lfloor{\mathbf{Q}}\bm{v}\rfloor)
and
h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{v})
are finite.
10
10

10

For all entropy finiteness assumptions in the statement of Lemma
9
to be satisfied, it would suffice to assume that
H(\lfloor\bm{v}\rfloor)<+\infty
and
h([\bm{v}]_{\mathcal{I}})>-\infty
for all
\mathcal{I}\subset[n]
. In fact,
H(\lfloor\bm{v}\rfloor)>-\infty
is obvious from the non-negativity of discrete entropy, whereas
h([\bm{v}]_{\mathcal{I}})<+\infty
would ensue from
h([\bm{v}]_{\mathcal{I}})\leq H(\lfloor[\bm{v}]_{\mathcal{I}}\rfloor)\leq H(\lfloor\bm{v}\rfloor)<+\infty
, wherein the first inequality holds due to Lemma
7
.

Proof:

The proof is deferred to Appendix
H
.
∎

Lemma 10
.

For a right-invertible
{\mathbf{T}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times n}
and an absolutely continuous variable
\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with finite entropies
H(\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor)
and
h(\bm{u})
, we have

\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\Bigl\{H({\mathbf{T}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)-H(\lfloor\nu{\mathbf{T}}\bm{u}\rfloor)\Bigr\}=0.

(33)

Proof:

The proof is deferred to Appendix
I
.
∎

With the help of the above Lemmata
7
,
9
,
8
and
10
, we can now proceed to proving Lemma
5
straightforwardly.
Let
r
be shorthand for
\rank({\mathbf{Q}})
in what follows.
Recall that, according to Definition
7
, one can factorize
{\mathbf{Q}}
as
{\mathbf{S}}({\mathbf{Q}}){\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{Q}}){\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})
with left-invertible
{\mathbf{S}}({\mathbf{Q}})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times r}
, full-rank diagonal
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{Q}})\in\mathbb{Z}^{r\times r}
and right-invertible
{\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})\in\mathbb{Z}^{r\times n}
.
Using Lemma
10
, we can exchange
H({\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)
and
H(\lfloor\nu{\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})\bm{u}\rfloor)
in the limit as
\nu\to\infty
. Hence,

\displaystyle d_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\frac{H({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)}{\log(\nu)}

(34)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\frac{H({\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)}{\log(\nu)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\frac{H(\lfloor\nu{\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})\bm{u}\rfloor)}{\log(\nu)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d({\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})\bm{u}).

Here, the first equality follows from the left-invertibility of
{\mathbf{S}}({\mathbf{Q}}){\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{Q}})
.
Since
\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is absolutely continuous and
{\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})
is right-invertible, the vector
{\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
is absolutely continuous and consequently, has information dimension
r
. Hence
d_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u})=d({\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})\bm{u})=r
, which proves (
27a
).

Regarding (
27b
), recall that by its definition, the algebraic entropy is

\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u})=\liminf_{\nu\to\infty}\Bigl\{H({\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)-r\log(\nu)\Bigr\}.

(35)

Again, Lemma
10
allows us to exchange
H({\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)
with
H(\lfloor\nu{\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})\bm{u}\rfloor)
inside the limit, leading to

\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u})=\liminf_{\nu\to\infty}\Bigl\{H(\lfloor\nu{\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})\bm{u}\rfloor)-r\log(\nu)\Bigr\}.

(36)

Since
H(\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor)
is finite by assumption, we have that
H(\lfloor{\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})\bm{u}\rfloor)
is also finite, because by Lemma
8
,
H(\lfloor{\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})\bm{u}\rfloor)\leq H({\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor)+r\log\frac{\lVert{\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})\rVert_{1}}{r}\leq H(\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor)+r\log\frac{\lVert{\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})\rVert_{1}}{r}
.
Hence, since
H(\lfloor{\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})\bm{u}\rfloor)
and
h({\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})\bm{u})
are both finite, we conclude from Lemma
7

that the right-hand side of (
36
) equals
h({\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})\bm{u})
, which concludes the proof of (
27b
).

Remark 2
.

Without much additional effort, one could prove a more general result, similar to
[
48
, Thm. 3]
, that has Lemmata
3
and
5
as corollaries. Namely, that for any non-singular
\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, which by Lebesgue’s decomposition theorem can be uniquely decomposed as
\bm{u}\sim\mu=\alpha\mu_{\mathrm{d}}+(1-\alpha)\mu_{\mathrm{c}}
(where
\mu_{\mathrm{d}}
is a measure supported on a discrete subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
\mu_{\mathrm{c}}
is an absolutely continuous measure on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
), the algebraic information dimension and entropy can be expressed as

\displaystyle d_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-\alpha)\rank({\mathbf{Q}})

(37a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha H({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}_{\mathrm{d}})+(1-\alpha)h({\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})\bm{u}_{\mathrm{c}})-H_{\mathsf{b}}(\alpha)

(37b)

provided that the entropies
H(\bm{u}_{\mathrm{d}})
,
H(\lfloor\bm{u}_{\mathrm{c}}\rfloor)
and
h([\bm{u}_{\mathrm{c}}]_{\mathcal{I}})
are finite for every index set
\mathcal{I}\subset[n]
, where
\bm{u}_{\mathrm{d}}\sim\mu_{\mathrm{d}}
and
\bm{u}_{\mathrm{c}}\sim\mu_{\mathrm{c}}
, and where
H_{\mathsf{b}}(\alpha)=-\alpha\log(\alpha)-(1-\alpha)\log(1-\alpha)
denotes the binary entropy function. Setting
\alpha=1
in (
37a
)–(
37b
) recovers Lemma
3
, whereas setting
\alpha=0
recovers Lemma
5
.

IV-C

Euclidean lattices

In the following we will state some frequently used results from lattice theory.
Let
\mathscr{K}
denote any closed convex body of positive volume in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
that is symmetric around the origin, in the sense that
\mathbf{x}\in\mathscr{K}
implies
-\mathbf{x}\in\mathscr{K}
.

Definition 8

(Euclidean lattice and lattice dimension)
.

In Euclidean space, the lattice
\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
generated by the linearly independent columns of a matrix
{\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
is defined as

\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}})=\bigl\{{\mathbf{Q}}\mathbf{v}\colon\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\bigr\}.

(38)

The matrix
{\mathbf{Q}}
is called a
generator matrix
or
basis
of the lattice, and by the above linear independence assumption, has full column rank.
A more general definition of lattices (beyond Euclidean space) was given in Section
II
.

The column rank
d
of the generator matrix
{\mathbf{Q}}
is the
rank
of the lattice
\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}})
. It represents the largest number of linearly independent vectors that can be constructed with lattice elements.

Definition 9

(Lattice determinant)
.

For an
n
-dimensional lattice
\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
of rank
d
with a basis
{\mathbf{Q}}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{q}_{1}&\dotso&\mathbf{q}_{d}\end{bmatrix}
, the parallelepiped

\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\alpha_{i}\mathbf{q}_{i}\colon 0\leq\alpha_{i}<1\right\}

(39)

is called the
fundamental domain
of
\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}})
associated to the basis
{\mathbf{Q}}
.
The volume of this parallelepiped is called the
determinant

\det(\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}}))
of the lattice. It is invariant with respect to the choice of basis and is equal to

\det(\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}}))=\sqrt{\det({\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\mathbf{Q}})}.

(40)

For a full-rank lattice, this expression simplifies to
\det(\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}}))=\left|\det({\mathbf{Q}})\right|
.

Definition 10

(Gauge, successive minima and minimal basis)
.

The gauge
g\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
of
\mathscr{K}
is defined as the function

g(\mathbf{x})=\min\bigl\{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\colon\mathbf{x}\in\lambda\mathscr{K}\bigr\}.

(41)

A
d
-dimensional lattice
\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
has
d

successive minima
. The
k
-th successive minimum
\lambda_{k}({\mathbf{Q}})
of
\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}})
with respect to
\mathscr{K}
(or with respect to the gauge
g
) is defined as the infimum of positive numbers
\lambda>0
such that
\lambda\mathscr{K}
contains at least
k
linearly independent vectors of
\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}})
. We have

\lambda_{1}({\mathbf{Q}})\leq\dotso\leq\lambda_{d}({\mathbf{Q}}).

(42)

The symmetric convex body
\mathscr{K}
with respect to which successive minima are defined, will always be clear from context. To make notation unambiguous, we may sometimes write
\lambda_{p,i}({\mathbf{Q}})
(instead of
\lambda_{i}({\mathbf{Q}})
) when the successive minima are with respect to the
p
-norm ball
\mathscr{S}_{p}^{n}=\{\bm{x}\colon\lVert\bm{x}\rVert_{p}\leq 1\}
.

Definition 11

(Korkin–Zolotarev reduced basis)
.

Let
{\mathbf{Q}}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{q}_{1}&\dotsc&\mathbf{q}_{d}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n\times d}
be an integer lattice basis and let us define
\mu_{i,j}={\mathbf{q}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{q}_{j}}/{\lVert\mathbf{q}_{j}\rVert_{2}^{2}}
.
The basis
{\mathbf{Q}}
is reduced in the sense of Korkin and Zolotarev if it satisfies the following set of recursive conditions:

•

\mathbf{q}_{1}
is a shortest non-zero vector of
\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}})
in the Euclidean norm;

•

\left|\mu_{i,1}\right|\leq\frac{1}{2}
for
2\leq i\leq d
;

•

if
\Lambda^{(n-1)}({\mathbf{Q}})
denotes the orthogonal projection
11
11

11

Note that
\Lambda^{(n-1)}({\mathbf{Q}})
is not necessarily an integer lattice.
of
\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}})
onto the orthogonal complement of
\Span(\mathbf{q}_{1})
, then the projected basis
\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{q}_{2}-\mu_{2,1}\mathbf{q}_{1}&\dotso&\mathbf{q}_{d}-\mu_{d,1}\mathbf{q}_{1}\end{bmatrix}
is reduced in the sense of Korkin and Zolotarev.

Any subset of vectors
\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{q}_{1}&\dotso&\mathbf{q}_{\ell}\end{bmatrix}
(for any
\ell\in[d]
) form a sublattice basis that is reduced in the sense of Korkin and Zolotarev.

If
{\mathbf{Q}}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{q}_{1}&\dotsc&\mathbf{q}_{d}\end{bmatrix}
is a Korkin–Zolotarev reduced basis and
\lambda_{i}({\mathbf{Q}}),i=1,\dotsc,d
denote the successive minima with respect to the Euclidean norm (i.e., with respect to the unit sphere
\mathscr{K}=\{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\colon\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert_{2}\leq 1\}
), then we have
[
52
, Thm. 2.1]

\frac{4}{i+3}\lambda_{i}({\mathbf{Q}})^{2}\leq\lVert\mathbf{q}_{i}\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{i+3}{4}\lambda_{i}({\mathbf{Q}})^{2}\quad\textnormal{(for $1\leq i\leq d$).}

(43)

Definition 12

(Integer lattice)
.

A lattice
\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is called an
integer
lattice if it only contains points with integer coordinates, i.e.,
\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}})\subset\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. A lattice
\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}})
is an integer lattice if and only if
{\mathbf{Q}}
is an integer matrix.

Definition 13

(Orthogonal lattice and orthogonal basis)
.

Let
\ker({\mathbf{Q}})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times(n-d)}
denote the nullspace of the column space
\Span({\mathbf{Q}})
of
{\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
.

The
orthogonal lattice

\Lambda^{\perp}({\mathbf{Q}})\subset\mathbb{Z}^{n}
of a rank-
d
integer lattice
\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}})\subset\mathbb{Z}^{n}
is an integer lattice of rank
n-d
defined as

\Lambda^{\perp}({\mathbf{Q}})=\ker({\mathbf{Q}})\cap\mathbb{Z}^{n}.

(44)

The transpose of the Korkin–Zolotarev reduced basis of
\Lambda^{\perp}({\mathbf{Q}})
shall be denoted as
{\mathbf{Q}}_{\perp}\in\mathbb{Z}^{(n-d)\times n}
, so we have
\Lambda^{\perp}({\mathbf{Q}})=\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}}_{\perp}^{\mathsf{T}})
. By extension, we shall agree that
({\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathsf{T}})_{\perp}={\mathbf{Q}}_{\perp}^{\mathsf{T}}
.

Note that if
{\mathbf{Q}}_{\perp}
is broad (resp. tall) then it is right-invertible (resp. left-invertible). As a consequence, when it is applied on a right-invertible or left-invertible
{\mathbf{Q}}
, the
(\cdot)_{\perp}
operation is involutive, i.e,
({\mathbf{Q}}_{\perp})_{\perp}={\mathbf{Q}}
.

Furthermore, if
{\mathbf{Q}}
is left-invertible, then by
[
53
, Thm. 1]
we have the determinant identity

\det({\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\mathbf{Q}})=\det({\mathbf{Q}}_{\perp}{\mathbf{Q}}_{\perp}^{\mathsf{T}})

(45)

or equivalently,
\det(\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}}))=\det(\Lambda^{\perp}({\mathbf{Q}}))
.

Lemma 11

(Van der Corput’s Convex Body Theorem
[
54
, Thm. 3.3]
)
.

Let
\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
denote a full-rank
n
-dimensional lattice. If

\vol(\mathscr{K})\geq m2^{n}\det(\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}}))

(46)

then
\mathscr{K}
contains at least
m
symmetric pairs of non-zero lattice points in
\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}})
.
This Theorem is due to van der Corput
[
55
]
and generalizes the special case
m=1
, which is well known as Minkowski’s First Theorem.

In its contrapositive formulation, Lemma
11
states that, if
\mathscr{K}
contains at most
m
pairs of non-zero lattice points, then

\vol(\mathscr{K})<(m+1)2^{n}\det(\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}})).

(47)

Note that,
\mathscr{K}
being a symmetric body that contains the origin, it contains
m
pairs of non-zero lattice points if and only if it contains
2m+1
lattice points (including the origin), that is, if and only if
\left|\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}})\cap\mathscr{K}\right|=2m+1
. Hence, a corollary of Lemma
11
is the following inequality, relating the volume of any convex closed symmetric body
\mathscr{K}
to the number of lattice points it encloses:

\vol(\mathscr{K})<\left(\left|\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}})\cap\mathscr{K}\right|+1\right)2^{n-1}\det(\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}}))

(48)

Lemma 12

(Minkowski’s Second Theorem
[
54
, Thm. 3.4]
)
.

Let
\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an
n
-dimensional Euclidean lattice of rank
d
. Let
\mathscr{K}
be a symmetric convex body and let
\lambda_{i}({\mathbf{Q}})
denote the successive minima of
\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}})
with respect to
\mathscr{K}
. Then
12
12

12

Most references like
[
54
, Thm. 3.4]
state Minkowski’s Second Theorem for the case
n=d
. Our statement (
49
) is for
d\leq n
, which can be easily derived from the case
n=d
by restricting
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to its
d
-dimensional linear subspace that contains the rank-
d
lattice.

\frac{2^{d}}{d!}\det(\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}}))\leq\prod_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}({\mathbf{Q}})\leq 2^{d}\det(\Lambda({\mathbf{Q}})).

(49)

IV-D

Matroids

We define matroids and state some fundamental results from matroid theory that will be leveraged in the proofs of Theorems
2
and
3
.

Definition 14

(Matroids)
.

A matroid
M
is a pair
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{I})
consisting of a finite set
\mathcal{G}
and a collection of subsets
\mathcal{I}\subset 2^{\mathcal{G}}
satisfying the properties
[
56
, Sec. 1.1]
:

1.

\emptyset\in\mathcal{I}

2.

If
I\in\mathcal{I}
and
I^{\prime}\subset I
, then
I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}

3.

If
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
are in
\mathcal{I}
and
|I_{1}|<|I_{2}|
, then there exists an element
J\in I_{2}\setminus I_{1}
such that
I_{1}\cup J\in\mathcal{I}
.

We refer to
\mathcal{G}
as the
ground set
of
M
and to
\mathcal{I}
as the
independent sets
of
M
.

We say that
B\in\mathcal{I}
is a
basis
of
M=(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{I})
if there is no larger
B^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}
that contains
B
. In other words, a basis is a maximal independent set of the matroid. All bases have the same cardinality
[
56
, Lem. 1.2.1, 1.2.4]
and a matroid is uniquely defined by the collection of its bases, which we will generally denote as
\mathscr{B}(M)

[
56
, Lem. 1.2.2, Thm. 1.2.3]
.

The
size
of a matroid
M
is its ground set cardinality
|\mathcal{G}|
, whereas the
rank

r(M)
is the maximum cardinality of its bases.

Definition 15

(Representable matroids)
.

If
\mathcal{G}
denotes the set of column labels of a matrix
{\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{A}^{m\times n}
over a ring or field
\mathbb{A}
, and if
\mathcal{I}
denotes the set of subsets of
\mathcal{G}
such that for every
I\in\mathcal{I}
, the rows of
[{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathsf{T}}]_{I}
are linearly independent (in the vector space
\mathbb{A}^{m}
), then
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{I})
is a matroid, called the
vector matroid
of
{\mathbf{Q}}
(cf.
[
56
, Proposition 1.1.1]
) and is denoted as
M({\mathbf{Q}})
.

If a matroid
M
is isomorphic to the vector matroid of some matrix
{\mathbf{Q}}
over some ring
\mathbb{A}
, then we say that
M
is
representable
over
\mathbb{A}
or
\mathbb{A}
-representable. Accordingly,
{\mathbf{Q}}
is a (matrix)
representation
for
M
over
\mathbb{A}
. Note that not all matroids are representable over all rings or fields, and some matroids are not representable over any field
[
56
, Chapter 6]
.

We define
\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{A}}(n)
as the set of all representable matroids of size
n
that have a representation over
\mathbb{A}
. Regardless of the alphabet
\mathbb{A}
, the set
\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{A}}(n)
always includes the empty matroid
([n],\{\emptyset\})
and the full-rank matroid
([n],2^{[n]})
.

Similarly, we define
\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{A}}(M)
as the set of all matrix representations over
\mathbb{A}
of the matroid
M
.

Definition 16

(Characteristic set)
.

The characteristic set
\chi(M)
of a matroid
M
is the subset of
\mathbb{P}\cup\{0\}
containing all values
\mathsf{q}
such that
M
is representable over some field of characteristic
\mathsf{q}
.

Characteristic sets of representable matroids have the following properties:

Lemma 13

(Rado, 1957)
.

If
0\in\chi(M)
, then
\chi(M)
contains all primes except a finite set.

Lemma 14

(Vámos, 1971)
.

If
\chi(M)
is infinite, then
0\in\chi(M)
.

Proofs for lemmata
13
and
14
can be found, for example, in
[
56
, Proposition 6.8.2]
and
[
56
, Proposition 6.8.3]
.
In addition, let us point out the following basic equivalence regarding representability for characteristic
0
.

Lemma 15
.

For a given matroid size
n
, representability over the integers and over the rationals are equivalent. That is,
\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{Z}}(n)=\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}(n)
.

Proof:

The inclusion
\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{Z}}(n)\subset\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}(n)
follows immediately from
\mathbb{Z}\subset\mathbb{Q}
.
For the converse, consider a matrix
{\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{Q}^{m\times n}
that represents a matroid
M({\mathbf{Q}})\in\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}(n)
. Write the entries of
{\mathbf{Q}}
as fractions
Q_{i,j}=n_{i,j}/D
with
n_{i,j}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
D
a positive integer. Then
D{\mathbf{Q}}
is an integer matrix that represents
M({\mathbf{Q}})
. Hence
\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{Z}}(n)\supset\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}(n)
.
∎

Remark 3
.

The equality
\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{Z}}(n)=\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}(n)
does not further extend to finite fields in general. To appreciate this, consider the following matrix over
\mathbb{F}_{3}
:

{\mathbf{C}}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0&1&1\\
0&1&1&-1\end{bmatrix}.

(50)

Any
2\times 2
submatrix of this matrix has full rank, so
{\mathbf{C}}
is a representation of the so-called
uniform matroid
of size
4
and rank
2
,

M({\mathbf{C}})=(\{1,2,3,4\},\{\emptyset,\{1\},\{2\},\{3\},\{4\},\{1,2\},\{1,3\},\{1,4\},\{2,3\},\{2,4\},\{3,4\}\}).

(51)

However, this matroid is not representable over the binary field
\mathbb{F}_{2}
. In fact, one cannot create a set of four distinct
2\times 1
columns with binary entries such that all columns are pairwise linearly independent. Hence
\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}(4)\neq\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{F}_{3}}(4)
, and
\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}(4)\neq\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{Z}}(4)
.

Definition 17

(Dual matroids)
.

Let
M=(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{I})
be a matroid and
\mathscr{B}(M)
the collection of its bases. Then
\{\mathcal{G}\setminus B\colon B\in\mathscr{B}(M)\}
is the set of bases of a matroid on
\mathcal{G}
, called the
dual
of
M
, and denoted as
M^{*}
(cf.
[
56
, Thm. 2.1.1]
).

Note that if
{\mathbf{Q}}
is a representation of
M
, then
{\mathbf{Q}}_{\perp}^{\mathsf{T}}
is a representation of
M^{*}
.

V
A general formula for compute–forward achievable rates

In the following,
\mathbb{U}
denotes a ring and
\mathbb{A}\subseteq\mathbb{U}
denotes a discrete additive subgroup of
\mathbb{U}
, that is, a
lattice
over
\mathbb{U}
. As already anticipated in Section
III
,
\mathbb{U}
represents the alphabet in which auxiliary codewords
U_{k}^{n}
take value, such that the receiver attempts to recover linear combinations of these codewords, with coefficients in
\mathbb{A}
.

In this paper, the three main compute–forward theorems presented further below will be concerned with the following three choices of
(\mathbb{U},\mathbb{A})
, respectively:

•

(\mathbb{U},\mathbb{A})=(\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}},\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}})
(Theorem
1
);

•

(\mathbb{U},\mathbb{A})=(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z})
(Theorem
2
);

•

(\mathbb{U},\mathbb{A})=(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{Z})
(Theorem
3
).

Though we suspect that our theorems continue to hold for more general pairs
(\mathbb{U},\mathbb{A})
of rings/lattices, we limit the scope of this paper to the three above-mentioned cases.

Let
(\bm{u},Y)\in\mathbb{U}^{K}\times\mathcal{Y}
follow a joint distribution
P_{\bm{u},Y}=\prod_{k=1}^{K}P_{U_{k}}P_{Y|\bm{u}}
, where
P_{Y|\bm{u}}=P_{Y|X_{1},\dotsc,X_{K}}(y|x_{1}(u_{1}),\dotsc,x_{K}(u_{K}))
. In the following, for some natural numbers
1\leq L_{\mathsf{B}}\leq K
,

•

{\mathbf{B}}
denotes a full row-rank matrix over
\mathbb{A}
of size
L_{\mathsf{B}}\times K
;

•

M
denotes a matroid of size
L_{\mathsf{B}}
;

•

\mathcal{T}
denotes a subset of
[K]
.

In the following, we state achievability theorems for simultaneous joint decoding, and for sequential decoding.

V-A

Simultaneous joint decoding

With the above defined notations in mind, we first define the set

\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T})\triangleq\Bigl\{(R_{1},\dotsc,R_{K})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{K}\colon\sum_{k\in\mathcal{T}}R_{k}<\mathcal{H}([\bm{u}]_{\mathcal{T}})-\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{B}}}(\bm{u}|Y)+J({\mathbf{B}},M)\Bigr\}

(52)

where
J({\mathbf{B}},M)
denotes a minimum-entropy term defined as

J({\mathbf{B}},M)\triangleq\inf_{{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{A}}(M)}\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}}(\bm{u}|Y)

(53)

and where
\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{A}}(M)
, as already defined in Section
IV-D
, Definition
15
, denotes the set of matrix representations over
\mathbb{A}
of the matroid
M
.
With these definitions settled, we then define the set

\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}})=\bigcap_{M}\;\bigcup_{\mathcal{S}}\;\bigcap_{\mathcal{T}}\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T})

(54)

where the three nested set operations are over triples
(M,\mathcal{S},\mathcal{T})
meeting the following constraints:

1.

M
iterates over all matroids of size
L_{\mathsf{B}}
except the full-rank matroid, i.e.,
M\in\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{A}}(L_{\mathsf{B}})\setminus([L_{\mathsf{B}}],2^{[L_{\mathsf{B}}]})
. Henceforth, we shall denote this set of matroids (excluding the full-rank matroid) as
\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{A}}^{\circ}(L_{\mathsf{B}})
;

2.

\mathcal{S}
iterates over all index sets that correspond to bases of the dual matroid
M^{*}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{B}(M^{*})
;

3.

\mathcal{T}
iterates over all index sets that correspond to bases of the matroid of which
[{\mathbf{B}}]_{\mathcal{S}}
(the matrix
{\mathbf{B}}
with rows selected by
\mathcal{S}
) is a representation, i.e.,
\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{B}(M([{\mathbf{B}}]_{\mathcal{S}}))
.

Finally, let us define the so-called
joint simultaneous decoding
rate region.

Definition 18
.

The simultaneous decoding rate region for recovering the
{\mathbf{A}}
-linear combinations of auxiliary codewords is defined as

\mathscr{R}({\mathbf{A}})\triangleq\bigcup_{{\mathbf{B}}}\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}})

(55)

where
{\mathbf{B}}\in\mathbb{A}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}\times K}
runs over all full row-rank matrices satisfying
\Lambda_{\mathbb{A}}({\mathbf{B}}^{\mathsf{T}})\supseteq\Lambda_{\mathbb{A}}({\mathbf{A}}^{\mathsf{T}})
.

The upcoming theorems will provide an operational meaning to this rate region. Meanwhile, a few remarks are in order.

Remark 4
.

Note that for some triples
({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T})
, the region
\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T})
might be an empty set because the right-hand side of the inequality in (
52
) can be negative. For example, consider the two-user case (
K=2
), with
(\mathbb{U},\mathbb{A})=(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z})
and the set
\mathscr{Q}([1,1],(\{1\},\emptyset),\{1\})
with an auxiliary
\bm{u}=(U_{1},U_{2})\in\{0,1\}^{2}
such that
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
are i.i.d. binary uniform: the right-hand side of (
52
) reduces to
1-H(U_{1}+U_{2}|Y)
, which can be negative.
13
13

13

A sufficient condition is for the channel’s sum mutual information
I(U_{1},U_{2}|Y)
to be smaller than
\tfrac{1}{2}
(bits), because it holds that
H(U_{1}+U_{2}|Y)=\tfrac{3}{2}-I(U_{1}+U_{2};Y)>\tfrac{3}{2}-I(U_{1},U_{2}|Y)
.

However,
\mathscr{R}({\mathbf{A}})
is never empty, because the union-taking over
{\mathbf{B}}
always ensures that
\mathscr{R}({\mathbf{A}})
does at least contain the region
\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{I}})
generated by the identity
{\mathbf{B}}={\mathbf{I}}
. The latter set is never empty. In fact,
\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{I}})
contains the corner points of the classic multiple-access rate region.

Remark 5
.

The joint decoding rate region
\mathscr{R}({\mathbf{A}})
is invariant to elementary row operations, in the sense that
\mathscr{R}({\mathbf{A}})=\mathscr{R}({\mathbf{Q}}{\mathbf{A}})
for any full column-rank
{\mathbf{Q}}
. This follows immediately from Definition
18
. At a high level, this is consistent with our interpretation of
\mathscr{R}({\mathbf{A}})
as a compute–forward achievable rate region (as we shall make precise in the main theorem statements): one should be able to recover the
{\mathbf{A}}
-linear combinations of codewords at the receiver if and only if one can recover the
{\mathbf{Q}}{\mathbf{A}}
-linear combinations.

Remark 6
.

In light of Remark
5
, it may be tempting to interpret the union-taking operation
\mathscr{R}({\mathbf{A}})=\bigcup_{\mathbf{B}}\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}})
as a means of assembling multiple rate regions
\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}})
that are
individually
achievable. As a consequence, one might be led to conjecture that, much like
\mathscr{R}({\mathbf{A}})
, each region
\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}})
is invariant against elementary row operations in the sense that
\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}})=\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{Q}}{\mathbf{B}})
for a full column-rank
{\mathbf{Q}}
. However, we have no proof or disproof of this claim, which we leave as an open question.

We now state the three main achievability Theorems.

The proof is deferred to Appendix
K
.
The proof is deferred to Appendix
L
.

Theorem 1

(Finite-field compute–forward)
.

Let
(\mathbb{U},\mathbb{A})=(\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}},\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}})
for some prime field size
\mathsf{q}
. A rate tuple
(R_{1},\dotsc,R_{K})
is achievable for decoding the
{\mathbf{A}}
-linear combinations of codewords if it is contained in
\mathscr{R}({\mathbf{A}})
for some auxiliary pmf
p_{\bm{u}}(\mathbf{u})=\prod_{k=1}^{K}p_{U_{k}}(u_{k})
and modulation mappings
(x_{1}(u_{1}),\dotsc,x_{K}(u_{K}))
.

Theorem 2

(Integer compute–forward)
.

Let
(\mathbb{U},\mathbb{A})=(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z})
and assume that
\mathcal{H}(\bm{u})
is finite. A tuple
(R_{1},\dotsc,R_{K})
is achievable for decoding the
{\mathbf{A}}
-linear combinations of codewords if it is contained in
\mathscr{R}({\mathbf{A}})
for some auxiliary pmf
p_{\bm{u}}(\mathbf{u})=\prod_{k=1}^{K}p_{U_{k}}(u_{k})
and modulation mappings
(x_{1}(u_{1}),\dotsc,x_{K}(u_{K}))
.

Theorem 3

(Continuous compute–forward)
.

Let
(\mathbb{U},\mathbb{A})=(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{Z})
. Assume that the vector of auxiliaries
\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
has an absolutely continuous distribution, finite entropy
H(\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor)
and finite differential entropy
h(\bm{u})
. In addition, we have either of the two (mutually exclusive) situations:

1.

the mappings
\mathbf{x}(\mathbf{u})
have finitely many (jump) discontinuities and images
x_{k}(\mathbb{R})
of finite cardinality;

2.

the mappings
\mathbf{x}(\mathbf{u})
and the channel law
P_{Y|\bm{x}}
are such that the resulting channel
P_{Y|\bm{u}}
is given by the system equation
Y=\sum_{k}h_{k}u_{k}+Z
with some real-valued coefficients
h_{k}
and real-valued independent additive noise
Z
.

Under these assumptions, a tuple
(R_{1},\dotsc,R_{K})
is achievable for decoding the
{\mathbf{A}}
-linear combinations of codewords if it is contained in
\mathscr{R}({\mathbf{A}})
for some auxiliary pdf
f_{\bm{u}}(\mathbf{u})=\prod_{k=1}^{K}f_{U_{k}}(u_{k})
and modulation mappings
(x_{1}(u_{1}),\dotsc,x_{K}(u_{K}))
satisfying the above (alternative) conditions.

Remark 7
.

When evaluating the term
J({\mathbf{B}},M)
in the context of Theorem
2
or
3
, we can restrict the integer matrix
{\mathbf{C}}
to being right-invertible without loss of generality. This is because
\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}}(\bm{u}|Y)=\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{C}}){\mathbf{B}}}(\bm{u}|Y)
for any
{\mathbf{C}}
, and because
{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{Z}}(M)
if and only if
{\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{C}})\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{Z}}(M)
.

V-B

Sequential decoding

Under sequential decoding, the receiver does not decode the linear combinations
W_{\mathbf{a}_{1}}^{n},\dotsc,W_{\mathbf{a}_{L}}^{n}
simultaneously, but instead it recovers them successively while using previously decoded linear combinations as side information. That is, for decoding the linear combination
W_{\mathbf{a}_{\ell}}^{n}
, the decoder uses
(Y^{n},W_{\mathbf{a}_{1}}^{n},\dotsc,W_{\mathbf{a}_{\ell-1}}^{n})
as side information. The decoder architecture for sequential decoding is depicted in Figure
2
.
The generalized rate region (corresponding to Theorems
1
–
3
and expressed in terms of algebraic entropies) is stated in the following.

decoder
1

decoder
2

decoder
L

Y^{n}
\hat{W}_{\mathbf{a}_{1}}^{n}
(\hat{W}_{\mathbf{a}_{1}}^{n},\hat{W}_{\mathbf{a}_{2}}^{n})
(\hat{W}_{\mathbf{a}_{1}}^{n},\dotsc,\hat{W}_{\mathbf{a}_{L}}^{n})

decoder

Fig. 2:
Sequential decoder for successively recovering multiple linear combinations.

Let
\mathbf{b}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}=[b_{j,1}\ldots b_{j,K}]
denote the
j
-th row of
{\mathbf{B}}
and let
{\mathbf{B}}_{[j]}=[\mathbf{b}_{1},\dotsc,\mathbf{b}_{j}]^{\mathsf{T}}=[{\mathbf{B}}]_{[j]}
denote the submatrix formed by the first
j
rows of
{\mathbf{B}}
.

We define
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathrm{seq}}({\mathbf{B}})
as the simplex set of non-negative rate tuples satisfying

R_{k}<\mathcal{H}(U_{k})+\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{B}}_{[j-1]}}(\bm{u}|Y)-\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{B}}_{[j]}}(\bm{u}|Y)

(56)

for all
j\in[L_{\mathsf{B}}]
and
k\in\{k^{\prime}\colon b_{j,k^{\prime}}\neq 0\}
. By convention,
\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{B}}_{[0]}}(\bm{u}|Y)=0
.

Definition 19
.

The
sequential decoding rate region
for recovering the
{\mathbf{A}}
-linear combinations of auxiliary codewords is defined as

\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{seq}}({\mathbf{A}})\triangleq\bigcup_{{\mathbf{B}}}\mathscr{Q}_{\mathrm{seq}}({\mathbf{B}})

(57)

where
{\mathbf{B}}\in\mathbb{A}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}\times K}
runs over all full row-rank matrices satisfying
\Lambda_{\mathbb{A}}({\mathbf{B}}^{\mathsf{T}})\supseteq\Lambda_{\mathbb{A}}({\mathbf{A}}^{\mathsf{T}})
.

Remark 8
.

Note that, unlike its counterpart
\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}})
for
simultaneous
decoding [cf. (
54
)], which is invariant against permutations of the rows of
{\mathbf{B}}
, the
sequential
decoding rate region
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathrm{seq}}({\mathbf{B}})
depends on the order of rows, which represents the decoding order. The union operation in (
57
), however, restores this invariance in the order of the rows of the coefficient matrix
{\mathbf{A}}
.

The following three theorems are counterparts to Theorems
1
,
2
and
3
, but for sequential decoding instead of simultaneous joint decoding.
Theorem
4
is proven in
[
44
, Thm. 2]
, whereas Theorems
5
and
6
are proven in Appendices
O
and
P
, respectively.

Theorem 4

(Finite-field compute–forward under sequential decoding)
.

Let
(\mathbb{U},\mathbb{A})=(\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}},\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}})
for some prime field size
\mathsf{q}
. A rate tuple
(R_{1},\dotsc,R_{K})
is achievable for sequentially decoding the
{\mathbf{A}}
-linear combinations of codewords if it is contained in
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{seq}}({\mathbf{A}})
for some auxiliary pmf
p_{\bm{u}}(\mathbf{u})=\prod_{k=1}^{K}p_{U_{k}}(u_{k})
and modulation mappings
(x_{1}(u_{1}),\dotsc,x_{K}(u_{K}))
.

Theorem 5

(Integer compute–forward under sequential decoding)
.

Let
(\mathbb{U},\mathbb{A})=(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z})
. A rate tuple
(R_{1},\dotsc,R_{K})
is achievable for sequentially decoding the
{\mathbf{A}}
-linear combinations of codewords if it is contained in
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{seq}}({\mathbf{A}})
for some auxiliary pmf
p_{\bm{u}}(\mathbf{u})=\prod_{k=1}^{K}p_{U_{k}}(u_{k})
and modulation mappings
(x_{1}(u_{1}),\dotsc,x_{K}(u_{K}))
.

Theorem 6

(Continuous compute–forward under sequential decoding)
.

Let
(\mathbb{U},\mathbb{A})=(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{Z})
. A rate tuple
(R_{1},\dotsc,R_{K})
is achievable for sequentially decoding the
{\mathbf{A}}
-linear combinations of codewords if it is contained in
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{seq}}({\mathbf{A}})
for some auxiliary pdf
f_{\bm{u}}(\mathbf{u})=\prod_{k=1}^{K}f_{U_{k}}(u_{k})
and modulation mappings
(x_{1}(u_{1}),\dotsc,x_{K}(u_{K}))
.

VI
Special cases

The three main theorems stated in the previous Section have established achievable rate regions which are based on relatively complex nested set operations, and it is therefore difficult to glean any insight into their behavior. To provide more intuition, let us elaborate on some special cases:

•

Subsection
VI-A
: The two-user case
K=L=2
, when the receiver decodes two linear combinations with
simultaneous
decoding;

•

Subsection
VI-B
: The two-user case
K=L=2
, when the receiver decodes two linear combinations with
sequential
decoding;

•

Subsection
VI-C
: The two-user case when the receiver decodes a single linear combination (
K=2
,
L=1
);

•

Subsection
VI-D
: Theorem 3 particularized to Gaussian auxiliaries, which recovers and improves upon most lattice-based compute–forward results known in the literature.

Note that the first three special cases apply equally to finite-field, integer and continuous auxiliary computation (Theorems
1
,
2
,
3
): the derivations are provided in a unified manner by using the properties of algebraic entropy.

VI-A

Decoding two linear combinations with two users under simultaneous decoding

For the case
K=L=2
, the matrix
{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathbb{A}^{2\times 2}
is square and full-rank.
This problem amounts to a two-user MAC setting in which the two users employ nested linear codes and attempt to recover two linearly independent combinations of messages (and hence both messages). As in previous publications
[
43
,
44
]
, we refer to this problem as the
linear MAC
(LMAC) and denote the corresponding achievable rate region as
\mathscr{R}({\mathbf{A}})=\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}
.
14
14

14

Note that
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}
does not depend on any specific matrix
{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathbb{A}^{2\times 2}
, as long as
{\mathbf{A}}
is full-rank.

The following lemma will be instrumental for simplifying the LMAC rate region expression.

Lemma 16
.

Consider full-rank square matrices
{\mathbf{B}}\in\mathbb{A}^{2\times 2}
. When taking the union of sets
\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}})
over such matrices, it suffices to only retain the identity matrix
{\mathbf{B}}={\mathbf{I}}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
, in that

\bigcup_{{\mathbf{B}}\in\mathbb{A}^{2\times 2}\colon\text{${\mathbf{B}}$ is full rank}}\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}})=\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{I}}).

(58)

Proof:

The proof consists in an exhaustive evaluation of the union on the left-hand side of (
58
), and is deferred to Appendix
Q
.
∎

By direct application of Lemma
16
to the rate region expression (
18
), we infer that for
K=L=2
,

\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}=\mathscr{R}({\mathbf{A}})=\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{I}})

(59)

(where
{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathbb{A}^{2\times 2}
is any full-rank matrix).
This rate region
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}
can be expressed as [cf. Appendix
Q
, Equations (
349
), (
354a
)–(
354c
)]

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}=\Bigl\{(R_{1},R_{2})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\colon R_{1}+R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(U_{1},U_{2};Y)

(60a)

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(U_{1};Y,U_{2})

(60b)

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(U_{2};Y,U_{1})

(60c)

\displaystyle\min\{R_{1}-\mathcal{H}(U_{1}),R_{2}-\mathcal{H}(U_{2})\}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\inf_{c_{1}\neq 0,\,c_{2}\neq 0}\mathcal{H}_{[c_{1}\ c_{2}]}(\bm{u}|Y)\Bigr\}.

(60d)

Notice that
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}
is obviously a subset (sometimes proper) of the conventional multiple-access rate region

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}=\Bigl\{(R_{1},R_{2})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\colon R_{1}+R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(U_{1},U_{2};Y)

(61a)

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(U_{1};Y,U_{2})

(61b)

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(U_{2};Y,U_{1})\Bigr\}.

(61c)

The set
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}
, defined by the inequalities (
60a
)–(
60d
), is depicted in Figure
3
(with a dashed line). Notice that when the inequality (
60d
) is inactive,
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}
coincides with
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}
, as illustrated in Figure
3a
. Otherwise,
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}
is diminished with respect to
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}
by a triangular shaped region located along the dominant face, as pictured in Figure
3b
.

R_{2}
R_{1}

(a)
Case
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}=\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}

\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}

R_{2}
R_{1}

(b)
Case
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}\subsetneq\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}

Fig. 3:
Rate region
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}=\mathscr{R}({\mathbf{A}})=\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{I}})
(dashed and shaded). The boundary of the MAC capacity rate region
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}
is shown for reference (solid gray).

Let us derive the exact condition (in terms of an entropy inequality) under which
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}\subsetneq\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}
. For this purpose, let us define the set [cf. (
60d
)]

\mathscr{R}^{\prime}\triangleq\Bigl\{(R_{1},R_{2})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\colon\\
\min\{R_{1}-\mathcal{H}(U_{1}),R_{2}-\mathcal{H}(U_{2})\}<-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\inf_{c_{1}\neq 0,\,c_{2}\neq 0}\mathcal{H}_{[c_{1}\ c_{2}]}(\bm{u}|Y)\Bigr\}

(62)

and denote its complement in
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
as
\overline{\mathscr{R}^{\prime}}
.
We can now express
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}
as

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}\cap\mathscr{R}^{\prime}

(63)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}\setminus\overline{\mathscr{R}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}\setminus(\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}\cap\overline{\mathscr{R}^{\prime}}).

The condition
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}\subsetneq\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}
is thus equivalent to
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}\cap\overline{\mathscr{R}^{\prime}}
being non-empty, which occurs if and only if there exists a rate pair
(R_{1},R_{2})
that will simultaneously satisfy the following three inequalities:

\displaystyle R_{1}+R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(U_{1},U_{2};Y)

(64a)

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{1})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\inf_{c_{1}\neq 0,\,c_{2}\neq 0}\mathcal{H}_{[c_{1}\ c_{2}]}(\bm{u}|Y)

(64b)

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{2})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\inf_{c_{1}\neq 0,\,c_{2}\neq 0}\mathcal{H}_{[c_{1}\ c_{2}]}(\bm{u}|Y).

(64c)

Upon subtracting (
64b
)–(
64c
) from (
64a
) and recalling that by the chain rule,
I(U_{1},U_{2};Y)=\mathcal{H}(U_{1})+\mathcal{H}(U_{2})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)
, we infer the following lemma.

Lemma 17
.

The linear MAC region
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}
is strictly contained in the MAC region
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}
if and only if there exists a coefficient vector with non-zero entries
\mathbf{c}^{\mathsf{T}}=[c_{1}\ c_{2}]\in\mathbb{A}^{2}
such that

2\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{c}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)<\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y).

(65)

Otherwise,
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}=\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}
.

VI-B

Decoding two linear combinations with two users under sequential decoding

Consider the same setting
K=L=2
as in the previous section, except that we replace
simultaneous decoding
with
sequential decoding
. Under these circumstances, the set
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathrm{seq}}({\mathbf{B}})
[cf. (
56
)] can be expressed as follows. Let
\mathbf{b}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}=[b_{j,1}\ b_{j,2}]
,
j=1,2
denote the
