b_{2,1}&b_{2,2}\end{bmatrix}
. The set
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathrm{seq}}({\mathbf{B}})
is defined as

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{1})-\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{b}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)

(if
b_{1,1}\neq 0
)

(66a)

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{2})-\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{b}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)

(if
b_{1,2}\neq 0
)

(66b)

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{1})+\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{b}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)

(if
b_{2,1}\neq 0
)

(66c)

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{2})+\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{b}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)

(if
b_{2,2}\neq 0
).

(66d)

The resulting sequential decoding rate region
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{seq}}({\mathbf{A}})=\bigcup_{{\mathbf{B}}}\mathscr{Q}_{\mathrm{seq}}({\mathbf{B}})
[cf. Definition

Fig. 4:
Two-user sequential-decoding achievable rate region
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{seq}}({\mathbf{A}})
(shaded area) for two different Gaussian channels. The set
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{seq}}({\mathbf{A}})
is expressible as the union of rectangular rate regions
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathrm{seq}}({\mathbf{B}})
(dotted lines) for different two-by-two full-rank matrices
{\mathbf{B}}
. The simultaneous-decoding rate region
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}=\mathscr{R}({\mathbf{A}})=\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{I}})
[cf. (
59
)] is shown for reference (dashed line).

To see why this is true, observe that the first row of any full-rank matrix
{\mathbf{B}}\in\mathbb{A}^{2\times 2}
satisfies one out of three possible cases: either
b_{1,1}
and
b_{1,2}
are both non-zero, or one of them is zero while the other is non-zero.

VI-B
1
Case
b_{1,1}\neq 0
and
b_{1,2}=0
, or
b_{1,1}=0
and
b_{1,2}\neq 0

We can assume without loss of generality that
\mathbf{b}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}=[1\ 0]
or
\mathbf{b}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}=[0\ 1]
, since
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathrm{seq}}({\mathbf{B}})
is invariant against scaling of any row of
{\mathbf{B}}
. Since
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathrm{seq}}({\mathbf{B}})
, as expressed in (
66a
)–(
66d
), depends on the second row of
{\mathbf{B}}
only via the conditions under which (
66c
) and (
66d
) need to hold, it is clear that setting either
b_{2,1}
or
b_{2,2}
to zero will remove one of two constraints, thus potentially enlarging the set
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathrm{seq}}({\mathbf{B}})
. Hence, without loss of generality, it suffices to consider the matrices
{\mathbf{B}}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
and
{\mathbf{B}}=\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}
.
For
{\mathbf{B}}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
, evaluating (
66a
)–(
66d
) reduces to

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{1})-\mathcal{H}(U_{1}|Y)

(67a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I(U_{1};Y)

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{2})+\mathcal{H}(U_{1}|Y)-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)

(67b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I(U_{2};Y,U_{1})

which corresponds to the upper corner point of the conventional MAC rate region. Likewise, for the flipped identity matrix
{\mathbf{B}}=\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}
, we obtain the lower corner point,

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(U_{1};Y,U_{2})

(68a)

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(U_{2};Y).

(68b)

VI-B
2
Case
b_{1,1}\neq 0
and
b_{1,2}\neq 0

By a similar argument as before,
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathrm{seq}}({\mathbf{B}})
is not decreased by setting either
b_{2,1}
or
b_{2,2}
to zero, hence we can consider
{\mathbf{B}}=\begin{bmatrix}b_{1,1}&b_{1,2}\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}
or
{\mathbf{B}}=\begin{bmatrix}b_{1,1}&b_{1,2}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
without loss of generality. We again distinguish two cases, depending on whether
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}\subsetneq\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}
or
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}=\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}
[cf. Lemma
17
]:

•

There exists a coefficient vector with non-zero entries
\mathbf{c}^{\mathsf{T}}=[c_{1}\ c_{2}]
such that (
65
) holds;

•

There exists no such coefficient vector.

Case
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}\subsetneq\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}

Consider the sets
\mathscr{Q}\bigl(\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}c_{1}&c_{2}\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]\bigr)
and
\mathscr{Q}\bigl(\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}c_{1}&c_{2}\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]\bigr)
, which are delimited respectively by inequalities (
66a
), (
66b
), (
66c
) for the former, and (
66a
), (
66b
), (
66d
) for the latter. Due to the inequality (
65
) holding, the right-hand sides of (
66a
) and (
66b
) are larger than the right-hand sides of (
66c
) and (
66d
), respectively. Hence, the set
\mathscr{Q}\bigl(\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}c_{1}&c_{2}\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]\bigr)
is delimited by the inequalities

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{1})+\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{c}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)

(69a)

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{2})-\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{c}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)

(69b)

whereas the set
\mathscr{Q}\bigl(\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}c_{1}&c_{2}\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]\bigr)
is delimited by the inequalities

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{1})-\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{c}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)

(70a)

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{2})+\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{c}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y).

(70b)

Clearly, by summing together both rate constraints (
69a
)–(
69b
) on the one hand, and (
70a
)–(
70b
) on the other hand, we see that these rate constraints yield two points located on the dominant face
R_{1}+R_{2}<I(\bm{u};Y)
of the multiple-access channel, as can be observed on Figure
4a
.

Case
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}=\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}

Consider the sets
\mathscr{Q}\bigl(\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}b_{1,1}&b_{1,2}\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]\bigr)
and
\mathscr{Q}\bigl(\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}b_{1,1}&b_{1,2}\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]\bigr)
with some
\mathbf{b}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}=[b_{1,1}\ b_{1,2}]
having non-zero entries, these sets being delimited respectively by inequalities (
66a
), (
66b
), (
66c
) for the former, and (
66a
), (
66b
), (
66d
) for the latter. Due to the inequality (
65
)
not
holding, the right-hand sides of (
66a
) and (
66b
) are smaller than or equal to the right-hand sides of (
66c
) and (
66d
), respectively. Hence, the sets
\mathscr{Q}\bigl(\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}b_{1,1}&b_{1,2}\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]\bigr)
and
\mathscr{Q}\bigl(\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}b_{1,1}&b_{1,2}\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]\bigr)
are delimited by the same two inequalities (
66a
)–(
66b
), namely,

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{1})-\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{b}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)

(if
b_{1,1}\neq 0
)

(71a)

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{2})-\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{b}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)

(if
b_{1,2}\neq 0
)
.

(71b)

This leads to a rate region like the one depicted on Figure
4b
.

VI-C

Decoding a single linear combination with two users

For the special case of
K=2
users and
L=1
linear combinations, the matrix
{\mathbf{A}}
reduces to a one-by-two row vector
\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}=[a_{1}\ a_{2}]
. Since there is only a single linear combination to recover, the rate regions under simultaneous decoding and sequential decoding coincide, i.e.,
\mathscr{R}(\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}})=\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{seq}}(\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}})
. Using Lemma
16
and the identity (
59
), one can immediately state the rate region
\mathscr{R}(\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}})
as a union of two sets:

\mathscr{R}(\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}})=\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{I}})\cup\mathscr{Q}(\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}})=\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}\cup\mathscr{Q}(\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}).

(72)

Here, the set
\mathscr{Q}(\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}})
can be expressed as [cf. (
366
)]

\displaystyle\mathscr{Q}(\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}})=\Bigl\{(R_{1},R_{2})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\colon R_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{1})-\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)

(73a)

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{2})-\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)\Bigr\}.

(73b)

Lemma 18
.

For
(K,L)=(2,1)
, the rate region
\mathscr{R}(\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}})
can be expressed as follows:

•

If
\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}
(with non-zero entries) satisfies

2\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)<\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)

(74)

then

\mathscr{R}(\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}})=\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}\cup\mathscr{Q}(\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}).

(75)

•

Otherwise,

\mathscr{R}(\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}})=\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}.

(76)

Proof:

The proof is deferred to Appendix
S
.
∎

The geometric interpretation of Lemma
18
is shown in Figure
5
, where we depict the shapes of the sets
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}
and
\mathscr{Q}(\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}})
for the two different cases determined by whether (
74
) holds or not.

(dashed) and

(dotted), as well as the MAC capacity region
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}
(solid). These sets can be assembled by union operations to obtain
\mathscr{R}(\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}})
(shaded area).

Lemma
18
states that if the coefficients
\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}
and the distribution of
(\bm{u},Y)
are such that the inequality (
74
) is satisfied, the set
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}
that appears in the (more generally valid) identity (
72
) can be swapped out for
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}
. On the contrary, if the reverse of (
74
) holds, then it is the union operation with
\mathscr{Q}(\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}})
, in this same identity (
72
), that becomes redundant and can thus be removed.

VI-D

Gaussian channels

Arguably the most important special case of Theorem
3
is its evaluation for Gaussian distributions. Consider a multiple access channel with
M
receiver antennas which obeys the system equation

\bm{y}={\mathbf{H}}\bm{x}+\bm{z}

(77)

where
\bm{x}=\bigl[X_{1},\dotsc,X_{K}\bigr]^{\mathsf{T}}
represents the vector or channel inputs,
{\mathbf{H}}\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times K}
stands for the channel gain matrix,
\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
is the vector of channel outputs and
\bm{z}\sim\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbf{0},{\mathbf{I}})
is i.i.d. additive Gaussian noise.
We assume that the channel inputs are subject to average power constraints
\E[X_{k}^{2}]\leq P_{k}
,
k=1,\dotsc,K
. We define
{\mathbf{P}}=\diag(P_{1},\dotsc,P_{K})
as the covariance matrix of
\bm{x}
.

The following corollary to Theorem
3
provides a generalization of the compute–forward rate region from
[
27
]
for the Gaussian channel (
77
), to the effect of simultaneously computing an
arbitrary
number of linearly independent combinations of Gaussian codewords, rather than only a single linear combination. This improves on the best-known rate region from prior work, which was based on nested lattice encoding and sequential decoding
[
39
]
.

Corollary 1

(Gaussian compute–forward)
.

Let
(\mathbb{U},\mathbb{A})=(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{Z})
. We evaluate Theorem
3
for the Gaussian channel (
77
), auxiliary variables
U_{k}\sim\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{R}}(0,\beta_{k}^{2}P_{k})
with scaling parameters
\beta_{k}>0
and modulation mappings
X_{k}=U_{k}/\beta_{k}
to satisfy the power constraints
\E[X_{k}^{2}]=P_{k}
.
Then,
\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T})
in Theorem
3
specializes to the set of rate tuples
(R_{1},\dotsc,R_{K})
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathcal{T}}R_{k}<\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{T}}\log(\beta_{k}^{2}P_{k})-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\det\bigl({\mathbf{B}}{\mathbf{K}}{\mathbf{B}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bigr)}{\det({\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{B}}))^{2}}+J({\mathbf{B}},M)

(78)

where
{\mathbf{K}}
stands for the conditional covariance matrix

{\mathbf{K}}=\E\bigl[\bm{u}\bm{u}^{\mathsf{T}}\big|Y\bigr]=\diag(\bm{\beta})\left({\mathbf{P}}^{-1}+{\mathbf{H}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\mathbf{H}}\right)^{-1}\diag(\bm{\beta})

(79)

and where

J({\mathbf{B}},M)=\inf_{{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{Z}}(M)}\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\det\bigl({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}{\mathbf{K}}{\mathbf{B}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bigr)}{\det({\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}))^{2}}.

(80)

Note that Corollary
1
is obtained directly from evaluating (
52
)–(
55
) by means of the Gaussian entropy formula and (
28
). As has been argued in Remark
7
, the matrix
{\mathbf{C}}
in the infimum (
80
) can be restricted to right-invertible. If, in addition,
{\mathbf{B}}
is also right-invertible, then both
{\mathbf{B}}
and
{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}
are right-invertible and thus have elementary divisors all equal to one, hence the denominators on the right-hand sides of (
78
) and (
80
) disappear, thus leading to simpler expressions. Note, however, that we have no proof nor disproof
15
15

15

Except for the two-user case
K=2
, for which we can show that the two right-invertible matrices
{\mathbf{B}}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
[cf. Subsection
VI-A
] and
{\mathbf{B}}=[b_{1}\ b_{2}]
(with coprime
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
) [cf. Subsection
VI-C
] suffice to attain the entire rate region
\mathscr{R}({\mathbf{A}})
.
that in the union over matrices
{\mathbf{B}}
in (
55
),
{\mathbf{B}}
can be restricted to being right-invertible without loss of optimality. This stands as an open problem.

Finally, let us particularize (
78
) to the case of two users (
K=2
) and a single linear combination (
L=1
), setting all parameters
\beta_{k}
to one and equal power constraints
P_{1}=P_{2}=P
, and evaluating the union (
55
) only for
{\mathbf{B}}=[a_{1}\ a_{2}]
. Denote the latter row vector as
\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}
and the channel matrix
{\mathbf{H}}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times 2}
as the row vector
\mathbf{h}^{\mathsf{T}}
.
With these choices, we can readily recover the well-known compute–forward rectangular rate region due to Nazer and Gastpar
[
27
]
:

\max\{R_{1},R_{2}\}<\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{P}{\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}\left(P^{-1}{\mathbf{I}}+\mathbf{h}\mathbf{h}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{a}}\right)+\log\gcd{(|a_{1}|,|a_{2}|)}.

(81)

We observe how in this special case, the term
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{B}})
from (
78
), which here reduces to a scalar, evaluates to the greatest common divisor of
|a_{2}|
and
|a_{2}|
.
Also note that for
L=1
, the term
J({\mathbf{B}},M)
disappears, which further contributes to simplification.

Corollary 2

(Gaussian compute–forward under sequential decoding)
.

Let
(\mathbb{U},\mathbb{A})=(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{Z})
. We evaluate Theorem
6
for the Gaussian channel (
77
), auxiliary variables
U_{k}\sim\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{R}}(0,\beta_{k}^{2}P_{k})
with scaling parameters
\beta_{k}>0
and modulation mappings
X_{k}=U_{k}/\beta_{k}
to satisfy the power constraints
\E[X_{k}^{2}]=P_{k}
.
Then,
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathrm{seq}}({\mathbf{B}})
in Theorem
6
specializes to the set of rate tuples
(R_{1},\dotsc,R_{K})
such that

R_{k}<\frac{1}{2}\log(\beta_{k}^{2}P_{k})-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\det\bigl({\mathbf{B}}_{[j]}{\mathbf{K}}{\mathbf{B}}_{[j]}^{\mathsf{T}}\bigr)}{\det({\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{B}}_{[j]}))^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\det\bigl({\mathbf{B}}_{[j-1]}{\mathbf{K}}{\mathbf{B}}_{[j-1]}^{\mathsf{T}}\bigr)}{\det({\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{B}}_{[j-1]}))^{2}}.

(82)

In the following, we provide some numerical evaluations for the Gaussian case in the context of Corollary
1
.

Example 1
.

Consider a
K=3
user Gaussian multiple-access channel with
M=1
receive antenna and channel gain matrix
{\mathbf{H}}=[1\ 1\ 1]
, symmetric transmit power
P=3
(for all users), and
{\mathbf{A}}=[1\ 1\ 1]
. The rate region from Corollary
1
is shown in Figure
6
. The sequential decoding points in Corollary
2
with
{\mathbf{B}}=[1\ 1\ 1]
and

{\mathbf{B}}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&1\end{bmatrix},

(83)

are labelled as
A
and
B
, respectively.

Fig. 6:
Three-user simultaneous decoding rate region from Example
1
.

.

Consider a
K=3
user Gaussian multiple-access channel with
M=3
receive antennas and channel gain matrix

\mathbf{H}=\begin{bmatrix}1&1.5&0.75\\
0.75&1&1.5\\
1.5&0.75&1\end{bmatrix},

(84)

symmetric transmit power
P=2
, and
{\mathbf{A}}=\left[1\ 1\ 1\right]
. Then, the rate region in Corollary
1
is evaluated in Figure
7
. The sequential decoding points in Corollary
2
with
{\mathbf{B}}=\left[1\ 1\ 1\right]
and

{\mathbf{B}}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0&0\\

(85)

are marked as
A
and

, respectively.

.

VII
Conclusion

In this paper, we proposed a discretization approach to translate compute–forward achievability results from discrete to continuous-valued channels. This enabled us to obtain a simultaneous-decoding, compute–forward rate region for a class of continuous channels, including those of the form
Y=\sum_{k}h_{k}X_{k}+Z
for some independent additive noise. As a special case, we obtained the simultaneous-decoding rate region for Gaussian channels, which had been an open question. Since these rate regions are expressed in terms of differences of entropies, special care was needed to overcome the fact that differential entropy is not scale-invariant. To this end, we introduced algebraic generalizations of Rényi’s information dimension and
d
-dimensional entropy, and used these to obtain a single, unified expression for both discrete and continuous compute–forward rate regions. Our discretization approach is well-suited for generalizing other algebraic network information theory results from discrete to continuous-valued channels, and many of our supporting lemmas may be directly employed to these problems.

For two users, it is now known that the simultaneous-decoding, compute–forward rate region for discrete memoryless channels is optimal, under the critical assumption that the transmitters employ random nested linear codes
[
57
]
. An open question is whether this result can be extended to
K
users for both the discrete and the Gaussian setting. Another interesting direction for future study is to develop an analogue of an information-density decoder (see, e.g.,
[
58
, Theorem 18.5]
) and use it to directly derive compute–forward rate regions for a broader class of channels.

Appendix A
Proof of Lemma
1

Given that
I(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2};Y)
is finite by assumption, the limit

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\Bigl\{H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}_{2}\rfloor|Y)-H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}_{2}\rfloor|Y,\lfloor\nu\bm{u}_{1}\rfloor)\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}I(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}_{2}\rfloor;Y,\lfloor\nu\bm{u}_{1}\rfloor)

(86)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I(\bm{u}_{2};Y,\bm{u}_{1})

is finite too, since it is upper-bounded by
I(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2};Y)
. The last equality is due to
[
59
, Lem. 7.18]
.
Likewise, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\Bigl\{H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}_{2}\rfloor|Y)-H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}_{2}\rfloor|Y,\bm{u}_{1})\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}I(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}_{2}\rfloor;Y,\bm{u}_{1})

(87)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I(\bm{u}_{2};Y,\bm{u}_{1}).

Combining the above two limits by subtraction, we get

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\Bigl\{H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}_{2}\rfloor|Y,\lfloor\nu\bm{u}_{1}\rfloor)-H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}_{2}\rfloor|Y,\bm{u}_{1})\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

(88)

If the information dimensions
d(\bm{u}_{1}|Y)
and
d(\bm{u}_{2}|Y,\bm{u}_{1})
exist, then the claimed chain rule for information dimension is an immediate consequence of the chain rule for discrete entropy and of (
88
):

\displaystyle d(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2}|Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\frac{H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}_{1}\rfloor|Y)+H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}_{2}\rfloor|Y,\lfloor\nu\bm{u}_{1}\rfloor)}{\log(\nu)}

(89)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\biggl\{\frac{H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}_{1}\rfloor|Y)}{\log(\nu)}+\frac{H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}_{2}\rfloor|Y,\bm{u}_{1})}{\log(\nu)}\biggr\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(\bm{u}_{1}|Y)+d(\bm{u}_{2}|Y,\bm{u}_{1}).

Similarly, for the entropy
\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2}|Y)
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2}|Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}_{1}\rfloor|Y)-d(\bm{u}_{1}|Y)\log(\nu)+H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}_{2}\rfloor|Y,\lfloor\nu\bm{u}_{1}\rfloor)-d(\bm{u}_{2}|Y,\bm{u}_{1})\log(\nu)

(90)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}_{1}\rfloor|Y)-d(\bm{u}_{1}|Y)\log(\nu)+H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}_{2}\rfloor|Y,\nu\bm{u}_{1})-d(\bm{u}_{2}|Y,\bm{u}_{1})\log(\nu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}_{1}|Y)+\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}_{2}|Y,\bm{u}_{1}).

This concludes the proof.

Appendix B
Proof of Lemma
2

First note that due to
[
59
, Lem. 7.18]

\lim_{\nu\to\infty}I(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor;Y)=I(\bm{u};Y)

(91)

being finite, we have

\displaystyle d(\bm{u}|Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\frac{H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y)}{\log(\nu)}

(92)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\frac{H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y)+I(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor;Y)-I(\bm{u};Y)}{\log(\nu)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\frac{H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)-I(\bm{u};Y)}{\log(\nu)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\frac{H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)}{\log(\nu)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(\bm{u}).

Hence,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(\bm{u})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\Bigl\{H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)-d(\bm{u})\log(\nu)-H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y)+d(\bm{u}|Y)\log(\nu)\Bigr\}

(93)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}I(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor;Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I(\bm{u};Y).

This concludes the proof.

Appendix C
Proof of Lemma
3

Since
H({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)\leq H(\bm{u})
, clearly
d_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u})=0
.
By the definition of algebraic entropy,
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u})=\liminf_{\nu\to\infty}H({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)
.
Defining
\bm{d}_{\nu}={\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor-\lfloor\nu{\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}\rfloor
we have

\displaystyle H({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H(\lfloor\nu{\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}\rfloor+\bm{d}_{\nu})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H\bigl({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}+\tfrac{1}{\nu}\bm{d}_{\nu}\bigr).

Denote the
(i,j)
-th entry of
{\mathbf{Q}}
as
Q_{i,j}
, and the
i
-th entry of
\bm{d}_{\nu}
as
[\bm{d}_{\nu}]_{i}
. Since
\bm{d}_{\nu}
can be written as

\bm{d}_{\nu}=\lfloor{\mathbf{Q}}(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor-\nu\bm{u})\rfloor

(94)

we can readily derive the bound
\bigl|[\bm{d}_{\nu}]_{i}\bigr|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}|Q_{i,j}|
. Hence, for
\nu>2\max_{i}\sum_{j=1}^{n}|Q_{i,j}|
, the support of
\tfrac{1}{\nu}\bm{d}_{\nu}
is entirely contained in the open cube
(-\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})^{m}
. In these circumstances, the sum
{\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}+\tfrac{1}{\nu}\bm{d}_{\nu}
can be one-to-one mapped to the pair
({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u},\bm{d}_{\nu})
. Therefore, if
\nu>2\max_{i}\sum_{j=1}^{n}|Q_{i,j}|
, then

\displaystyle H({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u},\bm{d}_{\nu})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle H({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}).

Hence
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u})\geq H({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u})
. On the other hand, if
\nu
is integer, we have
\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor=\nu\bm{u}
, since
\bm{u}
is integer. It follows that
H({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)=H({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u})
for integer
\nu
, so we conclude that
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{Q}}(\bm{u})=H({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u})
.

Appendix D
Proof of Lemma
4

Let us denote the reduced Smith normal decompositions of
{\mathbf{Q}}
and
{\mathbf{Q}}^{\sharp}
as
{\mathbf{Q}}={\mathbf{S}}({\mathbf{Q}}){\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{Q}}){\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})
and
{\mathbf{Q}}^{\sharp}=\hat{{\mathbf{S}}}({\mathbf{Q}})\hat{{\mathbf{\Sigma}}}({\mathbf{Q}})\hat{{\mathbf{T}}}({\mathbf{Q}})
(assuming
{\mathbf{Q}}^{\sharp}
exists), respectively.
By definition,
1)
is equivalent to there existing an integer
{\mathbf{Q}}^{\sharp}
such that
{\mathbf{Q}}{\mathbf{Q}}^{\sharp}={\mathbf{I}}
. The equivalence
1)

\Leftrightarrow

2)
is obvious by transposition. The implication
3)

\Rightarrow

1)
becomes clear when noticing that, if
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{Q}})
is the identity, then
{\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})^{\sharp}{\mathbf{S}}({\mathbf{Q}})^{-1}
is a right inverse of
{\mathbf{Q}}
. Conversely, given
1)
, i.e., given that
{\mathbf{Q}}^{\sharp}=\hat{{\mathbf{S}}}({\mathbf{Q}})\hat{{\mathbf{\Sigma}}}({\mathbf{Q}})\hat{{\mathbf{T}}}({\mathbf{Q}})
exists, we have that
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{Q}}){\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})\hat{{\mathbf{S}}}({\mathbf{Q}})\hat{{\mathbf{\Sigma}}}({\mathbf{Q}})={\mathbf{S}}({\mathbf{Q}})^{-1}\hat{{\mathbf{T}}}({\mathbf{Q}})^{-1}
is unimodular, hence
\det({\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{Q}}))=\bigl|\det({\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})\hat{{\mathbf{S}}}({\mathbf{Q}}))\bigr|=\det(\hat{{\mathbf{\Sigma}}}({\mathbf{Q}}))=1
, so
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{Q}})
and
\hat{{\mathbf{\Sigma}}}({\mathbf{Q}})
are diagonal non-negative and unimodular, and therefore
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{Q}})=\hat{{\mathbf{\Sigma}}}({\mathbf{Q}})={\mathbf{I}}
. Thus, the reverse implication
1)

\Rightarrow

3)
also holds. Now, assuming
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{Q}})={\mathbf{I}}
, consider the (non-reduced) Smith normal decomposition
{\mathbf{Q}}=\tilde{{\mathbf{S}}}({\mathbf{Q}})\tilde{{\mathbf{\Sigma}}}({\mathbf{Q}})\tilde{{\mathbf{T}}}({\mathbf{Q}})=\tilde{{\mathbf{S}}}({\mathbf{Q}})\bigl[{\mathbf{I}}\ \mynull\bigr]\tilde{{\mathbf{T}}}({\mathbf{Q}})
with unimodular
\tilde{{\mathbf{T}}}({\mathbf{Q}})=\bigl[{\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})^{\mathsf{T}}\ {\mathbf{R}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bigr]^{\mathsf{T}}
, which gives us
{\mathbf{Q}}=\tilde{{\mathbf{S}}}({\mathbf{Q}}){\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{Q}})
. The matrix
{\mathbf{R}}
completes
{\mathbf{Q}}
to a unimodular matrix, because
\bigl[{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathsf{T}}\ {\mathbf{R}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bigr]=\Bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}\tilde{{\mathbf{S}}}({\mathbf{Q}})&\mynull\\
\mynull&{\mathbf{I}}\end{smallmatrix}\Bigr]\tilde{{\mathbf{T}}}({\mathbf{Q}})
, which is a product of unimodular matrices, and therefore itself unimodular. Hence
3)

\Rightarrow

4)
. The implication
4)

\Rightarrow

1)
is obvious, since the first
n
columns of
\bigl(\bigl[{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathsf{T}}\ {\mathbf{R}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bigr]^{\mathsf{T}}\bigr)^{-1}
are clearly a right inverse of
{\mathbf{Q}}
. Finally, from (
26
) it is clear that the greatest common divisor of the
n\times n
minors, i.e.,
d_{n}({\mathbf{Q}})
, is equal to the product
\prod_{i}\sigma_{i}({\mathbf{Q}})=\det({\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{Q}}))
, which equals one if and only if
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{Q}})={\mathbf{I}}
, that is, if
{\mathbf{Q}}
is right-invertible. This settles
5)
.

Appendix E
Proof of Lemma
6

Since
\mathcal{Q}
is countable, we can enumerate its atoms. Let
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}
denote a sequence of all atoms of
\Sigma
having positive Lebesgue measure
\lambda(\mathcal{A}_{i})>0
. Let
f_{\bm{u}}
denote the pdf of
\bm{u}
. Then, by the concavity of
\Phi(x)=-x\log(x)
and Jensen’s inequality,

\displaystyle h(\bm{u})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}\Phi(f_{\bm{u}}(\mathbf{u})){\,\operatorname{d}}\mathbf{u}

(95)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\lambda(\mathcal{A}_{i})\Phi\left(\int_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}\frac{f_{\bm{u}}(\mathbf{u})}{\lambda(\mathcal{A}_{i})}{\,\operatorname{d}}\mathbf{u}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\lambda(\mathcal{A}_{i})\Phi\left(\frac{\P\{\bm{u}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\}}{\lambda(\mathcal{A}_{i})}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H(\lceil\bm{u}\rfloor_{\mathcal{Q}})+\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\P\{\bm{u}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\}\log(\lambda(\mathcal{A}_{i}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle H(\lceil\bm{u}\rfloor_{\mathcal{Q}})+\log(\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}).

This concludes the proof.

Appendix F
Proof of Lemma
7

Let
\nu
and
m
be some positive integers and let
\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and
\bm{k}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
denote integer vectors. Define

p_{\bm{k},\bm{k}^{\prime},m}(\nu\bm{u})\triangleq\P\bigl\{\nu\bm{u}\in\bm{k}+\tfrac{\bm{k}^{\prime}}{m}+\bigl[0,\tfrac{1}{m}\bigr)^{n}\bigr\}.

(96)

Note that

\sum_{\bm{k}^{\prime}\in[0:m-1]^{n}}p_{\bm{k},\bm{k}^{\prime},m}(\nu\bm{u})=\P\bigl\{\nu\bm{u}\in\bm{k}+[0,1)^{n}\bigr\}

(97)

Due to the concavity of
\Phi(x)=-x\log(x)
and Jensen’s inequality,
16
16

16

This is also known as the log-sum inequality.

\displaystyle S_{m\nu}+n\log(m\nu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H(\lfloor m\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\sum_{\bm{k}^{\prime}\in[0:m-1]^{n}}\Phi(p_{\bm{k},\bm{k}^{\prime},m}(\nu\bm{u}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle m^{n}\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\Phi\bigl(m^{-n}\P\bigl\{\nu\bm{u}\in\bm{k}+[0,1)^{n}\bigr\}\bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle m^{n}\Phi(m^{-n})+\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\Phi\bigl(\P\bigl\{\nu\bm{u}\in\bm{k}+[0,1)^{n}\bigr\}\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)+n\log(m)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S_{\nu}+n\log(m\nu)

hence
S_{m\nu}\leq S_{\nu}
. Here, the equality
(a)
is due to
\Phi(xy)=x\Phi(y)+y\Phi(x)
. It immediately follows that
S_{\nu}\leq S_{1}=H(\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor)
, which settles the right-hand inequality of (
30
).
As to the lower bound in (
30
), it follows from particularizing Lemma
6
to the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the cubes
\nu^{-1}(\bm{i}+[0,1)^{n}),\bm{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. It remains to prove the limit (
31
), for which we refer to
[
48
, Thm. 1]
.

Appendix G
Proof of Lemma
8

Let us denote the
(i,j)
-th entry of
{\mathbf{T}}
as
T_{i,j}
, and define the random vector

\displaystyle\bm{d}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\lfloor{\mathbf{T}}\bm{v}\rfloor-{\mathbf{T}}\lfloor\bm{v}\rfloor

(98)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lfloor{\mathbf{T}}\bm{v}-{\mathbf{T}}\lfloor\bm{v}\rfloor+{\mathbf{T}}\lfloor\bm{v}\rfloor\rfloor-{\mathbf{T}}\lfloor\bm{v}\rfloor

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lfloor{\mathbf{T}}\left(\bm{v}-\lfloor\bm{v}\rfloor\right)\rfloor.

Notice that each entry of
\bm{v}-\lfloor\bm{v}\rfloor
takes value in
[0;1)
. Hence the
i
-th entry of
\bm{d}
takes value in a finite set

\mathcal{D}_{i}=\mathbb{Z}\cap\begin{cases}\left(\textstyle\sum_{j}^{-}T_{i,j};\textstyle\sum_{j}^{+}T_{i,j}\right)&\text{if $\textstyle\sum_{j}^{-}T_{i,j}\neq 0$ and $\textstyle\sum_{j}^{+}T_{i,j}\neq 0$}\\
\left[\textstyle\sum_{j}^{-}T_{i,j};\textstyle\sum_{j}^{+}T_{i,j}\right)&\text{if $\textstyle\sum_{j}^{-}T_{i,j}=0$ and $\textstyle\sum_{j}^{+}T_{i,j}\neq 0$}\\
\left(\textstyle\sum_{j}^{-}T_{i,j};\textstyle\sum_{j}^{+}T_{i,j}\right]&\text{if $\textstyle\sum_{j}^{-}T_{i,j}\neq 0$ and $\textstyle\sum_{j}^{+}T_{i,j}=0$.}\end{cases}

(99)

Here, we have denoted

\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{j}^{-}T_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j\colon T_{i,j}\leq 0}T_{i,j}

\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{j}^{+}T_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j\colon T_{i,j}\geq 0}T_{i,j}

(100)

for notational brevity. Note that in all three cases considered in (
99
), the cardinality of
\mathcal{D}_{i}
is upper-bounded by
\sum_{j}|T_{i,j}|
.
Hence, we have the uniform upper bound

\displaystyle\bigl|H({\mathbf{T}}\lfloor\bm{v}\rfloor)-H(\lfloor{\mathbf{T}}\bm{v}\rfloor)\bigr|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigl|I({\mathbf{T}}\lfloor\bm{v}\rfloor;\bm{d})-I(\lfloor{\mathbf{T}}\bm{v}\rfloor;\bm{d})\bigr|

(101)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle H(\bm{d})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\log\left|\mathcal{D}_{i}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\log\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}|T_{i,j}|\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle m\log\left(\frac{\lVert{\mathbf{T}}\rVert_{1}}{m}\right)

where the last inequality follows from the arithmetic–geometric mean inequality.

Appendix H
Proof of Lemma
9

Proving the finiteness of
H(\lfloor{\mathbf{Q}}\bm{v}\rfloor)
is straightforward, since by Lemma
8
, we have

\displaystyle 0\leq H(\lfloor{\mathbf{Q}}\bm{v}\rfloor)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle H({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{v}\rfloor)+m\log\left(\frac{\lVert{\mathbf{Q}}\rVert_{1}}{m}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle H(\lfloor\bm{v}\rfloor)+m\log\left(\frac{\lVert{\mathbf{Q}}\rVert_{1}}{m}\right).

By Lemma
7
, we immediately conclude that
h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{v})<+\infty
due to the upper bound
h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{v})\leq H(\lfloor{\mathbf{Q}}\bm{v}\rfloor)
.

To determine a lower bound on
h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{v})
, take
\tilde{{\mathbf{Q}}}={\mathbf{R}}{\mathbf{Q}}
to be in reduced row echelon form, where
{\mathbf{R}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
is full-rank. Let
V_{j}
denote the
j
-th entry of
\bm{v}
and let
\tilde{Q}_{i,\pi(i)}
denote the pivot element
17
17

17

In a matrix in row echelon form, the pivot element of the
i
-th row is the first non-zero entry of the
i
-th row, read from left to right.
in the
i
-th row of
\tilde{{\mathbf{Q}}}
. Using the chain rule for differential entropy, we can obtain a lower bound on
h(\tilde{{\mathbf{Q}}}\bm{v})
as follows:

\displaystyle h(\tilde{{\mathbf{Q}}}\bm{v})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h\bigl([\tilde{{\mathbf{Q}}}]_{\{m\}}\bm{v}\bigr)+h\bigl([\tilde{{\mathbf{Q}}}]_{\{m-1\}}\bm{v}\big|[\tilde{{\mathbf{Q}}}]_{\{m\}}\bm{v}\bigr)+\dotsc+h\bigl([\tilde{{\mathbf{Q}}}]_{\{1\}}\bm{v}\big|[\tilde{{\mathbf{Q}}}]_{[2:m]}\bm{v}\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}h\bigl([\tilde{{\mathbf{Q}}}]_{\{m-i+1\}}\bm{v}\big|[\tilde{{\mathbf{Q}}}]_{[m-i+2:m]}\bm{v}\bigr)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}h\bigl([\tilde{{\mathbf{Q}}}]_{\{m-i+1\}}\bm{v}\big|[\tilde{{\mathbf{Q}}}]_{[m-i+2:m]}\bm{v},[\bm{v}]_{[\pi(m-i+1)+1{:}n]}\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}h\bigl([\tilde{{\mathbf{Q}}}]_{\{m-i+1\}}\bm{v}\big|[\bm{v}]_{[\pi(m-i+1)+1{:}n]}\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}h\bigl(V_{\pi(m-i+1)}\big|[\bm{v}]_{[\pi(m-i+1)+1{:}n]}\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}h\bigl(V_{\pi(i)}\big|[\bm{v}]_{[\pi(i)+1{:}n]}\bigr).

This lower bound is finite because

\displaystyle h\bigl(V_{\pi(i)}\big|[\bm{v}]_{[\pi(i)+1{:}n]}\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h\bigl(V_{\pi(i)}\big|V_{\pi(i)+1},\dotsc,V_{n}\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h\bigl(V_{\pi(i)},\dotsc,V_{n}\bigr)-h\bigl(V_{\pi(i)+1},\dotsc,V_{n}\bigr)

and these differential entropies are finite by assumption. Therefore,
h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{v})
has a finite lower bound

h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{v})\geq-\left|\log\det({\mathbf{R}})\right|+\sum_{i=1}^{m}h\bigl(V_{\pi(i)}\big|V_{\pi(i)+1},\dotsc,V_{n}\bigr).

(102)

This concludes the proof.

Appendix I
Proof of Lemma
10

Lemma
10
is a multivariate generalization of a result by Makkuva and Wu
[
51
, Lemma 1]
. Our proof borrows their line of reasoning. First off, notice that

H({\mathbf{T}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)-H(\lfloor\nu{\mathbf{T}}\bm{u}\rfloor)=I(\lfloor\nu{\mathbf{T}}\bm{u}\rfloor;\bm{d}_{\nu})-I({\mathbf{T}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor;\bm{d}_{\nu})

(103)

where

\bm{d}_{\nu}=\lfloor\nu{\mathbf{T}}\bm{u}\rfloor-{\mathbf{T}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor.

(104)

It thus suffices to prove that both mutual information terms on the right-hand side of (
103
) vanish as
\nu\to\infty
. Since

\displaystyle\bm{d}_{\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big\lfloor{\mathbf{T}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor+\nu{\mathbf{T}}\bm{u}-{\mathbf{T}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\big\rfloor-{\mathbf{T}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor

(105)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big\lfloor{\mathbf{T}}\bigl(\nu\bm{u}-\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\bigr)\big\rfloor

the second mutual information term on the right-hand side of (
103
) can be upper-bounded using the data-processing inequality as

\displaystyle I({\mathbf{T}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor;\bm{d}_{\nu})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I\bigl({\mathbf{T}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor;\big\lfloor{\mathbf{T}}\bigl(\nu\bm{u}-\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\bigr)\big\rfloor\bigr)

(106)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle I(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor;\nu\bm{u}-\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)

the right-hand side of which tends to zero as
\nu\to\infty
by the following lemma.

Lemma 19

(Asymptotic independence of quantization output and error)
.

For any random vector
\bm{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}I\bigl(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor;\nu\bm{u}-\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\bigr)=0.

Proof:

By
[
60
, Lemma 7.18]
, we have

\displaystyle I(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor;\nu\bm{u}-\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)=\lim_{m\to\infty}I\bigl(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor;\big\lfloor m(\nu\bm{u}-\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)\big\rfloor\bigr).

The mutual information
I\bigl(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor;\big\lfloor m(\nu\bm{u}-\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)\big\rfloor\bigr)
can be upper-bounded as follows:

I
(
⌊
ν
u
⌋
;
⌊
m(
ν
u
-
⌊
ν
u
⌋
)
⌋
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)+H\bigl(\big\lfloor m\nu\bm{u}-m\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\big\rfloor\bigr)-H\bigl(\big\lfloor m\nu\bm{u}-m\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\big\rfloor,\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)+H\bigl(\big\lfloor m\nu\bm{u}-m\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\big\rfloor\bigr)-H\bigl(\lfloor m\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)+n\log(m+1)-H\bigl(\lfloor m\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\bigr)

where step
(a)
follows because there is a one-to-one correspondence between
\bigl(\big\lfloor m\nu\bm{u}-m\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\big\rfloor,\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\bigr)
and
\lfloor m\nu\bm{u}\rfloor
and step
(b)
follows because each of the
n
coordinates of
\big\lfloor m\nu\bm{u}-m\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\big\rfloor
takes at most
m+1
values. Taking the limit as
\nu\to\infty
, we thus get an upper bound

\displaystyle I(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor;\nu\bm{u}-\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)+\lim_{m\to\infty}\bigl\{n\log(m+1)-H\bigl(\lfloor m\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\bigr)\bigr\}

(107)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)-n\log(\nu)-h(\bm{u}).

Finally, taking the limit as
\nu\to\infty
we obtain the desired result

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}I\bigl(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor;\nu\bm{u}-\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

This concludes the proof.

∎

Prior to studying the first mutual information term in (
103
), we introduce some auxiliary definitions and state Lemmata
20
,
21
and
22
, which will be needed in the subsequent proof.

Definition 20
.

Let
P_{X}
and
P_{Y}
denote two probability measures defined on a common probability space
\Omega
. The total variation distance between
P_{X}
and
P_{Y}
is defined as

d_{\mathsf{TV}}(P_{X}\|P_{Y})\triangleq\sup_{\mathcal{A}\subset\Omega}|P_{X}(\mathcal{A})-P_{Y}(\mathcal{A})|.

(108)

Following the terminology of
[
61
]
, we define the
T
-information between random random variables
X
and
Y
, with joint distribution
P_{XY}
, as

T(X;Y)\triangleq d_{\mathsf{TV}}(P_{XY}\|P_{X}\times P_{Y}).

(109)

Note that
T
-information is upper-bounded by one.

Lemma 20
.

For any probability measure
P_{XY}
on a probability space
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
, we have

\displaystyle I(X;Y)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\log(\min(|\mathcal{X}|,|\mathcal{Y}|)-1)T(X;Y)+h_{\mathsf{b}}(T(X;Y))

(110a)

\displaystyle I(X;Y)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 2T(X;Y)^{2}

(110b)

where
h_{\mathsf{b}}(x)=-x\log(x)-(1-x)\log(1-x)
denotes the binary entropy function, and where (
110a
) only holds if either
\mathcal{X}
or
\mathcal{Y}
is of finite cardinality.

Proof:

Proofs of (
110a
) and (
110b
) can be found in
[
61
, Proposition 12]
(via Fano’s inequality) and
[
62
]
, respectively. The inequality (
110b
) is commonly known as the
Pinsker-Csiszár
or
Pinsker
inequality.
∎

Lemma 21
.

Let the triple
(X,Y,Z)
of random variables satisfy
Z=f(X)=f(Y)
where
f
denotes some measurable function. Then for any measurable set
\mathcal{B}
such that
\P\{Z\in\mathcal{B}\}>0
,

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(P_{X|Z\in\mathcal{B}}\middle\|P_{Y|Z\in\mathcal{B}}\right)\leq\frac{d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(P_{X}\middle\|P_{Y}\right)}{\P\{Z\in\mathcal{B}\}}.

(111)

Proof:

For any measurable
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{A}
,

—

P

{X
∈
A
—Z
∈
B
} -

P

{Y
∈
A
—Z
∈
B
}
—

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\bigl|\P\{X\in\mathcal{A},f(X)\in\mathcal{B}\}-\P\{Y\in\mathcal{A},f(Y)\in\mathcal{B}\}\bigr|}{\P\{Z\in\mathcal{B}\}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\sup_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}\bigl|\P\{X\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime},f(X)\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\}-\P\{Y\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime},f(Y)\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\}\bigr|}{\P\{Z\in\mathcal{B}\}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d_{\mathsf{TV}}(P_{X}\|P_{Y})}{\P\{Z\in\mathcal{B}\}}.

∎

Lemma 22

(Continuity of total variation distance)
.

Let
\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
be a continuous random vector with pdf
f_{\bm{u}}
. Then it holds that

\lim_{\lVert\bm{\Delta}\rVert_{2}\to 0}d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(f_{\bm{u}}\middle\|f_{\bm{u}+\bm{\Delta}}\right)=0.

(112)

Proof:

Let
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{K})
denote the space of
L^{1}
-integrable functions
\mathbb{R}^{K}\to\mathbb{R}
, endowed with the
L^{1}
-norm
\lVert f\rVert_{1}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K}}|f(x)|{\,\operatorname{d}}x
. Since the set of compactly supported continuous functions is dense in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{K})
, for any
\epsilon>0
there exists a continuous and compactly supported function
q\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{K})
such that
\lVert f_{\bm{u}}-q\rVert_{1}<\epsilon
. Due to the Heine–Cantor Theorem
[
63
, Theorem 4.19]
, continuous and compactly supported functions are uniformly continuous. Therefore, there exists
\delta(\epsilon)>0
such that for all vectors
\bm{\Delta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
of norm
\lVert\bm{\Delta}\rVert_{2}<\delta(\epsilon)
, we have
\lVert q(\cdot+\bm{\Delta})-q(\cdot)\rVert_{1}<\epsilon
. By the triangle inequality,
\lVert f_{\bm{u}}(\cdot+\bm{\Delta})-f_{\bm{u}}(\cdot)\rVert_{1}\leq 2\lVert f_{\bm{u}}(\cdot)-q(\cdot)\rVert_{1}+\lVert q(\cdot+\bm{\Delta})-q(\cdot)\rVert_{1}<3\epsilon
. Hence the claim follows.
∎

With the above lemmata, we can proceed with the proof of Lemma

.
The random vector

). This upper bound tends to zero as
\nu\to\infty
because on one hand, by definition of the finite set
\mathcal{D}
, the limit
\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\P\{\bm{d}_{\nu}=\tilde{\mathbf{d}}\}
is positive for all
\tilde{\mathbf{d}}\in\mathcal{D}
by definition of
\mathcal{D}
, while on the other hand, the mutual information
I\bigl({\mathbf{T}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor;\bm{d}_{\nu}\bigr)
vanishes, as already argued in (
106
).
Since, by assumption,
{\mathbf{T}}
is right-invertible in the integers, let us denote a right-inverse as
{\mathbf{T}}^{\sharp}\in\mathbb{Z}^{K\times L_{\mathsf{Q}}}
, so that we can write

{\mathbf{T}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor+\mathbf{d}^{\prime}-\mathbf{d}={\mathbf{T}}\bigl(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor+{\mathbf{T}}^{\sharp}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime}-\mathbf{d})\bigr).

(118)

The first total variation term on the right-hand side of (
117
) can thus be upper-bounded as follows:

d_
TV
(
P_
T
⌊
ν
u
⌋+
d
’—
d
_
ν
=
d
’
∥
P_
T
⌊
ν
u
⌋+
d
—
d
_
ν
=
d
’
)

(119)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{TV}}\bigl(P_{{\mathbf{T}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor+(\mathbf{d}^{\prime}-\mathbf{d})|\bm{d}_{\nu}=\mathbf{d}^{\prime}}\big\|P_{{\mathbf{T}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|\bm{d}_{\nu}=\mathbf{d}^{\prime}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{TV}}\bigl(P_{{\mathbf{T}}(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor+{\mathbf{T}}^{\sharp}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime}-\mathbf{d}))|\bm{d}_{\nu}=\mathbf{d}^{\prime}}\big\|P_{{\mathbf{T}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|\bm{d}_{\nu}=\mathbf{d}^{\prime}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{TV}}\bigl(P_{\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor+{\mathbf{T}}^{\sharp}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime}-\mathbf{d})|\bm{d}_{\nu}=\mathbf{d}^{\prime}}\big\|P_{\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|\bm{d}_{\nu}=\mathbf{d}^{\prime}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\P\{\bm{d}_{\nu}=\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\}}d_{\mathsf{TV}}\bigl(P_{\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor+{\mathbf{T}}^{\sharp}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime}-\mathbf{d})}\big\|P_{\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor}\bigr).

Here, the bounding step
(a)
follows from the data-processing inequality for
T
-information, whereas step
(b)
follows from Lemma
21
, which is applicable because
\bm{d}_{\nu}

results from either [cf. (
105
)]
\bm{u}+\tfrac{1}{\nu}{\mathbf{T}}^{\sharp}(\bm{d}^{\prime}-\bm{d})
or
\bm{u}
, by an application of the function

f\colon\mathbb{R}^{K}\to\mathbb{R}^{L_{\mathsf{Q}}},\ \bm{x}\mapsto\lfloor{\mathbf{T}}(\nu\bm{x}-\lfloor\nu\bm{x}\rfloor)\rfloor.

(120)

Indeed, one can readily verify that

\displaystyle f\bigl(\bm{u}+\tfrac{1}{\nu}{\mathbf{T}}^{\sharp}(\bm{d}^{\prime}-\bm{d})\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big\lfloor{\mathbf{T}}\bigl(\nu\bm{u}+{\mathbf{T}}^{\sharp}(\bm{d}^{\prime}-\bm{d})-\big\lfloor\nu\bm{u}+{\mathbf{T}}^{\sharp}(\bm{d}^{\prime}-\bm{d})\big\rfloor\bigr)\big\rfloor

(121)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big\lfloor{\mathbf{T}}(\nu\bm{u}-\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)\big\rfloor

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(\bm{u})=\bm{d}_{\nu}.

Taking the limit as
\nu\to\infty
on both sides of (
119
), and recalling that by definition of
\mathcal{D}
, we have that
\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\P\{\bm{d}_{\nu}=\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\}>0
for any
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}
, by Lemma
22
we infer that (
119
) tends to zero as
\nu\to\infty
. This finalizes the proof of Lemma
10
.

Appendix J
Proof of Theorem
1

We start by restating the compute–forward achievable region from
[
44
, Thm. 1]
and then prove step by step that it is equal to the rate region
\mathscr{R}({\mathbf{A}})
from Theorem
1
.

Let
(\mathbb{U},\mathbb{A})=(\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}},\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}})
. For matrices
{\mathbf{B}}\in\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}\times K}
and
{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}\times L_{\mathsf{B}}}
and an index set
\mathcal{T}\subset[K]
, let us define

\widetilde{\mathscr{Q}}({\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}},\mathcal{T})\triangleq\Bigl\{(R_{1},\dotsc,R_{K})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{K}\colon\sum_{k\in\mathcal{T}}R_{k}<H([\bm{u}]_{\mathcal{T}})-H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y)+H({\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y)\Bigr\}

(122)

as well as

\widetilde{\mathscr{Q}}({\mathbf{B}})\triangleq\bigcap_{{\mathbf{C}}}\bigcup_{\mathcal{S}}\bigcap_{\mathcal{T}}\widetilde{\mathscr{Q}}({\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}},\mathcal{T})

(123)

where the three nested set operations are over triples
({\mathbf{C}},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{T})
meeting the following constraints:

1.

{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}\times L_{\mathsf{B}}}
is iterated over all full-rank matrices (including empty matrices)
19
19

19

As in
[
44
]
, here we invoke the notion of an
empty matrix,
which is a matrix with zero rows (resp. zero columns). By convention, we will consider that an empty matrix has
full row rank
(resp.
full column rank
), its rank being
0
. The product of an empty matrix with another matrix is an empty matrix, e.g., if
{\mathbf{A}}
is a
0\times 3
empty matrix and
{\mathbf{B}}
is a
3\times 5
matrix, then
{\mathbf{A}}{\mathbf{B}}
is an empty matrix of size
0\times 5
.
such that
0\leq L_{\mathsf{C}}<L_{\mathsf{B}}
,

2.

\mathcal{S}\subseteq[L_{\mathsf{B}}]
is iterated over all index sets of size
|\mathcal{S}|=L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}}
satisfying

\rank\left(\begin{bmatrix}{\mathbf{C}}\\
[{\mathbf{I}}]_{\mathcal{S}}\end{bmatrix}\right)=L_{\mathsf{B}}.

(124)

3.

\mathcal{T}\subseteq[K]
is iterated over all index sets of size
|\mathcal{T}|=L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}}
satisfying

\rank\left(\begin{bmatrix}[{\mathbf{B}}]_{\mathcal{S}}\\
[{\mathbf{I}}]_{[K]\setminus\mathcal{T}}\end{bmatrix}\right)=K.

(125)

Finally, let us define the joint rate region

\widetilde{\mathscr{R}}({\mathbf{A}})\triangleq\bigcup_{{\mathbf{B}}}\widetilde{\mathscr{Q}}({\mathbf{B}})

(126)

where
{\mathbf{B}}\in\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}\times K}
is iterated over all full-rank matrices satisfying
\Lambda_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}({\mathbf{B}})\supseteq\Lambda_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}({\mathbf{A}})
.

Notice that
\widetilde{\mathscr{R}}({\mathbf{A}})
is the achievable rate–region from
[
44
, Thm. 1]
. To see this, bear in mind that in (
122
) one can rewrite the difference of the last two entropy terms as a single one to match the expression from
[
44
, Eq. (5)]
:

H({\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y)-H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y)=H({\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y,{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}).

(127)

We will now transform the expression of
\widetilde{\mathscr{R}}({\mathbf{A}})
in order to prove its equality with
\mathscr{R}({\mathbf{A}})
from Theorem
1
. In particular, we will introduce the concept of matroids, as the set over which
{\mathbf{C}}
,
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{T}
are iterated is more naturally represented in terms of matroids.

Note that we can express the full-rank conditions (
124
) and (
125
), which respectively define the sets over which
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{T}
are iterated, in terms of matroids. Specifically,

i)

condition (
124
) holds if and only if
\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{B}(M^{*}({\mathbf{C}}))
;

ii)

condition (
125
) holds if and only if
\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{B}(M([{\mathbf{B}}]_{\mathcal{S}}))
.

The set
\widetilde{\mathscr{Q}}({\mathbf{B}})
as defined in (
123
)–(
125
) can thus be expressed more concisely as

\widetilde{\mathscr{Q}}({\mathbf{B}})=\bigcap_{{\mathbf{C}}}\;\bigcup_{\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{B}(M^{*}({\mathbf{C}}))}\;\bigcap_{\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{B}([{\mathbf{B}}]_{\mathcal{S}})}\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}},\mathcal{T})

(128)

where for the first intersection,
{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}\times L_{\mathsf{B}}}
is iterated over all full-rank matrices such that
0\leq L_{\mathsf{C}}<L_{\mathsf{B}}
(including empty matrices).
Recall that the set of all full row-rank matrices
{\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}^{r\times L_{\mathsf{B}}}
that represent a given matroid
M\in\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(L_{\mathsf{B}})
of some rank
0\leq r\leq L_{\mathsf{B}}
is denoted as
\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(M)
[cf. Section
IV-D
, Definition
15
].
In the outmost intersection of (
123
), as
{\mathbf{C}}
is iterated over all matrices from
\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}\times L_{\mathsf{B}}}
with
0\leq L_{\mathsf{C}}<L_{\mathsf{B}}
, the corresponding matroids
M({\mathbf{C}})
will be iterated over all size-
L_{\mathsf{B}}
matroids representable over
\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}
except the full-rank matroid
\{[L_{\mathsf{B}}],2^{[L_{\mathsf{B}}]}\}
, i.e., over the set

\{M({\mathbf{C}})\colon{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}\times L_{\mathsf{B}}},0\leq L_{\mathsf{C}}<L_{\mathsf{B}},\text{${\mathbf{C}}$ has full column rank}\}=\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(L_{\mathsf{B}})\setminus\{[L_{\mathsf{B}}],2^{[L_{\mathsf{B}}]}\}.

(129)

Let us denote the latter set as
\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}^{\circ}(L_{\mathsf{B}})
.
We can now rearrange the intersection operations over matrices
{\mathbf{C}}
in (
123
) so as to group them according to their associated matroids, as follows:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{Q}}({\mathbf{B}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigcap_{M\in\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}^{\circ}(L_{\mathsf{B}})}\;\bigcap_{{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(M)}\;\bigcup_{\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{B}(M^{*})}\;\bigcap_{\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{B}([{\mathbf{B}}]_{\mathcal{S}})}\widetilde{\mathscr{Q}}({\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}},\mathcal{T}).

(130)

Now, from the definition of
\widetilde{\mathscr{Q}}({\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}},\mathcal{T})
[cf. (
122
)] it is manifest that for any two matrices
{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
that represent the same matroid
M
, that is,
{\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(M)
, an inequality
H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y)\leq H({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y)
will imply an inclusion relation
\widetilde{\mathscr{Q}}({\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}},\mathcal{T})\subset\widetilde{\mathscr{Q}}({\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime},\mathcal{T})
, from which it follows that

\bigcup_{\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{B}(M^{*})}\;\bigcap_{\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{B}([{\mathbf{B}}]_{\mathcal{S}})}\widetilde{\mathscr{Q}}({\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}},\mathcal{T})\subset\bigcup_{\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{B}(M^{*})}\;\bigcap_{\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{B}([{\mathbf{B}}]_{\mathcal{S}})}\widetilde{\mathscr{Q}}({\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}).

(131)

Therefore, if we denote the intersection of the sets
\widetilde{\mathscr{Q}}({\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}},\mathcal{T})
when
{\mathbf{C}}
runs over all members of
\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(M)
as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{Q}}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\bigcap_{{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(M)}\widetilde{\mathscr{Q}}({\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}},\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Bigl\{(R_{1},\dotsc,R_{K})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{K}\colon\sum_{k\in\mathcal{T}}R_{k}<\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad

H([
u
]_
T
) + J(
B
,M) - H(
Bu
—Y)
}

(132)

where

J({\mathbf{B}},M)=\inf_{{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(M)}H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y)

(133)

then the order of set operations in (
130
) can be exchanged in such way that the intersection over
{\mathbf{C}}
is moved to the innermost position (inside the intersection over
\mathcal{T}
), leading to

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{Q}}({\mathbf{B}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigcap_{M\in\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}^{\circ}(L_{\mathsf{B}})}\;\bigcup_{\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{B}(M^{*})}\;\bigcap_{\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{B}([{\mathbf{B}}]_{\mathcal{S}})}\widetilde{\mathscr{Q}}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T}).

(134)

By comparison between (
52
) and (
132
), as well as between (
54
) and (
134
), we clearly identify that for
(\mathbb{U},\mathbb{A})=(\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}},\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}})
we have
\widetilde{\mathscr{Q}}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T})=\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T})
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{Q}}({\mathbf{B}})=\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}})
. This proves Theorem
1
.

Appendix K
Proof of Theorem
2

In order to extend the finite-field result of Theorem
1
to the integers, we set the auxiliary alphabet to be
\mathbb{U}=\mathbb{Z}
and fix an auxiliary distribution
P_{\bm{u}}=\prod_{k=1}^{K}P_{U_{k}}
, an integer coefficient matrix
{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L\times K}
and a channel law
P_{Y|X_{1},\dotsc,X_{K}}
. Given these, we will show how one can construct a sequence of rate regions that are achievable by Theorem
1
such that asymptotically, its limit represents a rate region for reliably recovering the
{\mathbf{A}}
-linear combination of auxiliary codewords, and is given by
\mathscr{R}({\mathbf{A}})
as evaluated in the context of Theorem
2
.

We will prove Theorem
2
in two major steps: in a first part, we will assume that the integer auxiliary vector
\bm{u}=(U_{1},\dotsc,U_{K})\in\mathbb{Z}^{K}
is finitely supported, and prove that under this assumption, as long as we let the field size
\mathsf{q}
be large enough, all finite-field operations (i.e., with modulo-
\mathsf{q}
reduction) involved in the evaluation of
\mathscr{R}(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{A}}))
become isomorphic to integer operations (i.e.,
without
modulo-
\mathsf{q}
operations) almost surely. Thereby we prove Theorem
2
for the special case of finitely supported integer auxiliaries. In a second and final part, we will relax this assumption of finite support so as to extend the validity of Theorem
2
to infinitely supported, finite-entropy integer auxiliaries.

In essence, the proof consists in determining an inner bound on the set limit

\mathscr{R}^{\infty}({\mathbf{A}})\triangleq\varliminf_{\mathsf{q}\to\infty}\mathscr{R}(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{A}}))

(135)

that will turn out to be precisely
\mathscr{R}({\mathbf{A}})
(from Theorem
2
). A first inner-bounding step is taken by reducing the union over
{\mathbf{B}}
that appears in

\mathscr{R}(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{A}}))=\bigcup_{{\mathbf{B}}}\mathscr{Q}(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}})).

(136)

Recall that here,
{\mathbf{B}}
runs over all matrices satisfying
\Lambda_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}})^{\mathsf{T}})\supseteq\Lambda_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{A}})^{\mathsf{T}})
.
This inclusion relation is implied by
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}({\mathbf{B}}^{\mathsf{T}})\supseteq\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}({\mathbf{A}}^{\mathsf{T}})
. In fact, the relationship
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}({\mathbf{B}}^{\mathsf{T}})\supseteq\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}({\mathbf{A}}^{\mathsf{T}})
is equivalent to there existing a (tall) integer matrix
{\mathbf{R}}^{\mathsf{T}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}\times L}
such that
{\mathbf{B}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\mathbf{R}}^{\mathsf{T}}={\mathbf{A}}^{\mathsf{T}}
. If such a matrix exists, then clearly there also exists a finite-field matrix
{\mathbf{R}}_{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathsf{T}}\in\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}\times L}
such that
\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}}^{\mathsf{T}}){\mathbf{R}}_{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathsf{T}}=\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{A}}^{\mathsf{T}})
, for it suffices to set
{\mathbf{R}}_{\mathsf{q}}=\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{R}})
. Hence, by replacing the constraint
\Lambda_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}})^{\mathsf{T}})\supseteq\Lambda_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{A}})^{\mathsf{T}})
with the constraint
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}({\mathbf{B}}^{\mathsf{T}})\supseteq\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}({\mathbf{A}}^{\mathsf{T}})
, the union over matrices
{\mathbf{B}}
is not increased. It follows that

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}^{\infty}({\mathbf{A}})

\displaystyle\supseteq

\displaystyle\varliminf_{\mathsf{q}\to\infty}\bigcup_{{\mathbf{B}}\colon\Lambda({\mathbf{B}}^{\mathsf{T}})\supseteq\Lambda({\mathbf{A}}^{\mathsf{T}})}\mathscr{Q}(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}}))

(137)

\displaystyle\supseteq

\displaystyle\bigcup_{{\mathbf{B}}\colon\Lambda({\mathbf{B}}^{\mathsf{T}})\supseteq\Lambda({\mathbf{A}}^{\mathsf{T}})}\varliminf_{\mathsf{q}\to\infty}\mathscr{Q}(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}}))

(where we have omitted the subscript
\mathbb{Z}
in
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}(\cdot)
to alleviate notation).
The exchange of limit and union in the last step is due to (
12
).
In (
137
), the set
\mathscr{Q}(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}}))
is expressible as [cf. (
54
)]

\mathscr{Q}(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}}))=\bigcap_{M^{*}\in\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}^{\circ}(L_{\mathsf{B}})}\;\bigcup_{\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{B}(M^{*})}\;\bigcap_{\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{B}([\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}})]_{\mathcal{S}})}\mathscr{Q}(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}}),M,\mathcal{T})

(138)

where
\mathscr{Q}(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}}),M,\mathcal{T})
, in turn, can be expressed as [cf. (
52
)]

\mathscr{Q}(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}}),M,\mathcal{T})=\Bigl\{(R_{1},\dotsc,R_{K})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{K}\colon\sum_{k\in\mathcal{T}}R_{k}<\\
H(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}([\bm{u}]_{\mathcal{T}}))+\min_{\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}})\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(M)}H(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u})|Y_{\mathsf{q}})-H(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}}\bm{u})|Y_{\mathsf{q}})\Bigr\}

(139)

since
\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}})\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}})\phi_{\mathsf{q}}(\bm{u})=\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u})
where
Y_{\mathsf{q}}
denotes the channel output induced by the auxiliary
\phi_{\mathsf{q}}(\bm{u})
. In writing (
139
), we have replaced the algebraic entropies that appear in (
52
) with discrete entropies. This replacement is legitimate due to all random variables taking value in finite fields (cf. Lemma
3
).

For now, let us assume that
\bm{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{K}
has finite support and let
\tau\in\mathbb{N}
be some natural number large enough to satisfy

\bm{u}\in\llbracket 2\tau+1\rrbracket^{K}\quad\text{(almost surely)}

(140)

or equivalently,
\lVert\bm{u}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\tau
almost surely.

We will prove that for any given
{\mathbf{B}}
, when
\mathsf{q}
is larger than a certain constant
\bar{\mathsf{q}}
(that possibly depends on
{\mathbf{B}}
and
\tau
), we have

\mathscr{Q}(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}}),M,\mathcal{T})=\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T})

(141)

wherein on the left-hand side, the rate region
\mathscr{Q}(\cdot,M,\mathcal{T})
is evaluated with the first argument taking value in the finite field (
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}
), whereas on the right-hand side, it takes value in the integers (
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{Z}
).
For a more compact notation, we henceforth refer to the min-entropy term appearing in (
139
) as
20
20

20

The only innovation in notation as compared to (
53
) is the subscript
\mathsf{q}
, to emphasize the dependency of this quantity on the field size
\mathsf{q}

J_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}},M)\triangleq\min_{\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}})\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(M)}H(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u})|Y_{\mathsf{q}}).

(142)

Without loss of generality, we will assume throughout that the matrix
{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}\times L_{\mathsf{B}}}
has full column rank, i.e., that its number of rows
L_{\mathsf{C}}
is always equal to the rank of the matroid
M
.

In the following, we will prove in successive steps that the multiple dependencies on the field size
\mathsf{q}
in the expressions (
138
), (
139
) and (
142
) can all be removed for sufficiently large
\mathsf{q}
. This will eventually allow us to evaluate
\varliminf_{\mathsf{q}\to\infty}\mathscr{Q}(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}}))
. Specifically, we will show that (
141
) holds for any prime
\mathsf{q}
larger than the prime number
\bar{\mathsf{q}}
, which shall be defined as the smallest prime larger than
\max_{i=1,\dotsc,10}\bar{\mathsf{q}}_{i}
, where

\displaystyle\bar{\mathsf{q}}_{1}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle(2\tau+1)\lVert{\mathbf{B}}\rVert_{\infty},

(143a)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathsf{q}}_{2}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle 1+2\prod_{i=1}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}}\lVert\mathbf{b}_{i}\rVert_{2},

(143b)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathsf{q}}_{3}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle 9^{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}},

(143c)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathsf{q}}_{4}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\psi_{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}}^{2}L_{\mathsf{C}}^{8},

(143d)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathsf{q}}_{5}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\tau,

(143e)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathsf{q}}_{6}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\left(4\left\lVert{\mathbf{B}}\right\rVert_{\infty}\tau\right)^{2L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}},

(143f)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathsf{q}}_{7}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\min\{\mathsf{q}\in\mathbb{P}\colon\forall M\in\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(L_{\mathsf{B}})\colon|\chi(M)|=\infty\},

(143g)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathsf{q}}_{8}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\max\bigl\{\max\{\mathbb{P}\setminus\chi(M)\}\colon M\in\mathscr{M}_{\infty}(L_{\mathsf{B}})\bigr\},

(143h)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathsf{q}}_{9}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle 2^{2^{2^{L_{\mathsf{B}}^{5}}}},

(143i)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathsf{q}}_{10}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\bar{\theta}(\tau\lVert{\mathbf{B}}\rVert_{\infty})^{4/3}.

(143j)

Here,
\mathbf{b}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}
denotes the
i
-th row of
{\mathbf{B}}=[\mathbf{b}_{1}\dotso\mathbf{b}_{L_{\mathsf{B}}}]^{\mathsf{T}}
,
\psi_{\ell}
,
\chi(\cdot)
,
\mathscr{M}_{\infty}(\cdot)
, and
\bar{\theta}(\cdot)
denote specific sequences, functions and sets, all of which will be duly defined in what follows. This constant
\bar{\mathsf{q}}
depends solely on
{\mathbf{B}}
and its dimensions
(K,L_{\mathsf{B}})
, as well as on the parameter
\tau
. Each constant
\bar{\mathsf{q}}_{i},i=1,\dotsc,10
will be specified and motivated along the proof steps that will be detailed in the following.
For clarity, we will structure these successive proof steps into five separate paragraphs. Namely, we will prove that for
\mathsf{q}>\bar{\mathsf{q}}
,

•

the entropies
H(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}([\bm{u}]_{\mathcal{T}}))
and
H(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}}\bm{u})|Y_{\mathsf{q}})
appearing in (
139
) become equal to
H([\bm{u}]_{\mathcal{T}})
and
H({\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y)
, respectively (Step 1);

•

the index set
\mathscr{B}([\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}})]_{\mathcal{S}})
appearing in (
138
) becomes equal to
\mathscr{B}([{\mathbf{B}}]_{\mathcal{S}})
(Step 2);

•

the min-entropy term
J_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}},M)
appearing in (
139
), which involves an entropy
H(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u})|Y_{\mathsf{q}})
of matrix-vector products over the finite field
\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}
, can be expressed in terms of the entropy
H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y)
which only involves matrix-vector products over the integers, and does not depend on
\mathsf{q}
(Step 3);

•

the set
\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}^{\circ}(L_{\mathsf{B}})
appearing in (
138
) in the intersection over matroids
M^{*}
becomes equal to
\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\circ}(L_{\mathsf{B}})
(Step 4);

•

in the evaluation of
J_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}},M)
[cf. (
142
)], we can have the integer matrix
{\mathbf{C}}
run over the set
\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{Z}}(M)
instead of having
\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}})
run over
\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(M)
, without loss of generality (Step 5).

We argue in conclusion that the entire finite-field rate region tends (in the sense of an inner set limit) to its integer counterpart as
\mathsf{q}\to\infty
.
Since all the above steps rely on the assumption of a
finitely supported
integer auxiliary
\bm{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{K}
, in a final proof step we will relax this assumption by another limit-taking, so as to allow for integer auxiliaries with
infinite support
(under mild assumptions).

Step 1

Notice that since, by assumption, the support of
\bm{u}
is contained in
\llbracket 2\tau+1\rrbracket^{K}
almost surely, for
\mathsf{q}>2\tau+1
we have
H(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}([\bm{u}]_{\mathcal{T}})))=H([\bm{u}]_{\mathcal{T}})
.
Likewise, for
\mathsf{q}\geq(2\tau+1)\lVert{\mathbf{B}}\rVert_{\infty}
, there is a one-to-one correspondence between
\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}}\bm{u})
and
{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}
such that all instances of
Y_{\mathsf{q}}
can be replaced by
Y
, hence
H(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}}\bm{u})|Y_{\mathsf{q}})=H({\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y)
.
Since
\mathsf{q}>(2\tau+1)\lVert{\mathbf{B}}\rVert_{\infty}\geq 2\tau+1
is implied by
\mathsf{q}>\bar{\mathsf{q}}
[cf. (
143a
)], we have that (
139
) simplifies to

\mathscr{Q}(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}}),M,\mathcal{T})=\Bigl\{(R_{1},\dotsc,R_{K})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{K}\colon\\
\sum_{k\in\mathcal{T}}R_{k}<H([\bm{u}]_{\mathcal{T}})+J_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}},M)-H({\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y)\Bigr\},\quad\textnormal{(for $\mathsf{q}\geq\bar{\mathsf{q}}$)}.

(144)

Likewise,
H(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u})|Y_{\mathsf{q}})=H(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u})|Y)
, so for sufficiently large
\mathsf{q}
, (
142
) may be written as

J_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}},M)\triangleq\min_{\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}})\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(M)}H(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u})|Y),\quad\textnormal{(for $\mathsf{q}\geq\bar{\mathsf{q}}$)}.

(145)

Step 2

Using the following Lemma
23
, we can establish that
\mathscr{B}([\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}})]_{\mathcal{S}})=\mathscr{B}([{\mathbf{B}}]_{\mathcal{S}})
for sufficiently large
\mathsf{q}
.

Lemma 23
.

For any full row-rank integer matrix
{\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times n}
and an odd prime number
\mathsf{q}
satisfying

\mathsf{q}>1+2\sqrt{\det({\mathbf{Q}}{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathsf{T}})}
, we have
M(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{Q}}))=M({\mathbf{Q}})
.

By applying Lemma
23
to the matrix
{\mathbf{Q}}=[{\mathbf{B}}]_{\mathcal{S}}
, we conclude that
\mathscr{B}([\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}})]_{\mathcal{S}})=\mathscr{B}([{\mathbf{B}}]_{\mathcal{S}})
if

\mathsf{q}\geq 1+2\max_{\mathcal{S}\subset[L_{\mathsf{B}}]}\sqrt{\det([{\mathbf{B}}]_{\mathcal{S}}[{\mathbf{B}}]_{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})}.

(146)

Due to Hadamard’s inequality applied on the rows of
{\mathbf{B}}=[\mathbf{b}_{1}\dotso\mathbf{b}_{L_{\mathsf{B}}}]^{\mathsf{T}}
, all of which have Euclidean norm
\lVert\mathbf{b}_{i}\rVert_{2}\geq 1
, a sufficient condition for (
146
) is

\mathsf{q}\geq 1+2\prod_{i=1}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}}\lVert\mathbf{b}_{i}\rVert_{2}

(147)

which is implied by
\mathsf{q}>\bar{\mathsf{q}}
[cf. (
143b
)].

Proof:

Recall that
M(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{Q}}))
is characterized by its collection of bases
\mathscr{B}(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{Q}}))
, which contains all index sets
\mathcal{B}\subset[n]
of cardinality
m
such that the submatrix
[{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathsf{T}}]_{\mathcal{B}}
has full rank. Equivalently, we can test this full-rank condition by establishing whether
\det(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}([{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathsf{T}}]_{\mathcal{B}}))\neq 0
, which is further equivalent to

\mymod_{\mathsf{q}}\bigl(\det([{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathsf{T}}]_{\mathcal{B}})\bigr)\neq 0.

(148)

By the Cauchy–Binet formula
[
65
, Sec. 0.8.7]
,

\displaystyle\det({\mathbf{Q}}{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathsf{T}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathcal{B}\subset[n]}\det([{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathsf{T}}]_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathsf{T}}[{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathsf{T}}]_{\mathcal{B}})

(149)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\det([{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathsf{T}}]_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathsf{T}}[{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathsf{T}}]_{\mathcal{B}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\det([{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathsf{T}}]_{\mathcal{B}})^{2}.

We infer that, if
\mathsf{q}
is large enough to satisfy

\left(\frac{\mathsf{q}-1}{2}\right)^{2}\geq\det({\mathbf{Q}}{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathsf{T}})

then
\left|\det([{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathsf{T}}]_{\mathcal{B}})\right|\leq(\mathsf{q}-1)/2
and therefore
\mymod_{\mathsf{q}}\bigl(\det([{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathsf{T}}]_{\mathcal{B}})\bigr)=\det([{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathsf{T}}]_{\mathcal{B}})
, which implies that (
148
) is equivalent to

\det([{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathsf{T}}]_{\mathcal{B}})\neq 0

for any
\mathcal{B}\subset[n]
. Consequently,
M(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{Q}}))
and
M({\mathbf{Q}})
have the same collection of bases, and are therefore equal matroids.
∎

Step 3

We now turn our attention to the min-entropy term
J_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}},M)
as defined in (
142
). For
\mathsf{q}>\bar{\mathsf{q}}
, the term
J_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}},M)
as expressed in (
145
) can be written as

\displaystyle J_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}},M)

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\colon\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}})\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(M)}H(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u})|Y)

(150)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{C}}\in\llbracket\mathsf{q}\rrbracket^{L_{\mathsf{C}}\times L_{\mathsf{B}}}\colon\\
\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}})\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(M)\end{subarray}}H(\mymod_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u})|Y),\quad\textnormal{(for $\mathsf{q}>\bar{\mathsf{q}}$)}.

The last equality holds because
\phi_{\mathsf{q}}(x)
is isomorphic to
\mymod_{\mathsf{q}}(x)
[see definitions of
\mymod_{\mathsf{q}}(\cdot)
and
\phi_{\mathsf{q}}(\cdot)
in (
4
), (
7
)] and thus switching from
\phi_{\mathsf{q}}(\cdot)
to
\mymod_{\mathsf{q}}(\cdot)
leaves the entropy value unchanged, as well as because the entries of
{\mathbf{C}}
may be restricted from
\mathbb{Z}
to the finite set
\llbracket\mathsf{q}\rrbracket
without loss of generality.

In what follows, we will show that the optimization domain in (
150
) can be further reduced, in that the entries of
{\mathbf{C}}
may be restricted to a subset of
\llbracket\mathsf{q}\rrbracket
, and that as a consequence, for sufficiently large
\mathsf{q}
, the modulo operation
\mymod_{\mathsf{q}}(\cdot)
in the entropy term of (
150
) can be removed—loosely speaking, because no “wrap-arounds” by the modulo operation ever occur.

The first thing to observe in (
150
) is that, by the definition of
\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(M)
[cf. Section
IV-D
, Definition
15
], for any full-rank
{\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}\times L_{\mathsf{C}}}
and any given matroid
M
, it holds that
\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}})\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(M)
if and only if
{\mathbf{Q}}\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}})\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(M)
. In addition, the entropy term, which constitutes the minimization’s objective function, is invariant against invertible row operations on
\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}})
, in the sense that

\displaystyle H({\mathbf{Q}}\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u})|Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H({\mathbf{Q}}\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}})\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}}\bm{u})|Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}})\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}}\bm{u})|Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u})|Y).

This means that the set
\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(M)
, which determines the optimization domain, can be partitioned into
equivalence classes
. Specifically, we define the equivalence class of some full row-rank
\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}})\in\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}\times L_{\mathsf{B}}}
as the set

\mathscr{E}[{\mathbf{C}}]\triangleq\Bigl\{{\mathbf{Q}}\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}})\colon\textnormal{${\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}\times L_{\mathsf{C}}}$ is full-rank}\Bigr\}

(151)

and say that
{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
are equivalent if and only if
\mathscr{E}[{\mathbf{C}}]=\mathscr{E}[{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}]
.
By contrast, let us define the lattice
21
21

21

Since this is a lattice of
matrices
rather than
vectors
, we deviate from the previously used notation
\Lambda({\mathbf{C}})
(with round parentheses) to avoid confusion.

\Lambda[{\mathbf{C}}]\triangleq\bigl\{{\mathbf{Q}}\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}})\colon{\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}\times L_{\mathsf{C}}}\bigr\}.

(152)

For any
{\mathbf{C}}\in\llbracket\mathsf{q}\rrbracket^{L_{\mathsf{C}}\times L_{\mathsf{B}}}
such that
\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}})
has full row rank, we shall choose as the
representative
of its equivalence class
\mathscr{E}[{\mathbf{C}}]
the
{\mathbf{C}}_{\star}\in\mathscr{E}[{\mathbf{C}}]
with smallest infinity norm, i.e.,

{\mathbf{C}}_{\star}\triangleq\argmin_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{E}[{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}]=\mathscr{E}[{\mathbf{C}}]}\left\lVert{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\infty}

(153)

(ties being broken arbitrarily).
22
22

22

Although
{\mathbf{C}}_{\star}
is technically a function of
{\mathbf{C}}
, we refrain from writing
{\mathbf{C}}_{\star}({\mathbf{C}})
to alleviate notation.

Then, by definition of the equivalence classes, the matrix
{\mathbf{C}}
in the minimization problem (
142
) can be replaced by its small-norm equivalent
{\mathbf{C}}_{\star}
without loss of generality, yielding

J_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}},M)=\min_{\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}})\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(M)}H(\mymod_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}}_{\star}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u})|Y),\quad\textnormal{(for $\mathsf{q}>\bar{\mathsf{q}}$)}.

(154)

Next, we will derive an upper bound on the norm of
{\mathbf{C}}_{\star}
. Using the
\vectorize(\cdot)
operation that stacks the columns of a matrix on top of each other into a single column vector, let us define the vectorized versions of the sets
\mathscr{E}[{\mathbf{C}}]
and
\Lambda[{\mathbf{C}}]
as

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{\mathrm{vec}}[{\mathbf{C}}]

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\Bigl\{\mathrm{vec}({\mathbf{Q}}\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}}))\colon\textnormal{${\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}\times L_{\mathsf{C}}}$ is full-rank}\Bigr\}

(155a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Bigl\{(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}})^{\mathsf{T}}\otimes{\mathbf{I}}_{L_{\mathsf{C}}})\mathrm{vec}({\mathbf{Q}})\colon\textnormal{${\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}\times L_{\mathsf{C}}}$ is full-rank}\Bigr\}

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\mathrm{vec}}[{\mathbf{C}}]

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\bigl\{\mathrm{vec}({\mathbf{Q}}\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}}))\colon{\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}\times L_{\mathsf{C}}}\bigr\}

(155b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigl\{(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}})^{\mathsf{T}}\otimes{\mathbf{I}}_{L_{\mathsf{C}}})\mathrm{vec}({\mathbf{Q}})\colon{\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}\times L_{\mathsf{C}}}\bigr\}.

Note that
\phi_{\mathsf{q}}^{-1}\left(\Lambda_{\mathrm{vec}}[{\mathbf{C}}]\right)

of the lattice
\Lambda_{\mathrm{vec}}[{\mathbf{C}}]
forms a sublattice of
\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}}
that can be expressed as follows:

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathsf{q}}^{-1}\left(\Lambda_{\mathrm{vec}}[{\mathbf{C}}]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Bigl\{\phi_{\mathsf{q}}^{-1}\bigl(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}}\otimes{\mathbf{I}}_{L_{\mathsf{C}}})\bm{k}\bigr)\colon\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}}\Bigr\}

(156)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{bmatrix}({\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}}\otimes{\mathbf{I}}_{L_{\mathsf{C}}})&\mathsf{q}{\mathbf{I}}_{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}}\end{bmatrix}\bm{k}\colon\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}+L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Lambda(\hat{{\mathbf{C}}})

where
\hat{{\mathbf{C}}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}\times L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}}
is a full-rank integer matrix whose columns form a basis of the lattice generated by the columns of
\bigl[({\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}}\otimes{\mathbf{I}}_{L_{\mathsf{C}}})\ \ \mathsf{q}{\mathbf{I}}_{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}}\bigr]
.
By a counting argument, the determinant of
\Lambda(\hat{{\mathbf{C}}})
equals

\det(\Lambda(\hat{{\mathbf{C}}}))=\bigl|\det(\hat{{\mathbf{C}}})\bigr|=\frac{\left|\llbracket\mathsf{q}\rrbracket^{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}}\right|}{\left|\Lambda_{\mathrm{vec}}[{\mathbf{C}}]\right|}=\mathsf{q}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}-L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}}.

(157)

Note that since
\mathscr{E}_{\mathrm{vec}}[{\mathbf{C}}]\subset\Lambda_{\mathrm{vec}}[{\mathbf{C}}]
, we have

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathsf{q}}^{-1}\left(\mathscr{E}_{\mathrm{vec}}[{\mathbf{C}}]\right)\subset\Lambda(\hat{{\mathbf{C}}}).

(158)

On the other hand, it becomes apparent from their definitions that the sets
\mathscr{E}_{\mathrm{vec}}[{\mathbf{C}}]
and
\mathscr{E}[{\mathbf{C}}]
have a cardinality
\left|\mathscr{E}_{\mathrm{vec}}[{\mathbf{C}}]\right|=\left|\mathscr{E}[{\mathbf{C}}]\right|
equal to the number of full-rank matrices in
\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}\times L_{\mathsf{C}}}
, which is given by a polynomial (in
\mathsf{q}
) of degree
L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}
with integer coefficients
[
66
]
:

\displaystyle\left|\mathscr{E}[{\mathbf{C}}]\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{i=0}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}-1}(\mathsf{q}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}}-\mathsf{q}^{i})=\sum_{i=0}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}(L_{\mathsf{C}}+1)/2}\gamma_{i}\mathsf{q}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}-i}.

(159)

We can readily check that
\gamma_{0}=1
. For other coefficients
\gamma_{i}
, it is easy to see that
|\gamma_{i}|
is upper-bounded by the number of ways
\psi_{i}
in which
i
can be written as the sum of distinct natural numbers
[
67
, p. 836, Table 24.5]
(see also
[
68
]
and references therein). For the evaluation of
\psi_{i}
, a recurrence relation and generating function are known
[
69
]
.
Hence, a simple lower bound on
\left|\mathscr{E}[{\mathbf{C}}]\right|
is given by

\displaystyle\left|\mathscr{E}[{\mathbf{C}}]\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{q}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}}+\sum_{i=1}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}(L_{\mathsf{C}}+1)/2}\gamma_{i}\mathsf{q}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}-i}

(160)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\mathsf{q}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}}-\sum_{i=1}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}(L_{\mathsf{C}}+1)/2}\psi_{i}\mathsf{q}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}-i}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\mathsf{q}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}}-\psi_{L_{\mathsf{C}}(L_{\mathsf{C}}+1)/2}\sum_{i=1}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}(L_{\mathsf{C}}+1)/2}\mathsf{q}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}-i}

\displaystyle\geq
