\displaystyle\mathsf{q}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}}-2\psi_{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}}L_{\mathsf{C}}^{4}\mathsf{q}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}-1}.

For the last bounding step, we have used that
\psi_{L_{\mathsf{C}}(L_{\mathsf{C}}+1)/2}\leq\psi_{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}}
(because
\psi_{i}
is a monotone sequence) and that
\mathsf{q}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}-i}\leq\mathsf{q}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}-1}
, as well as the fact that the sum of integers from
1
to
L_{\mathsf{C}}(L_{\mathsf{C}}+1)/2
is upper-bounded by
2L_{\mathsf{C}}^{4}
.

Note that since
\left|\Lambda[{\mathbf{C}}]\right|=\left|\Lambda_{\mathrm{vec}}[{\mathbf{C}}]\right|=\mathsf{q}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}}
, which equals the minuend on the right-hand side of (
160
), the cardinality of the complement

\bar{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{vec}}[{\mathbf{C}}]\triangleq\Lambda_{\mathrm{vec}}[{\mathbf{C}}]\setminus\mathscr{E}_{\mathrm{vec}}[{\mathbf{C}}]

(161)

is upper-bounded as

\left|\bar{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{vec}}[{\mathbf{C}}]\right|\leq 2\psi_{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}}L_{\mathsf{C}}^{4}\mathsf{q}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}-1}.

(162)

Using van der Corput’s Convex Body Theorem [cf. Lem.
11
, Subsection
IV-C
] in its form (
48
), we can lower-bound the number of lattice points from
\Lambda(\hat{{\mathbf{C}}})
enclosed in the closed convex body
\kappa\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}}=[-\kappa,\kappa]^{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}}
for any
\kappa>0
as follows:

\displaystyle\bigl|\Lambda(\hat{{\mathbf{C}}})\cap\kappa\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}}\bigr|+1

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle\frac{\vol(\kappa\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}})}{2^{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}-1}\det(\Lambda(\hat{{\mathbf{C}}}))}

(163)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\frac{\kappa^{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}}}{\mathsf{q}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}-L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}}}.

Hereinafter, let us set the parameter
\kappa
to be a function of
\mathsf{q}
, namely,

\kappa(\mathsf{q})\triangleq\mathsf{q}^{1-\frac{1}{2L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}}}.

(164)

On the one hand, this choice of
\kappa(\mathsf{q})
ensures that
\kappa(\mathsf{q})\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}}\cap\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}}
(whose diameter grows linearly in
\mathsf{q}
) is eventually contained in
\llbracket\mathsf{q}\rrbracket^{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}}
(whose diameter grows sublinearly in
\mathsf{q}
) for sufficiently large
\mathsf{q}
. Specifically, this inclusion holds as soon as
\mathsf{q}>9^{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}}
, which is implied by
\mathsf{q}>\bar{\mathsf{q}}
[cf. (
143c
)]. In fact,
\mathsf{q}>9^{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}}
is equivalent to
\mathsf{q}^{1-1/(2L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}})}<\mathsf{q}/3
, which implies
\mathsf{q}^{1-1/(2L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}})}<(\mathsf{q}-1)/2
(since
\mathsf{q}/3\leq(\mathsf{q}-1)/2
due to
\mathsf{q}\geq 3
, because
\mathsf{q}
is an odd prime). In summary,

\displaystyle\Lambda(\hat{{\mathbf{C}}})\cap\kappa(\mathsf{q})\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}}

\displaystyle\subset

\displaystyle\Lambda(\hat{{\mathbf{C}}})\cap\llbracket\mathsf{q}\rrbracket^{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}}

(165)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathsf{q}}^{-1}\bigl(\Lambda_{\mathrm{vec}}[{\mathbf{C}}]\bigr),\quad\textnormal{(for $\mathsf{q}>\bar{\mathsf{q}}$)}.

On the other hand, our choice
\kappa(\mathsf{q})=\mathsf{q}^{1-1/(2L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}})}
ensures that for sufficiently large
\mathsf{q}
, the scaled cube
\kappa(\mathsf{q})\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}}
contains more lattice points from
\Lambda(\hat{{\mathbf{C}}})
than does
\phi_{\mathsf{q}}^{-1}(\bar{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{vec}}[{\mathbf{C}}])\cap\llbracket\mathsf{q}\rrbracket^{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}}
. In fact, by combining the inequalities (
162
) and (
163
) for our choice of
\kappa(\mathsf{q})
,

\displaystyle\bigl|\Lambda(\hat{{\mathbf{C}}})\cap\kappa(\mathsf{q})\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}}\bigr|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle-1+\frac{2\mathsf{q}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}-1/2}}{\mathsf{q}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}-L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}}}

(166)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-1+2\mathsf{q}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}-1/2}

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle 2\psi_{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}}L_{\mathsf{C}}^{4}\mathsf{q}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}-1}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\left|\bar{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{vec}}[{\mathbf{C}}]\right|,\quad\textnormal{(for $\mathsf{q}>\bar{\mathsf{q}}$)}

where the second (strict) inequality certainly holds if
\sqrt{\mathsf{q}}>2\psi_{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}}L_{\mathsf{C}}^{4}
, which is implied by
\mathsf{q}>\bar{\mathsf{q}}
[cf. (
143d
)]. To see this, notice that
\sqrt{\mathsf{q}}>2\psi_{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}}L_{\mathsf{C}}^{4}
is equivalent to
\mathsf{q}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}-1/2}>2\psi_{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}}L_{\mathsf{C}}^{4}\mathsf{q}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}-1}
, which due to
\mathsf{q}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}-1/2}>1
(because of
\mathsf{q}\geq 3
and
L_{\mathsf{C}}\geq 1
) in turn implies said inequality
-1+2\mathsf{q}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}-1/2}>2\psi_{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}}L_{\mathsf{C}}^{4}\mathsf{q}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}^{2}-1}
.

It follows from this inequality that, for
\mathsf{q}>\bar{\mathsf{q}}
, the lattice points in the intersection
\Lambda(\hat{{\mathbf{C}}})\cap\kappa(\mathsf{q})\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}}
outnumber the points in the complement of
\mathscr{E}[{\mathbf{C}}]
, and therefore the cube
\kappa(\mathsf{q})\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}}
must contain at least one point from
\phi_{\mathsf{q}}^{-1}(\mathscr{E}_{\mathrm{vec}}[{\mathbf{C}}])\cap\llbracket\mathsf{q}\rrbracket^{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}}
. Hence the infinity norm of
{\mathbf{C}}_{\star}\in\phi_{\mathsf{q}}^{-1}(\mathscr{E}[{\mathbf{C}}])\cap\llbracket\mathsf{q}\rrbracket^{L_{\mathsf{C}}\times L_{\mathsf{B}}}
is at most
\kappa(\mathsf{q})
. This in turn means that in the expression of
J_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}},M)
from (
154
), without loss of generality we can restrain the domain of
{\mathbf{C}}
to the scaled cube
\kappa(\mathsf{q})\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}}
, whose side length grows sublinearly in
\mathsf{q}
, i.e.,

J_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}},M)=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{C}}\colon\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}})\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(M)\colon\\
\lVert{\mathbf{C}}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\kappa(\mathsf{q})\end{subarray}}H(\mymod_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u})|Y).

(167)

Recall that, by assumption [cf. (
140
)] we have
\bm{u}\in\tau\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}\cap\mathbb{Z}^{K}
almost surely, so the argument of the entropy term in (
167
) has infinity norm upper-bounded (almost surely) as

\displaystyle\left\lVert{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\kappa(\mathsf{q})\left\lVert{\mathbf{B}}\right\rVert_{\infty}\lVert\bm{u}\rVert_{\infty}

(168)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left\lVert{\mathbf{B}}\right\rVert_{\infty}\tau\kappa(\mathsf{q}),\quad\textnormal{(for $\mathsf{q}>\bar{\mathsf{q}}$).}

Since the right-hand side of (
168
) grows sublinearly in
\mathsf{q}
, we infer that it is eventually upper-bounded by
\frac{\mathsf{q}-1}{2}
as
\mathsf{q}
grows large. Specifically, a sufficient condition for
\left\lVert{\mathbf{B}}\right\rVert_{\infty}\tau\kappa(\mathsf{q})\leq\frac{\mathsf{q}-1}{2}
to hold is that

\mathsf{q}>\left(4\left\lVert{\mathbf{B}}\right\rVert_{\infty}\tau\right)^{2L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}}

(169)

which is implied by
\mathsf{q}>\bar{\mathsf{q}}
[cf. (
143f
)].
Hence, if
\mathsf{q}>\bar{\mathsf{q}}
the modulo operation
\mymod_{\mathsf{q}}(\cdot)
becomes superfluous, in the sense that for
\lVert{\mathbf{C}}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\kappa(\mathsf{q})
,

\mymod_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u})={\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u},\quad\textnormal{(for $\mathsf{q}>\bar{\mathsf{q}}$, almost surely).}

(170)

This identity finally allows us to write

\displaystyle J_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}},M)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{C}}\in\llbracket 2\lfloor\kappa(\mathsf{q})\rfloor+1\rrbracket^{L_{\mathsf{C}}\times L_{\mathsf{B}}}\colon\\
\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}})\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(M)\end{subarray}}H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y),\quad\textnormal{(for $\mathsf{q}>\bar{\mathsf{q}}$).}

(171)

This way, transitioning from (
167
) to (
171
), we have removed the
\mymod_{\mathsf{q}}(\cdot)
operation that appeared in the minimization’s objective.

Step 4

Consider the outer set limit

\mathscr{M}_{\infty}(L_{\mathsf{B}})\triangleq\varlimsup_{\mathsf{q}\to\infty}\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(L_{\mathsf{B}}).

(172)

Recalling the definition of the outer set limit given in (
10b
), we can make the outer intersection start above any arbitrary offset
\bar{\mathsf{q}}>0
without changing the value of the set limit, i.e.,

\mathscr{M}_{\infty}(L_{\mathsf{B}})=\bigcap_{\{\mathsf{q}_{n}\colon\mathsf{q}_{n}\geq\bar{\mathsf{q}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}}\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}_{n}}}(L_{\mathsf{B}})

(173)

where the intersection is over all growing sequences
\{\mathsf{q}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of prime numbers (starting at a value larger or equal to
\bar{\mathsf{q}}
).

Every matroid in
\mathscr{M}_{\infty}(L_{\mathsf{B}})
has infinite characteristic set. In fact, if some matroid
M\in\mathscr{M}_{\infty}(L_{\mathsf{B}})
had a finite characteristic set
|\chi(M)|<\infty
, we could pick the largest element of
\chi(M)
and set the offset
\bar{\mathsf{q}}
to be larger than that element. Since there are only finitely many matroids in
\mathscr{M}_{\infty}(L_{\mathsf{B}})
(in any case, no more than
2^{L_{\mathsf{B}}}
), this process of increasing the offset
\bar{\mathsf{q}}
can be repeated a finite number of times for all other matroids
M\in\mathscr{M}_{\infty}(L_{\mathsf{B}})
with
|\chi(M)|<\infty
until no more such matroids exist in
\{\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(L_{\mathsf{B}})\colon\mathsf{q}>\bar{\mathsf{q}}\}
, thus resulting in a contradiction. Hence, the prime number

\bar{\mathsf{q}}_{7}\triangleq\min\{\mathsf{q}\in\mathbb{P}\colon\forall M\in\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(L_{\mathsf{B}})\colon|\chi(M)|=\infty\}

(174)

is well defined. Since by lemma
14
(cf. Definition
16
),
|\chi(M)|=\infty
implies
0\in\chi(M)
, we conclude that every
M\in\mathscr{M}_{\infty}(L_{\mathsf{B}})
is representable over
\mathbb{Q}
. In conclusion, for
\mathsf{q}>\bar{\mathsf{q}}_{7}
(which is implied by
\mathsf{q}>\bar{\mathsf{q}}
[cf. (
143g
)]), we have

\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(L_{\mathsf{B}})\subset\mathscr{M}_{\infty}(L_{\mathsf{B}})\subset\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}(L_{\mathsf{B}}),\quad\text{(for $\mathsf{q}>\bar{\mathsf{q}}$)}

(175)

Furthermore, by lemma
13
, every
M\in\mathscr{M}_{\infty}(L_{\mathsf{B}})
is representable over all prime fields
\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}
except for a finite set of primes. Therefore, there exists a prime number

\bar{\mathsf{q}}_{8}\triangleq\max\bigl\{\max\{\mathbb{P}\setminus\chi(M)\}\colon M\in\mathscr{M}_{\infty}(L_{\mathsf{B}})\bigr\}

(176)

such that for
\mathsf{q}>\max\{\bar{\mathsf{q}}_{7},\bar{\mathsf{q}}_{8}\}
(which is implied by
\mathsf{q}>\bar{\mathsf{q}}
[cf. (
143g
)–(
143h
)]) the left-hand inclusion relation in (
175
) holds with equality,
\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(L_{\mathsf{B}})=\mathscr{M}_{\infty}(L_{\mathsf{B}})
. On the other hand, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 24

(Bell
et al
, 2020)
.

Let
M
be an
n
-element matroid representable over a field of characteristic
0
, and let
\mathsf{q}
be a prime satisfying

\log_{2}\log_{2}\log_{2}(\mathsf{q})>n^{5}.

(177)

Then
M
is representable over
\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}
.

A proof is provided in
[
70
]
.

It follows from lemma
24
that for
\mathsf{q}
larger than
\bar{\mathsf{q}}_{9}\triangleq 2^{2^{2^{L_{\mathsf{B}}^{5}}}}
(which is implied by
\mathsf{q}>\bar{\mathsf{q}}
[cf. (
143i
)]), we have
\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}(L_{\mathsf{B}})\subset\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(L_{\mathsf{B}})
. In conclusion, for
\mathsf{q}>\max\{\bar{\mathsf{q}}_{7},\bar{\mathsf{q}}_{8},\bar{\mathsf{q}}_{9}\}
(implied by
\mathsf{q}>\bar{\mathsf{q}}
), we have

\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(L_{\mathsf{B}})=\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}(L_{\mathsf{B}})=\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{Z}}(L_{\mathsf{B}}),\quad\text{(for $\mathsf{q}>\bar{\mathsf{q}}$)}

(178)

(the last equality being Lemma
15
).

Step 5

We will now show that in the minimization problem that defines the quantity
J_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}},M)
[cf. (
171
)], the constraint
\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}})\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(M)
can be replaced by
{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{Z}}(M)
. We will leverage the fact that matrices
{\mathbf{C}}
with a covolume
\det({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}})
exceeding a certain constant, can be removed from the minimization domain defining
J_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}},M)
[as expressed in (
171
)] without loss of generality. Recall that
{\mathbf{C}}
is right-invertible by assumption and that
{\mathbf{C}}_{\perp}
is left-invertible by definition of the orthogonal operation ‘
\perp
’ (see Subsection
IV-C
). Hence, we have the covolume identity
[
53
, Thm. 1]

\det({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}})=\det({\mathbf{C}}_{\perp}^{\mathsf{T}}{\mathbf{C}}_{\perp}).

(179)

Let us denote
\bm{v}={\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}
, which due to
\bm{u}
having bounded support
\P\{\lVert\bm{u}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\tau\}=1
, also has bounded support:

\displaystyle\lVert\bm{v}\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lVert{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}\rVert_{\infty}

(180)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lVert{\mathbf{B}}\rVert_{\infty}\lVert\bm{u}\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lVert{\mathbf{B}}\rVert_{\infty}\tau,\quad\text{(almost surely).}

In other words, this means that
\bm{v}\in\tau\lVert{\mathbf{B}}\rVert_{\infty}\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}}
almost surely. Let
\hat{\tau}=\tau\|{\mathbf{B}}\rVert_{\infty}
stand for the radius (with respect to the infinity norm) of the ball
\tau\lVert{\mathbf{B}}\rVert_{\infty}\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}}
.

As a first step, we argue that the minimization (
171
) can be restricted to only those matrices
{\mathbf{C}}
satisfying
\lambda_{\infty,1}({\mathbf{C}}_{\perp})\leq 2\hat{\tau}
. In fact, the objective
H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y)=H({\mathbf{C}}\bm{v}|Y)
can be expressed by means of

\displaystyle H({\mathbf{C}}\bm{v}|Y=y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}}}\Phi\bigl(\P\{{\mathbf{C}}\bm{v}=\mathbf{t}|Y=y\}\bigr)

(181)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\Lambda^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}}/\Lambda({\mathbf{C}}_{\perp})}\Phi\bigl(\P\{\bm{v}\in\Lambda^{\prime}|Y=y\}\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\Lambda^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}}/\Lambda({\mathbf{C}}_{\perp})}\Phi\bigl(\P\{\bm{v}\in\Lambda^{\prime}\cap\hat{\tau}\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}}|Y=y\}\bigr)

where
A/B
denotes the quotient set of
A
by
B
.
The last equality holds because the support of
\bm{v}
is contained in
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}}
. It becomes apparent that if the shortest vector of
\Lambda({\mathbf{C}}_{\perp})
is larger than the diameter of the cube
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}}
, i.e., if
\lambda_{\infty,1}({\mathbf{C}}_{\perp})>2\hat{\tau}
, then the intersection
\Lambda^{\prime}\cap\hat{\tau}\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}}
is either an empty set or a singleton set. This entails that

\displaystyle H({\mathbf{C}}\bm{v}|Y=y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\Lambda^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}}/\Lambda({\mathbf{C}}_{\perp})}\Phi\bigl(\P\{\bm{v}\in\Lambda^{\prime}\cap\hat{\tau}\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}}|Y=y\}\bigr)

(182)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}}}\Phi\bigl(\P\{\bm{v}=\mathbf{t}|Y=y\}\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H(\bm{v}|Y=y).

By the data-processing inequality
H({\mathbf{C}}\bm{v}|Y=y)\leq H(\bm{v}|Y=y)
, we conclude that, if
\lambda_{\infty,1}({\mathbf{C}}_{\perp})>2\hat{\tau}
, the objective
H({\mathbf{C}}\bm{v}|Y)
of the minimization equals its upper bound
H(\bm{v}|Y)
. Consequently, we can assume that
\lambda_{\infty,1}({\mathbf{C}}_{\perp})\leq 2\hat{\tau}
in the minimization (
171
) without loss of generality. In other words,

\displaystyle J_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}},M)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{C}}\in\llbracket 2\lfloor\kappa(\mathsf{q})\rfloor+1\rrbracket^{L_{\mathsf{C}}\times L_{\mathsf{B}}}\colon\\
\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}})\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(M)\\
\lambda_{\infty,1}({\mathbf{C}}_{\perp})\leq 2\hat{\tau}\end{subarray}}H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y),\quad\textnormal{(for $\mathsf{q}>\bar{\mathsf{q}}$).}

(183)

Next, we show that we can further restrict the minimization domain, in that
\det({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}})
need not be larger than a certain constant. In the special case where
L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}}=1
, the proof is immediate, since the orthogonal
{\mathbf{C}}_{\perp}
reduces to a column vector, so we directly have
\det({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}})=\lVert{\mathbf{C}}_{\perp}\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq L_{\mathsf{B}}\lVert{\mathbf{C}}_{\perp}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}=L_{\mathsf{B}}\lambda_{\infty,1}({\mathbf{C}}_{\perp})\leq 2L_{\mathsf{B}}\hat{\tau}
from the above assumption.
For
L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}}>1
, with a more elaborate reasoning we can still show that
\det({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}})
can be similarly upper-bounded: for the sake of argument, let us assume the
opposite
, namely, that
\det({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}})
is strictly
larger
than some positive constant
\theta
satisfying

\theta\geq(L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}})!(2\hat{\tau})^{L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}}}.

(184)

Since
\det({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}})=\det({\mathbf{C}}_{\perp}^{\mathsf{T}}{\mathbf{C}}_{\perp})
, we immediately have
\det({\mathbf{C}}_{\perp}^{\mathsf{T}}{\mathbf{C}}_{\perp})>\theta
.
Minkowski’s Second Theorem (Subsection
IV-C
, Thm.
12
) applied to successive minima
\lambda_{\infty,\ell}({\mathbf{C}}_{\perp}),\ell=1,\dotsc,L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}}
defined with respect to the convex body
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}}
yields

\prod_{\ell=1}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}}}\lambda_{\infty,\ell}({\mathbf{C}}_{\perp})\geq\frac{1}{(L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}})!}\det({\mathbf{C}}_{\perp}^{\mathsf{T}}{\mathbf{C}}_{\perp})

(185)

and therefore,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\infty,L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}}}({\mathbf{C}}_{\perp})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\prod_{\ell=1}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}}}\lambda_{\infty,\ell}({\mathbf{C}}_{\perp})^{1/(L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}})}

(186)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}})!^{1/(L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}})}}\det({\mathbf{C}}_{\perp}^{\mathsf{T}}{\mathbf{C}}_{\perp})^{1/(L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}})}

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle\frac{\theta^{1/(L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}})}}{(L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}})!^{1/(L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}})}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 2\hat{\tau}.

The above inequality
\lambda_{\infty,L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}}}({\mathbf{C}}_{\perp})>2\hat{\tau}
expresses that the last successive minimum of the lattice
\Lambda({\mathbf{C}}_{\perp})
strictly exceeds the diameter (in terms of infinity norm) of the ball
\hat{\tau}\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}}
, which contains the support of
\bm{v}
. Consequently, as long as
\det({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}})>\theta
(and the inequality
\lambda_{\infty,L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}}}({\mathbf{C}}_{\perp})>2\hat{\tau}
thus being satisfied), it is always guaranteed that there exists a largest index
\ell_{0}\in[L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}}-1]
such that all those successive minima of
\Lambda({\mathbf{C}}_{\perp})
with index up to
\ell_{0}
, do not exceed
2\hat{\tau}
(while those with index
\ell_{0}+1
or higher exceed
2\hat{\tau}
). That is,

\lambda_{\infty,1}({\mathbf{C}}_{\perp})\leq\dotso\leq\lambda_{\infty,\ell_{0}}({\mathbf{C}}_{\perp})\leq 2\hat{\tau}<\lambda_{\infty,\ell_{0}+1}({\mathbf{C}}_{\perp})\leq\dotso\leq\lambda_{\infty,L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}}}({\mathbf{C}}_{\perp}).

(187)

Recall from (
183
) that we have assumed that
{\mathbf{C}}
always satisfies
\lambda_{\infty,1}({\mathbf{C}}_{\perp})\leq 2\hat{\tau}
, and hence not all successive minima can simultaneously exceed
2\hat{\tau}
. Also note that the
\ell_{0}
first columns of the Korkin–Zolotarev reduced basis matrix
{\mathbf{C}}_{\perp}
form themselves a Korkin–Zolotarev reduced basis
[{\mathbf{C}}_{\perp}]_{[\ell_{0}]}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}\times\ell_{0}}
. Let us define the orthogonal
\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{(L_{\mathsf{B}}-\ell_{0})\times L_{\mathsf{B}}}
of the latter, i.e.,

\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}\triangleq\bigl([{\mathbf{C}}_{\perp}]_{[\ell_{0}]}\bigr)_{\perp}

(188)

and note that the row space of
\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}
contains the row space of
{\mathbf{C}}
, i.e.,
\Lambda({\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}})\subset\Lambda(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}^{\mathsf{T}})
.

Let us now define a partitioned integer matrix

{\mathbf{Z}}({\mathbf{X}},{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})\triangleq\begin{bmatrix}{\mathbf{X}}&{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}

(189)

of same dimension as
{\mathbf{C}}_{\perp}
, and which we assume to be in Korkin–Zolotarev reduced form. Its left-hand part
{\mathbf{X}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}\times\ell_{0}}
contains the first
\ell_{0}
columns of
{\mathbf{Z}}({\mathbf{X}},{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})
, whereas the right-hand part
{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}\times(L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}}-\ell_{0})}
contains the remaining columns.

Let us now fix the left-hand matrix to
{\mathbf{C}}_{\perp}
, so as to make
{\mathbf{Z}}({\mathbf{X}},{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})
and
{\mathbf{C}}_{\perp}
coincide on the first
\ell_{0}
columns. Consider
\mathscr{X}(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp},\hat{\tau})
, which we shall define as the set of all matrices
{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}\times(L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}}-\ell_{0})}
such that the following three properties are simultaneously satisfied:

1.

{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp},{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})
is in Korkin–Zolotarev reduced form;

2.

the largest index
\ell
such that
\lambda_{\infty,\ell}\bigl({\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp},{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})\bigr)\leq 2\hat{\tau}
, is equal to
\ell_{0}
;

3.

{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp},{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})_{\perp}
is a representation of
M({\mathbf{C}})
, or equivalently,
{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp},{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})^{\mathsf{T}}
is a representation of
M^{*}({\mathbf{C}})=M({\mathbf{C}}_{\perp}^{\mathsf{T}})
.

Since plugging
{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}=[{\mathbf{C}}_{\perp}]_{[L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}}]\setminus[\ell_{0}]}
into
{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp},\cdot)
obviously reconstitutes the original matrix
{\mathbf{C}}_{\perp}
, i.e.,

{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp},[{\mathbf{C}}_{\perp}]_{[L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}}]\setminus[\ell_{0}]})={\mathbf{Z}}([{\mathbf{C}}_{\perp}]_{[\ell_{0}]},[{\mathbf{C}}_{\perp}]_{[L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}}]\setminus[\ell_{0}]})={\mathbf{C}}_{\perp}

(190)

and since this choice of
{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}
will by definition satisfy the above three conditions, it is clear that the set
\mathscr{X}(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp},\hat{\tau})
always has at least one element.

Having fixed the left-hand part of
{\mathbf{Z}}({\mathbf{X}},{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})
to be
{\mathbf{X}}=\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp}
, let us view its orthogonal
{\mathbf{Z}}({\mathbf{X}},{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})_{\perp}
as a function of the right-hand matrix
{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{X}(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp},\hat{\tau})
and accordingly denote it as

{\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})\triangleq{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp},{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})_{\perp}.

(191)

Note that since
\mathscr{X}(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp},\hat{\tau})
is non-empty and since
{\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})
is an integer matrix (so the determinant
\det\bigl({\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}){\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})^{\mathsf{T}}\bigr)
is a non-negative integer), the minimum

\mu({\mathbf{C}},\hat{\tau})\triangleq\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{X}(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp},\hat{\tau})\colon\\
M({\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}))=M({\mathbf{C}})\end{subarray}}\det\bigl({\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}){\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})^{\mathsf{T}}\bigr)

(192)

is well defined and achieved by a (not necessarily unique) minimizing matrix
{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{\star}(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}})\in\mathscr{X}(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp},\hat{\tau})
, i.e.,

\mu(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp},\hat{\tau})=\det\bigl({\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{\star}(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp})){\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{\star}(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp}))^{\mathsf{T}}\bigr).

(193)

We further define the following constant:

\theta^{\star}(\hat{\tau})\triangleq\max_{1\leq\ell_{0}\leq L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}\times\ell_{0}}\colon\\
\lambda_{\infty,\ell_{0}}(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp})\leq 2\hat{\tau}\end{subarray}}\mu(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp},\hat{\tau}).

(194)

This constant is well defined, because the supremum is over a finite set (and can thus be replaced by a maximum). To see this, bear in mind that the constraint
\lambda_{\infty,\ell_{0}}(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp})\leq 2\hat{\tau}
implies that all successive minima of
\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp}
are upper-bounded by
2\hat{\tau}
. By virtue of (
43
), the norms of the columns of
\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp}
are upper-bounded as

\displaystyle\left\lVert[\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp}]_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{i+3}{4}\lambda_{2,i}(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp})^{2}

(195)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{i+3}{4}L_{\mathsf{B}}\lambda_{\infty,i}(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(i+3)L_{\mathsf{B}}\hat{\tau}^{2}\quad\textnormal{(for $1\leq i\leq\ell_{0}$)}

whence we conclude, for example, that the Frobenius norm of
\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp}
is upper-bounded as

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\ell_{0}}(i+3)L_{\mathsf{B}}\hat{\tau}^{2}

(196)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\ell_{0}+1}{2}+3\right)\ell_{0}L_{\mathsf{B}}\hat{\tau}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\frac{L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}}}{2}+3\right)\max\{1,L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}}-1\}L_{\mathsf{B}}\hat{\tau}^{2}.

where the last step follows because
1\leq\ell_{0}\leq L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}}-1
.

Next, we will argue that the entropy
H({\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})\bm{v}|Y)
, when viewed as a function of
{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{X}(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp},\hat{\tau})
, is constant.
To begin with, note that this entropy can be written as

H({\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})\bm{v}|Y)=\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\sum_{\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}}}\Phi\bigl(\P\{{\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})\bm{v}=\mathbf{t}|Y=y\}\bigr){\,\operatorname{d}}P_{Y}(y)

(197)

and since
{\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})
is right-invertible, the (unconstrained) linear equation
{\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})\bm{v}=\mathbf{t}
(in
\bm{v}
) has a translated lattice
\mathscr{V}({\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}),\mathbf{t})
as its solution set, which can be parametrized as

\displaystyle\mathscr{V}({\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}),\mathbf{t})

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\bigl\{{\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})^{\sharp}\mathbf{t}+{\mathbf{Z}}({\mathbf{X}})\mathbf{w}\colon\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}}}\bigr\}

(198)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})^{\sharp}\mathbf{t}+\Lambda({\mathbf{Z}}({\mathbf{X}})).

Note that
{\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})=({\mathbf{Z}}({\mathbf{X}}))_{\perp}
has been defined via the
(\cdot)_{\perp}
operation, which is why a right-inverse
{\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})^{\sharp}
exists.
In the above equation, the translation vector
{\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})^{\sharp}\mathbf{t}
can be replaced, without loss of generality, by a smallest-norm equivalent shift vector

\mathbf{d}({\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}),\mathbf{t})\triangleq{\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})^{\sharp}\mathbf{t}+{\mathbf{Z}}({\mathbf{X}})\mathbf{w}_{0}

(199)

where
\mathbf{w}_{0}
stands for

\mathbf{w}_{0}=\argmin_{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}}}}\big\lVert{\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})^{\sharp}\mathbf{t}+{\mathbf{Z}}({\mathbf{X}})\mathbf{w}\big\rVert_{\infty}.

(200)

(ties being resolved arbitrarily).
Hence,
\mathscr{V}({\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}),\mathbf{t})=\mathbf{d}({\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}),\mathbf{t})+\Lambda({\mathbf{Z}}({\mathbf{X}}))
. Furthermore, notice that for any shift vector
\mathbf{d}\in\hat{\tau}\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}}
and any
{\mathbf{X}}\in\mathscr{X}(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp},\hat{\tau})
, we have that, under the intersection with the scaled ball
\hat{\tau}\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}}
, the shifted lattice
\mathbf{d}+\Lambda({\mathbf{Z}}({\mathbf{X}}))
is the same as the shifted sublattice
\mathbf{d}+\Lambda([{\mathbf{C}}_{\perp}]_{[\ell_{0}-1]})=\mathbf{d}+\Lambda(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp})
, namely,

\displaystyle\mathscr{V}({\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}),\mathbf{t})\cap\hat{\tau}\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathbf{d}({\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}),\mathbf{t})+\Lambda({\mathbf{Z}}({\mathbf{X}}))\bigr)\cap\hat{\tau}\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathbf{d}({\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}),\mathbf{t})+\Lambda(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp})\bigr)\cap\hat{\tau}\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}}.

Note that for any pair
(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w})
such that
{\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})^{\sharp}\mathbf{t}+{\mathbf{Z}}({\mathbf{X}})\mathbf{w}
belongs to the cube
\hat{\tau}\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}}
, we have that the last
L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}}-\ell_{0}+1
entries of
\mathbf{w}_{0}=\begin{bmatrix}\bar{\mathbf{w}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}&\mathbf{0}^{\mathsf{T}}\end{bmatrix}^{\mathsf{T}}
are zeros, in such way that
{\mathbf{Z}}({\mathbf{X}})\mathbf{w}_{0}=\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{w}}_{0}
. On the other hand, since
\begin{bmatrix}\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}^{\sharp}&\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp}\end{bmatrix}
is unimodular, there exists a vector
\bar{\mathbf{t}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}-\ell_{0}+1}
such that
{\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})^{\sharp}\mathbf{t}+{\mathbf{Z}}({\mathbf{X}})\mathbf{w}_{0}=\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}^{\sharp}\bar{\mathbf{t}}+\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{w}}_{0}
. Hence, if we define the set

\mathscr{D}\triangleq\{\mathbf{d}({\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}),\mathbf{t})\colon\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}}\}\cap\hat{\tau}\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}}

(201)

then this set is equal to

\mathscr{D}=\{\mathbf{d}(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}},\mathbf{t})\colon\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}}\}\cap\hat{\tau}\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}}

(202)

and thus is not a function of
{\mathbf{X}}
.
In the derivation (
203
) underneath, let us adopt the convention that the probability that a random variable belongs to an empty set shall be equal to zero. With the notations defined above, the entropy term
H({\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})\bm{v}|Y)
can now be rewritten by means of

\displaystyle H({\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})\bm{v}|Y=y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}}}\Phi\bigl(\P\{{\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})\bm{v}=\mathbf{t}|Y=y\}\bigr)

(203)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}}}\Phi\bigl(\P\{\bm{v}\in\mathscr{V}({\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}),\mathbf{t})\cap\hat{\tau}\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}}|Y=y\}\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{d}\in\mathscr{D}}\Phi\bigl(\P\{\bm{v}\in(\mathbf{d}+\Lambda(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp}))\cap\hat{\tau}\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}}|Y=y\}\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}}}\Phi\bigl(\P\{\bm{v}\in(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}^{\sharp}\mathbf{t}+\Lambda(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp}))\cap\hat{\tau}\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}}|Y=y\}\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}\bm{v}|Y=y)

whereby we infer that
H({\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\prime})\bm{v}|Y)
is constant in
{\mathbf{X}}\in\mathscr{X}(\bar{{\mathbf{C}}}_{\perp},\hat{\tau})
.

In summary, we have shown that if the (right-invertible) matrix
{\mathbf{C}}
is large in the precise sense that
\det({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}})>\theta
with some integer
\theta\geq\bar{\theta}(\hat{\tau})
with

\bar{\theta}(\hat{\tau})\triangleq\max\bigl\{(L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}})!(2\hat{\tau})^{L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}}},\theta^{\star}(\hat{\tau})\bigr\}

(204)

then one can construct a matrix
{\mathbf{C}}^{\star}\triangleq\tilde{{\mathbf{C}}}({\mathbf{X}}^{\star})
with same matroid
M({\mathbf{C}})=M({\mathbf{C}}^{\star})
which is “smaller” than
{\mathbf{C}}
in the sense that
\det({\mathbf{C}}^{\star}({\mathbf{C}}^{\star})^{\mathsf{T}})\leq\min\{\det({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}}),\theta^{\star}(\hat{\tau})\}
and such that
H({\mathbf{C}}\bm{v}|Y)=H({\mathbf{C}}^{\star}\bm{v}|Y)
. As a consequence, we can restrict the minimization domain on which
{\mathbf{C}}
takes value, namely,

\displaystyle J_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}},M)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{C}}\in\llbracket 2\lfloor\kappa(\mathsf{q})\rfloor+1\rrbracket^{L_{\mathsf{C}}\times L_{\mathsf{B}}}\colon\\
\text{$\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}})\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(M)$ and $\det({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}})\leq\theta$}\end{subarray}}H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y),\quad\textnormal{(for $\mathsf{q}>\bar{\mathsf{q}}$, $\theta\geq\bar{\theta}(\hat{\tau})$).}

(205)

Now
\det({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}})
can be lower-bounded as follows:

\displaystyle\det({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\prod_{\ell=1}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}}\lambda_{\infty,\ell}({\mathbf{C}})

(206)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\max_{\ell}\lambda_{\infty,\ell}({\mathbf{C}})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{L_{\mathsf{B}}}}\max_{\ell}\lambda_{2,\ell}({\mathbf{C}})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\max_{\ell}\frac{2}{\sqrt{L_{\mathsf{B}}(\ell+3)}}\bigl\lVert[{\mathbf{C}}]_{\ell}\bigr\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\sqrt{L_{\mathsf{B}}(L_{\mathsf{C}}+3)}}\max_{\ell}\bigl\lVert[{\mathbf{C}}]_{\ell}\bigr\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\sqrt{L_{\mathsf{B}}(L_{\mathsf{C}}+3)}}\lVert{\mathbf{C}}\rVert_{\infty}.

Hence
\det({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}})\leq\theta
implies
\lVert{\mathbf{C}}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\Theta
where
\Theta=\frac{\theta}{2}\sqrt{L_{\mathsf{B}}(L_{\mathsf{C}}+3)}
. We can thus replace
2\lfloor\kappa(\mathsf{q})\rfloor+1
in the minimization domain from (
205
) by
2\min\{\Theta,\lfloor\kappa(\mathsf{q})\rfloor\}+1
. Hence, if
\mathsf{q}
is so large as to satisfy
\kappa(\mathsf{q})\geq\Theta
, then we can further modify the expression of
J_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}},M)
to the following:

J_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}},M)=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{C}}\in\llbracket 2\Theta+1\rrbracket^{L_{\mathsf{C}}\times L_{\mathsf{B}}}\colon\\
\text{$\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}})\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(M)$ and $\det({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}})\leq\Theta$}\end{subarray}}H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y),\qquad\textnormal{(for $\mathsf{q}>\max\{\bar{\mathsf{q}},\Theta^{4/3}\}$, $\Theta\geq\bar{\theta}(\hat{\tau})$).}

(207)

Here, we have used that
\mathsf{q}\geq\Theta^{4/3}
is a sufficient condition for
\kappa(\mathsf{q})\geq\Theta
. In fact, since
L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}\geq 2
, we have
\Theta^{4/3}\geq\Theta^{\frac{2L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}}{2L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}-1}}
, hence
\mathsf{q}\geq\Theta^{4/3}
implies
\mathsf{q}\geq\Theta^{\frac{2L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}}{2L_{\mathsf{B}}L_{\mathsf{C}}-1}}
, the latter being equivalent to
\kappa(\mathsf{q})\geq\Theta
.

On the other hand, if
\mathsf{q}>\sqrt{\Theta}
, then any square submatrix
[{\mathbf{C}}]_{\mathcal{L}}
of a matrix
{\mathbf{C}}
from the minimization domain in (
207
) has a determinant whose absolute value is strictly less than
\mathsf{q}
, because

\det([{\mathbf{C}}]_{\mathcal{L}})^{2}=\det([{\mathbf{C}}]_{\mathcal{L}}[{\mathbf{C}}]_{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathsf{T}})\leq\det({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}})\leq\Theta<\mathsf{q}^{2}.

(208)

Now, recall that the (finite-field) submatrix
\phi_{\mathsf{q}}([{\mathbf{C}}]_{\mathcal{L}})
is rank deficient if and only if
\det([{\mathbf{C}}]_{\mathcal{L}})
is a multiple of
\mathsf{q}
. However, under the assumption
\mathsf{q}>\sqrt{\Theta}
, the determinant
\det([{\mathbf{C}}]_{\mathcal{L}})
can be a multiple of
\mathsf{q}
only if
\det([{\mathbf{C}}]_{\mathcal{L}})=0
. Therefore, if
\mathsf{q}>\sqrt{\Theta}
, we have that
\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{C}})\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{q}}}(M)
if and only if
{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{Z}}(M)
. However,
\mathsf{q}>\sqrt{\Theta}
is already implied by
\mathsf{q}>\Theta^{4/3}
because
\theta^{\star}(\hat{\tau})\geq 1
.
As a consequence, we can write

J_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}},M)=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{C}}\in\llbracket 2\Theta+1\rrbracket^{L_{\mathsf{C}}\times L_{\mathsf{B}}}\colon\\
\text{${\mathbf{C}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{Z}}(M)$ and $\det({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}})\leq\Theta$}\end{subarray}}H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y),\qquad\textnormal{(for $\mathsf{q}>\max\{\bar{\mathsf{q}},\Theta^{4/3}\}$, $\Theta\geq\theta^{\star}(\hat{\tau})$).}

(209)

Recall that, on the right-hand side of (
209
),
\Theta
stands for an arbitrary parameter that can be set to any integer not smaller than
\bar{\theta}(\hat{\tau})
. Setting it equal to
\bar{\theta}(\hat{\tau})
, and observing that the right-hand side of (
209
) is not a function of
\mathsf{q}
, we infer that
J_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}},M)
is constant in
\mathsf{q}
for any prime
\mathsf{q}
larger than
\max\{\bar{\mathsf{q}},\bar{\theta}(\hat{\tau})^{4/3}\}=\bar{\mathsf{q}}
[cf. (
143j
)]. That is,

J_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}},M)=J_{\bar{\mathsf{q}}}({\mathbf{B}},M)=\lim_{\mathsf{q}\to\infty}J_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}},M),\quad\textnormal{(for $\mathsf{q}\geq\bar{\mathsf{q}}$).}

(210)

Furthermore, this limit is equal to

J_{\bar{\mathsf{q}}}({\mathbf{B}},M)=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{C}}\in\llbracket 2\Theta+1\rrbracket^{L_{\mathsf{C}}\times L_{\mathsf{B}}}\colon\\
\text{${\mathbf{C}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{Z}}(M)$ and $\det({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}})\leq\Theta$}\end{subarray}}H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y),\quad\textnormal{(for $\Theta\geq\theta^{\star}(\hat{\tau})$).}

(211)

Recall that
\Theta>\theta^{\star}(\hat{\tau})
is an arbitrary parameter. Hence, since the left-hand side of (
211
) does not depend on
\Theta
, neither does the right-hand side. We can thus let
\Theta
tend to infinity to obtain the simpler expression
23
23

23

Since the right-hand side of (
211
) is constant in
\theta
, we know that the infimum in (
212
) is in fact achieved by a minimizing matrix
{\mathbf{C}}
, and is therefore a minimum.

J_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}},M)=\inf_{{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{Z}}(M)}H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y),\quad\textnormal{(for $\mathsf{q}\geq\bar{\mathsf{q}}$).}

(212)

This concludes Step 5.

By combining Steps 1 through 5, we conclude that for any prime
\mathsf{q}>\bar{\mathsf{q}}
, the equality (
138
) can be expressed as

\displaystyle\mathscr{Q}(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}}))

\displaystyle\overset{(\mathsf{q}>\bar{\mathsf{q}})}{=}

\displaystyle\bigcap_{M^{*}\in\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\circ}(L_{\mathsf{B}})}\;\bigcup_{\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{B}(M^{*})}\;\bigcap_{\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{B}([{\mathbf{B}}]_{\mathcal{S}})}\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T})

(213)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}}).

This concludes the proof of Theorem
2
for finitely supported auxiliaries.

Next, as a final proof step, we will lift this finite-support restriction by a “clipping” argument. Let us define the clipping operator
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\tau}
for real vectors
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
as follows:

\langle\mathbf{u}\rangle_{\tau}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{u}&\textnormal{if $\lVert\mathbf{u}\rVert\leq\tau$}\\
\mynull&\textnormal{if $\lVert\mathbf{u}\rVert>\tau$.}\end{cases}

(214)

In the following, let us incorporate the dependency on the auxiliary distribution into the rate region notations, by writing
\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}};\bm{u})
and
\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T};\bm{u})
in lieu of
\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}})
and
\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T})
, respectively. By Theorem
2
, which thus far we have proven to hold for finitely supported auxiliaries, we can assert that
\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}};\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau})
is achievable. Therefore, the inner set limit
\varliminf_{\tau\to\infty}\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}};\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau})
is achievable. Upon writing out
\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}};\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau})
according to its definition (
54
) as

\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}};\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau})=\bigcap_{M\in\mathscr{M}^{\circ}_{\mathbb{Z}}(L_{\mathsf{B}})}\bigcup_{\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{B}(M^{*})}\bigcap_{\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{B}([{\mathbf{B}}]_{\mathcal{S}})}\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T};\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau})

(215)

and observing that this expression involves a finite number of set operations that are independent of
\tau
, we can argue that the inner set limit can be exchanged with the other set operations, so as to yield

\varliminf_{\tau\to\infty}\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}};\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau})=\bigcap_{M\in\mathscr{M}^{\circ}_{\mathbb{Z}}(L_{\mathsf{B}})}\bigcup_{\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{B}(M^{*})}\bigcap_{\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{B}([{\mathbf{B}}]_{\mathcal{S}})}\varliminf_{\tau\to\infty}\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T};\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}).

(216)

The set on the right-hand side of the last equation can in turn be expressed as

\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T};\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau})=\Bigl\{(R_{1},\dotsc,R_{K})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{K}\colon\sum_{k\in\mathcal{T}}R_{k}<\\
H([\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}]_{\mathcal{T}})+\inf_{{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{Z}}(M)}H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}|Y_{\tau})-H({\mathbf{B}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}|Y_{\tau})\Bigr\}

(217)

where
Y_{\tau}
denotes the channel output induced by an auxiliary
\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}
. Specifically, conditioned on any given
\bm{u}=\mathbf{u}
and given a modulation mapping
\mathbf{x}(\mathbf{u})=(x_{1}(u_{1}),\ldots,x_{K}(u_{K}))
, the variable
Y_{\tau}
is distributed as
Y_{\tau}\sim P_{Y|\bm{x}}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}(\langle\mathbf{u}\rangle_{\tau}))
.

Lemma 25

(Clipping lemma)
.

Given some real matrix
{\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
, an integer random vector
\bm{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
with finite entropy
H(\bm{u})
and any modulation mapping
\mathbf{x}(\mathbf{u})
, we have that, as
\tau\to\infty
, the entropy
H({\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}|Y_{\tau})
converges to
H({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}|Y)
uniformly in
{\mathbf{Q}}
, in the sense that

\bigl|H({\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}|Y_{\tau})-H({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}|Y)\bigr|\leq\delta(\tau)

(218)

for all
{\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
, where the function
\delta(\tau)
only depends on the distribution of
(\bm{u},Y)
(but not on
{\mathbf{Q}}
) and satisfies
\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\delta(\tau)=0
.

Proof:

Let us define the binary random variable

E_{\tau}=\mathds{1}\{\lVert\bm{u}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\tau\}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $\lVert\bm{u}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\tau$}\\
0&\text{if $\lVert\bm{u}\rVert_{\infty}>\tau$}.\end{cases}

(219)

The conditional mutual informations
I({\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau};E_{\tau}|Y_{\tau})
and
I({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u};E_{\tau}|Y)
can be expanded in two alternative ways, respectively. Namely,

\displaystyle H({\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}|Y_{\tau})-H({\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}|E_{\tau},Y_{\tau})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H(E_{\tau}|Y_{\tau})-H(E_{\tau}|{\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau},Y_{\tau})

(220a)

\displaystyle H({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}|Y)-H({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}|E_{\tau},Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H(E_{\tau}|Y)-H(E_{\tau}|{\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u},Y).

(220b)

Since the right-hand sides of (
220a
) and (
220b
) are each non-negative and upper-bounded by
H(E_{\tau})
, upon taking the difference between (
220a
) and (
220b
), we obtain the bound

\bigl|H({\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}|Y_{\tau})-H({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}|Y)\bigr|\leq H(E_{\tau})+\bigl|H({\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}|E_{\tau},Y_{\tau})-H({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}|E_{\tau},Y)\bigr|.

(221)

The conditional entropies
H({\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}|E_{\tau},Y_{\tau})
and
H({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}|E_{\tau},Y)
can be expressed as

\displaystyle H({\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}|E_{\tau},Y_{\tau})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H({\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}|E_{\tau}=0,Y_{\tau})\P\{E_{\tau}=0\}+{}\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad

+ H(
Q
⟨
u
⟩
_
τ
— E_
τ
= 1, Y_
τ
)

P

{ E_
τ
= 1 }

(222a)

\displaystyle H({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}|E_{\tau},Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}|E_{\tau}=0,Y)\P\{E_{\tau}=0\}+{}

+ H(
Qu
— E_
τ
= 1, Y)

P

{ E_
τ
= 1 }.

(222b)

Since
E_{\tau}=1
implies
\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}=\bm{u}
, it follows that, conditioned on
E_{\tau}=1
, the pair
(\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau},Y_{\tau})
has the same distribution as
(\bm{u},Y)
, hence
H({\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}|E_{\tau}=1,Y_{\tau})=H({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}|E_{\tau}=1,Y)
. On the other hand, conditioned on
E_{\tau}=0
, the clipped variable
\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}
is deterministically equal to the zero vector, hence
H({\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}|E_{\tau}=0,Y_{\tau})=0
. Subtracting (
222a
) from (
222b
), we thus get

\displaystyle H({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}|E_{\tau},Y)-H({\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}|E_{\tau},Y_{\tau})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}|E_{\tau}=0,Y)\P\{E_{\tau}=0\}.

The non-negativity of the right-hand side of the last equality reveals that the difference of entropies on the left-hand side is non-negative. Hence the absolute value of said difference can be bounded as

\displaystyle\bigl|H({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}|E_{\tau},Y)-H({\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}|E_{\tau},Y_{\tau})\bigr|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}|E_{\tau}=0,Y)\P\{E_{\tau}=0\}

(223)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle H(\bm{u}|E_{\tau}=0)\P\{E_{\tau}=0\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H(\bm{u}|E_{\tau})-H(\bm{u}|E_{\tau}=1)\P\{E_{\tau}=1\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle H(\bm{u})-H(\bm{u}|E_{\tau}=1)\P\{E_{\tau}=1\}.

Next, note that

\displaystyle H(\bm{u}|E_{\tau}=1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{K}\colon\\
\lVert\mathbf{u}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\tau\end{subarray}}\frac{\P\{\bm{u}=\mathbf{u}\}}{\P\{E_{\tau}=1\}}\log\frac{\P\{\bm{u}=\mathbf{u}\}}{\P\{E_{\tau}=1\}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log\P\{E_{\tau}=1\}-\frac{1}{\P\{E_{\tau}=1\}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{K}\colon\\
\lVert\mathbf{u}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\tau\end{subarray}}\P\{\bm{u}=\mathbf{u}\}\log\P\{\bm{u}=\mathbf{u}\}

from which, since
\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\P\{\lVert\bm{u}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\tau\}=\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\P\{E_{\tau}=1\}=1
, it becomes manifest that
\lim_{\tau\to\infty}H(\bm{u}|E_{\tau}=1)\P\{E_{\tau}=1\}=H(\bm{u})
. Hence, taking limits as
\tau\to\infty
we get

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\bigl\{H({\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}|E_{\tau},Y_{\tau})-H({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}|E_{\tau},Y)\bigr\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

which concludes the proof.
In addition, it is easy to see that this convergence is uniform by combining (
221
) and (
223
), which yields an upper bound
\bigl|H({\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}|E_{\tau},Y_{\tau})-H({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}|E_{\tau},Y)\bigr|\leq\delta(\tau)
which only depends on the distribution of
\bm{u}
, but not on
{\mathbf{Q}}
.

∎

By Lemma
25
, we have the limits

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to\infty}H([\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}]_{\mathcal{T}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H([\bm{u}]_{\mathcal{T}})

(224a)

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to\infty}H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}|Y_{\tau})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y)

(224b)

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to\infty}H({\mathbf{B}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}|Y_{\tau})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H({\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y)

(224c)

all of which are finite because
H(\bm{u})
, which upper bounds all three of them, is finite by assumption. Here, establishing the last two equalities (
224b
)–(
224c
) additionally requires the Dominated Convergence Theorem
[
71
, Theorem 1.6.9]
, which is applicable due to
H(\bm{u}|Y=y)
being an upper bound both on
H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}|Y=y)
and on
H({\mathbf{B}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}|Y=y)
, and this upper bound is integrable with respect to the distribution of
Y
, since
H(\bm{u}|Y)\leq H(\bm{u})<\infty
by assumption.
As regards the infimum term, by Lemma
25
we know that
H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}|Y)
converges to
H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y)
uniformly in
{\mathbf{C}}
, as
\tau\to\infty
. It follows that the order of limit-taking and infimization can be exchanged, yielding

\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\inf_{{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{Z}}(M)}H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}|Y_{\tau})=\inf_{{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{Z}}(M)}H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y).

(225)

Furthermore, by Lemma
3
we can revert all entropies back to the algebraic entropy notation:

\displaystyle H([\bm{u}]_{\mathcal{T}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}([\bm{u}]_{\mathcal{T}})

(226a)

\displaystyle H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}}(\bm{u}|Y)

(226b)

\displaystyle H({\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{B}}}(\bm{u}|Y).

(226c)

Combining (
224a
), (
224c
) and (
225
), and using algebraic entropy notation (
226
), we conclude that

\varliminf_{\tau\to\infty}\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T};\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau})=\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T};\bm{u})

(227)

and therefore, substituting this back into (
216
), we finally end up with

\varliminf_{\tau\to\infty}\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}};\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau})=\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}};\bm{u}).

(228)

This finalizes the proof of Theorem
2
.
24
24

24

Note that actually, inner and outer set limits in
\tau\to\infty
coincide, so we could as well strengthen the convergence claim (
228
) to
\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}};\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau})=\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}};\bm{u})
.

Appendix L
Proof of Theorem
3

We fix the coefficient matrix
{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L\times K}
, the channel law
P_{Y|X_{1},\dotsc,X_{K}}=P_{Y|\bm{X}}
(satisfying the technical conditions in the statement of Theorem
3
), modulation mappings
\mathbf{x}(\mathbf{u}):=(x_{1}(u_{1}),\dotsc,x_{K}(u_{K}))
and the distribution of an auxiliary vector
\bm{u}=\bigl[U_{1},\dotsc,U_{K}\bigr]^{\mathsf{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
with mutually independent entries and an absolutely continuous distribution (with respect to the Lebesgue measure).

In what follows, let
\nu>0
stand for a quantization resolution parameter. We will now append a subscript ‘
\nu
’ to the notations of rate regions and quantities defined in (
52
), (
53
), (
54
) and Definition
18
, so as to signify that these are generated by an integer auxiliary
\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\in\mathbb{Z}^{K}
and modulation mapping
\mathbf{u}\mapsto\mathbf{x}(\tfrac{1}{\nu}\mathbf{u})
. Specifically, we will write these as
\mathscr{R}_{\nu}({\mathbf{A}})
,
\mathscr{Q}_{\nu}({\mathbf{B}})
,
\mathscr{Q}_{\nu}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T})
and
J_{\nu}({\mathbf{B}},M)
, all of which being evaluated as per Theorem
2
.

Each member of the sequence of rate regions
\left\{\mathscr{R}_{\nu}({\mathbf{A}})\right\}_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}}
is achievable as per Theorem
2
. The proof of Theorem
3
consists in showing that this sequence has an inner limit, as
\nu
tends to infinity, that contains the rate region
\mathscr{R}({\mathbf{A}})
(evaluated as per Theorem
3
) with the continuous auxiliary
\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
and modulation mapping
\mathbf{x}(\bm{u})
. That is, we will show that

\mathscr{R}({\mathbf{A}})\subseteq\varliminf_{\nu\to\infty}\mathscr{R}_{\nu}({\mathbf{A}}).

(229)

The channel output variable induced by the continuous auxiliary
\bm{u}
and modulation mapping
\bm{u}\mapsto\mathbf{x}(\bm{u})
shall be denoted as
Y
, in accordance with the system model from Section
III
. By contrast, the channel output induced by the
quantized
auxiliary
\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor
and modulation mapping
\mathbf{u}\mapsto\mathbf{x}(\tfrac{1}{\nu}\mathbf{u})
shall be denoted as
Y_{\nu}
. This means that the joint distributions of
(\bm{u},Y)
and
(\bm{u},Y_{\nu})
are specified as follows: conditioned on
\bm{u}=\mathbf{u}
, we have that
Y
and
Y_{\nu}
are distributed respectively as

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle\sim P_{Y|\bm{X}}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}(\mathbf{u}))

\displaystyle Y_{\nu}

\displaystyle\sim P_{Y|\bm{X}}\left(\cdot\middle|\mathbf{x}(\tfrac{1}{\nu}\lfloor\nu\mathbf{u}\rfloor)\right)

(230)

Thus far, we have merely fixed the conditional distributions
P_{Y|\bm{u}=\mathbf{u}}
and
P_{Y_{\nu}|\bm{u}=\mathbf{u}}
and thus, together with the given distribution of
\bm{u}\sim P_{\bm{u}}
, we have determined the joint distributions of the pairs
(\bm{u},Y)
and
(\bm{u},Y_{\nu})
, respectively. But we have
not
specified a joint distribution on the triple
(\bm{u},Y,Y_{\nu})
. Since all informational quantities (entropies and mutual information) involved in the present proof only depend on either
(\bm{u},Y)
or
(\bm{u},Y_{\nu})
, we remain free to choose an arbitrary coupling between
Y
and
Y_{\nu}
as long as the marginals are given by the distributions of
(\bm{u},Y)
and
(\bm{u},Y_{\nu})
specified above. This degree of freedom will be exploited in the proof of Lemma
26
stated further below.

The proof follows a similar two-stage outline as that of Theorem
2
: we initially prove the theorem under the assumption of a compactly supported auxiliary, to then relax this assumption in a second and final step. To start off, let us state three auxiliary lemmata that will be needed at a later stage.

Since
\mathscr{R}_{\nu}({\mathbf{A}})=\bigcup_{\mathbf{B}}\mathscr{Q}_{\nu}({\mathbf{B}})
where the union runs over
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}({\mathbf{B}})\supseteq\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}({\mathbf{A}})
, and due to (
12
), in order to prove (
229
) it will suffice to prove

\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}})\subseteq\varliminf_{\nu\to\infty}\mathscr{Q}_{\nu}({\mathbf{B}}).

(231)

Recall that
\mathscr{Q}_{\nu}({\mathbf{B}})
is given by [cf. (
54
)]

\displaystyle\mathscr{Q}_{\nu}({\mathbf{B}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigcap_{M\in\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\circ}(L_{\mathsf{B}})}\;\bigcup_{\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{B}(M^{*})}\;\bigcap_{\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{B}([{\mathbf{B}}]_{\mathcal{S}})}\mathscr{Q}_{\nu}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T}).

(232)

Since, on the right-hand side of (
232
), the triple
(M,\mathcal{S},\mathcal{T})
is iterated over a finite set that does not depend on
\nu
, it follows that the order of set limit (as
\nu\to\infty
) and the set operations (unions and intersections) can be exchanged, leading to

\displaystyle\varliminf_{\nu\to\infty}\mathscr{Q}_{\nu}({\mathbf{B}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigcap_{M\in\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\circ}(L_{\mathsf{B}})}\;\bigcup_{\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{B}(M^{*})}\;\bigcap_{\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{B}([{\mathbf{B}}]_{\mathcal{S}})}\varliminf_{\nu\to\infty}\mathscr{Q}_{\nu}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T})

(233)

where
\mathscr{Q}_{\nu}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T})
is given by [cf. (
52
)]

\mathscr{Q}_{\nu}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T})=\Bigl\{(R_{1},\dotsc,R_{K})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{K}\colon\sum_{k\in\mathcal{T}}R_{k}<H([\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor]_{\mathcal{T}})-H({\mathbf{B}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y_{\nu})+J_{\nu}({\mathbf{B}},M)\Bigr\}

(234)

and where [cf. (
53
)]

J_{\nu}({\mathbf{B}},M)=\inf_{{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{Z}}(M)}H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y_{\nu}).

(235)

It will thus suffice to prove

lim

_
ν
→∞
Q
_
ν
(
B
,M,
T
)

(236)

\displaystyle\supseteq

\displaystyle\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Bigl\{(R_{1},\dotsc,R_{K})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{K}\colon\sum_{k\in\mathcal{T}}R_{k}<\mathcal{H}([\bm{u}]_{\mathcal{T}})-\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{B}}}(\bm{u}|Y)+J({\mathbf{B}},M)\Bigr\}.

For this purpose we will prove the following three limits:

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\bigl\{H([\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor]_{\mathcal{T}})-(L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}})\log(\nu)\bigr\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h([\bm{u}]_{\mathcal{T}})

(237a)

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\bigl\{H({\mathbf{B}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y_{\nu})-L_{\mathsf{B}}\log(\nu)\bigr\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h({\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y)

(237b)

\displaystyle\liminf_{\nu\to\infty}\bigl\{J_{\nu}({\mathbf{B}},M)-L_{\mathsf{C}}\log(\nu)\bigr\}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle J({\mathbf{B}},M).

(237c)

In summary, it suffices to prove (
237a
)–(
237c
) in order to prove Theorem
3
.

The equality (
237a
) follows directly from Lemma
5
applied to the right-invertible coefficient matrix
{\mathbf{Q}}=[{\mathbf{I}}]_{\mathcal{T}}\in\{0,1\}^{(L_{\mathsf{B}}-L_{\mathsf{C}})\times K}
. As for (
237b
), the proof is more involved and will require several steps. We will first make use of the following lemma.

Lemma 26
.

For any auxiliary variables
\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
, modulation mappings
\mathbf{x}(\bm{u})
and a channel law
P_{Y|X_{1},\dotsc,X_{K}}
that all conform with the assumptions of Theorem
3
, it holds that

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\Bigl\{H\bigl({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\big|Y_{\nu}\bigr)-H\bigl({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\big|Y\bigr)\Bigr\}=0

(238)

for any constant real-valued matrix
{\mathbf{Q}}
. Furthermore, this convergence is uniform in
{\mathbf{Q}}
, in that for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists an
\nu_{0}(\epsilon)>0
such that for any
\nu>\nu_{0}(\epsilon)
and any
{\mathbf{Q}}
, we have

\bigl|H\bigl({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\big|Y_{\nu}\bigr)-H\bigl({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\big|Y\bigr)\bigr|<\epsilon.

(239)

Proof:

See Appendix
N
.
∎

Lemma
26
allows us to swap out
Y_{\nu}
for
Y
in the conditional entropy term on the left-hand side of (
237b
), yielding

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\bigl\{H({\mathbf{B}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y_{\nu})-L_{\mathsf{B}}\log(\nu)\bigr\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\bigl\{H({\mathbf{B}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y)-L_{\mathsf{B}}\log(\nu)\bigr\}

(240)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\Bigl\{H\bigl({\mathbf{B}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\big|Y=y\bigr)-L_{\mathsf{B}}\log(\nu)\Bigr\}{\,\operatorname{d}}P_{Y}(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{B}}}(\bm{u}|Y=y){\,\operatorname{d}}P_{Y}(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{B}}}(\bm{u}|Y).

The exchange of limit and integration carried out in the second equality of (
240
) follows from the Dominated Convergence Theorem
[
71
, Theorem 1.6.9]
, which is applicable because by Lemmata
7
and
8
, the integrand can be bounded from above by

\displaystyle H\bigl({\mathbf{B}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\big|Y=y\bigr)-L_{\mathsf{B}}\log(\nu)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle H\bigl(\lfloor\nu{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}\rfloor\big|Y=y\bigr)+L_{\mathsf{B}}\log\left(\frac{\lVert{\mathbf{B}}\rVert_{1}}{\nu L_{\mathsf{B}}}\right)

(241)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle H\bigl(\lfloor{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}\rfloor\big|Y=y\bigr)+L_{\mathsf{B}}\log\left(\frac{\lVert{\mathbf{B}}\rVert_{1}}{L_{\mathsf{B}}}\right)

and from below by

\displaystyle H\bigl({\mathbf{B}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\big|Y=y\bigr)-L_{\mathsf{B}}\log(\nu)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle H\bigl(\lfloor\nu{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}\rfloor\big|Y=y\bigr)-L_{\mathsf{B}}\log\left(\frac{\lVert{\mathbf{B}}\rVert_{1}}{\nu L_{\mathsf{B}}}\right)

(242)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle h({\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y=y)-L_{\mathsf{B}}\log\left(\frac{\lVert{\mathbf{B}}\rVert_{1}}{L_{\mathsf{B}}}\right).

Both the upper bound (
241
) and lower bound (
242
) are integrable in
y
because
H(\lfloor{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}\rfloor|Y)
and
h({\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y)
are finite. The former is finite because
0\leq H(\lfloor{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}\rfloor|Y)\leq H(\lfloor{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}\rfloor)
where
H(\lfloor{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}\rfloor)
is finite by Lemma
9
, whereas the latter equals
h({\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}|Y)=h({\mathbf{B}}\bm{u})-I({\mathbf{B}}\bm{u};Y)
, where
h({\mathbf{B}}\bm{u})
is finite by Lemma
9
and
I({\mathbf{B}}\bm{u};Y)
is finite by the theorem’s assumption of a finite sum mutual information
I(\bm{u};Y)
.

Now, only the inequality (
237c
) remains to be proven. Due to the uniform convergence stated in Lemma
26
, we can swap out
Y_{\nu}
for
Y
in the limit on the left-hand side of (
237c
), i.e., if we define

\tilde{J}_{\nu}({\mathbf{B}},M)\triangleq\inf_{{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{Z}}(M)}H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y)

(243)

then
\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\bigl\{J_{\nu}({\mathbf{B}},M)-\tilde{J}_{\nu}({\mathbf{B}},M)\bigr\}=0
, hence

\liminf_{\nu\to\infty}\bigl\{J_{\nu}({\mathbf{B}},M)-L_{\mathsf{C}}\log(\nu)\bigr\}=\liminf_{\nu\to\infty}\bigl\{\tilde{J}_{\nu}({\mathbf{B}},M)-L_{\mathsf{C}}\log(\nu)\bigr\}.

(244)

Recall that the matrix
{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}
can be represented by Smith normal decomposition as [cf. Definition
7
]

{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}={\mathbf{S}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}){\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}){\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}})

(245)

where
{\mathbf{S}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}})\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}\times L_{\mathsf{C}}}
is unimodular,
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}})\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}\times L_{\mathsf{C}}}
is diagonal with positive diagonal entries
\sigma_{1}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}),\dotsc,\sigma_{L_{\mathsf{C}}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}})
, and
{\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}})\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}\times K}
is right-invertible.

Let
\bm{\Omega}^{\mathsf{T}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{K\times L_{\mathsf{C}}}
denote the matrix obtained from the Korkin–Zolotarev reduction of
{\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}})^{\mathsf{T}}
. In other words, the columns of
\bm{\Omega}^{\mathsf{T}}
are in Korkin–Zolotarev reduced form, and generate the same lattice as the columns of
{\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}})^{\mathsf{T}}
. Consider partition cells

\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{i}}=\bigl\{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\colon\bm{\Omega}\lfloor\mathbf{u}\rfloor=\mathbf{i}\bigr\}

(246)

indexed by
\mathbf{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}}
, which can be equivalently parametrized as

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{i}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigcup_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{K-L_{\mathsf{C}}}}\bigl\{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\colon\lfloor\mathbf{u}\rfloor=\bm{\Omega}^{\sharp}\mathbf{i}+\bm{\Omega}_{\perp}\mathbf{j}\bigr\}

(247)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{\Omega}^{\sharp}\mathbf{i}+\Lambda(\bm{\Omega}_{\perp})+[0,1)^{K}.

(248)

We have that the sets
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{i}}
are mutually disjoint and
\{\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{i}}\colon\mathbf{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}}\}
constitutes a partition of
\mathbb{R}^{K}
, in that
\mathbb{R}^{K}=\bigcup_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}}}\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{i}}
. Consider the entropy
H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y)
, which can be written out as

\displaystyle H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H(\bm{\Omega}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}}}\Phi\bigl(\P\{\bm{\Omega}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor=\mathbf{i}|Y=y\}\bigr){\,\operatorname{d}}P_{Y}(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}}}\Phi\bigl(P_{\bm{u}|Y=y}(\nu^{-1}\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{i}})\bigr){\,\operatorname{d}}P_{Y}(y)

where
\Phi(x)=-x\log(x)
. Let
\mathcal{Q}_{\nu}
denote the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the partition
\{\nu^{-1}\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{i}}\colon\mathbf{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}}\}
. Then the operation
\mathbf{u}\mapsto\bm{\Omega}\lfloor\nu\mathbf{u}\rfloor
(i.e., scaling by
\nu
, followed by flooring and left-multiplication with
\bm{\Omega}
) can be interpreted as a quantization operation with respect to the atoms of
\Sigma_{\nu}
. Accordingly, we can write

H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y)=H(\bm{\Omega}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y)=H(\lceil\bm{u}\rfloor_{\mathcal{Q}_{\nu}}|Y).

(249)

Let us assume from now on that
\bm{u}
has compact support, with a support set contained in the cube
\tau\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}

and let
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\nu}
denote the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the quantization cells
\{\nu^{-1}\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{i}}\cap\tau\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}\colon\mathbf{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}}\}
. Then

H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y)=H(\lceil\bm{u}\rfloor_{\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\nu}}|Y).

(250)

Next, we will upper-bound the volume of the
\mathbf{i}
-th quantization cell
\nu^{-1}\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{i}}\cap\tau\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}
.
For this purpose, we first outer-bound said quantization cell as follows:

\displaystyle\nu^{-1}\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{i}}\cap\tau\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nu^{-1}\left(\bm{\Omega}^{\sharp}\mathbf{i}+\Lambda(\bm{\Omega}_{\perp})+[0,1)^{K}\right)\cap\tau\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}

(251)

\displaystyle\subseteq

\displaystyle\bigl(\nu^{-1}(\bm{\Omega}^{\sharp}\mathbf{i}+\Lambda(\bm{\Omega}_{\perp}))\cap(\tau+\nu^{-1})\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}\bigr)+[0,\nu^{-1})^{K}.

To see why this holds, bear in mind that a vector
\mathbf{u}
belongs to the quantization cell
\nu^{-1}\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{i}}\cap\tau\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}
if and only if there exists a pair of vectors
(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{t})\in\mathbb{Z}^{K-L_{\mathsf{C}}}\times[0,1)^{K}
such that
\mathbf{u}=\nu^{-1}(\bm{\Omega}^{\sharp}\mathbf{i}+\bm{\Omega}_{\perp}\mathbf{j}+\mathbf{t})
and
\lVert\mathbf{u}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\tau
. The latter implies that
\nu^{-1}\lVert\bm{\Omega}^{\sharp}\mathbf{i}+\bm{\Omega}_{\perp}\mathbf{j}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\tau+\nu^{-1}
. Hence
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{u}_{1}+\mathbf{u}_{2}
is the sum of a vector
\mathbf{u}_{1}=\nu^{-1}(\bm{\Omega}^{\sharp}\mathbf{i}+\bm{\Omega}_{\perp}\mathbf{j})
satisfying
\mathbf{u}_{1}\in\nu^{-1}(\bm{\Omega}^{\sharp}\mathbf{i}+\Lambda(\bm{\Omega}_{\perp}))
as well as
\lVert\mathbf{u}_{1}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\tau+\nu^{-1}
, and a vector
\mathbf{u}_{2}=\nu^{-1}\mathbf{t}
satisfying
\mathbf{u}_{2}\in[0,\nu^{-1})^{K}
. This means that
\mathbf{u}
belongs to the set written out on the right-hand side of (
251
). Hence with the help of the inclusion relation (
251
), we can now upper-bound the volume of
\nu^{-1}\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{i}}\cap\tau\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}
as follows:

\displaystyle\vol\bigl(\nu^{-1}\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{i}}\cap\tau\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nu^{-K}\vol\bigl(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{i}}\cap\nu\tau\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}\bigr)

(252)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\nu^{-K}\vol{\Bigl(\left((\bm{\Omega}^{\sharp}\mathbf{i}+\Lambda(\bm{\Omega}_{\perp}))\cap(\nu\tau+1)\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}\right)+[0,1)^{K}\Bigr)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nu^{-K}\Bigl|(\bm{\Omega}^{\sharp}\mathbf{i}+\Lambda(\bm{\Omega}_{\perp}))\cap(\nu\tau+1)\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}\Bigr|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nu^{-K}\Bigl|\Lambda(\bm{\Omega}_{\perp})\cap\bigl((\nu\tau+1)\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}-\bm{\Omega}^{\sharp}\mathbf{i}\bigr)\Bigr|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\nu^{-K}\Bigl|\Lambda(\bm{\Omega}_{\perp})\cap\bigl(2\nu\tau\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}-\bm{\Omega}^{\sharp}\mathbf{i}\bigr)\Bigr|,

where the last inequality holds for
\nu\tau\geq 1
.
To further upper-bound the volume of quantization cells
\nu^{-1}\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{i}}\cap\tau\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}
, we will now elaborate an upper bound on the right-hand side of (
252
) via the following lemma.

Lemma 27

(Point-counting bound)
.

Let
{\mathbf{F}}=[\mathbf{f}_{1},\dotsc,\mathbf{f}_{L}]\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times L}
denote a tall matrix with linearly independent columns, and let
{\mathbf{G}}=[\mathbf{g}_{1},\dotsc,\mathbf{g}_{L}]\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times L}
denote a Gram–Schmidt orthogonalization of
{\mathbf{F}}
, with orthogonal columns
\mathbf{g}_{\ell},\ell=1,\dotsc,L
constructed from successive orthogonal projections as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{1}

(253a)

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{\ell}-\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell-1}\frac{\mathbf{f}_{\ell}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime}}}{\lVert\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime}}\rVert_{2}^{2}}\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime}},\quad\text{$\ell=2,\dotsc,L$.}

(253b)

The number of lattice points from
\Lambda({\mathbf{F}})
that lie within a
K
-dimensional infinity-norm ball of radius
\rho
and shifted by an arbitrary vector
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
is upper-bounded as

\displaystyle\left|\Lambda({\mathbf{F}})\cap(\rho\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}+\mathbf{t})\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\prod_{\ell=1}^{L}\left\lceil\frac{2\sqrt{K}\rho}{\lVert\mathbf{g}_{\ell}\rVert_{2}}\right\rceil.

(254)

Proof:

Let us define the
L
-dimensional cube

\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{\infty}^{L}=\left\{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\alpha_{\ell}\frac{\mathbf{g}_{\ell}}{\lVert\mathbf{g}_{\ell}\rVert_{2}}\colon(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{L})^{\mathsf{T}}\in[-1,1]^{L}\right\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{K}

(255)

which results from rotating the unit cube
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{L}=[-1,1]^{L}
(embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{K}
by zero-padding) about the origin to have its vertices aligned with the orthogonal basis
{\mathbf{G}}
. Alternatively, to represent
\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{\infty}^{L}
we may use the sumset notation

\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{\infty}^{L}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}[-1,1]\frac{\mathbf{g}_{\ell}}{\lVert\mathbf{g}_{\ell}\rVert_{2}}.

(256)

We have the inclusion relations

\displaystyle\Lambda({\mathbf{F}})\cap(\rho\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}+\mathbf{t})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\subset}}

\displaystyle\Span({\mathbf{G}})\cap(\rho\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}+\mathbf{t})

(257)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\subset}}

\displaystyle\Span({\mathbf{G}})\cap(\rho\sqrt{K}\mathscr{S}_{2}^{K}+\mathbf{t})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\subset}}

\displaystyle\Span({\mathbf{G}})\cap(\rho\sqrt{K}\mathscr{S}_{2}^{K}+{\mathbf{G}}({\mathbf{G}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\mathbf{G}})^{-1}{\mathbf{G}}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{t})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{\subset}}

\displaystyle\Span({\mathbf{G}})\cap(\rho\sqrt{K}\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{\infty}^{L}+{\mathbf{G}}({\mathbf{G}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\mathbf{G}})^{-1}{\mathbf{G}}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{t}).

Here, Step
(a)
is simply due to
\Lambda({\mathbf{F}})\subset\Span({\mathbf{F}})=\Span({\mathbf{G}})
; Step
(b)
follows from
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}\subset\sqrt{K}\mathscr{S}_{2}^{K}
; Step
(c)
holds because, if we decompose
\mathbf{t}=\mathbf{t}_{\perp}+\mathbf{t}_{\|}
into a vector
\mathbf{t}_{\perp}=({\mathbf{I}}-{\mathbf{G}}({\mathbf{G}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\mathbf{G}})^{-1}{\mathbf{G}}^{\mathsf{T}})\mathbf{t}
that is orthogonal to
\Span({\mathbf{G}})
and a vector
\mathbf{t}_{\|}={\mathbf{G}}({\mathbf{G}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\mathbf{G}})^{-1}{\mathbf{G}}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{t}
that lies in
\Span({\mathbf{G}})
, then the intersection of the
L
-dimensional plane
\Span({\mathbf{G}})
and the
K
-dimensional sphere
\mathscr{S}_{2}^{K}
shifted by
\mathbf{t}
, is maximal when the orthogonal component
\mathbf{t}_{\perp}
is zero; Step
(d)
follows because
\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{\infty}^{L}\subset\Span({\mathbf{G}})
includes all elements of
{\mathbf{G}}
with Euclidean norm smaller or equal to unity. Intersecting both sides of (
257
) with
\Lambda({\mathbf{F}})
we end up with

\Lambda({\mathbf{F}})\cap(\rho\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}+\mathbf{t})\subset\Lambda({\mathbf{F}})\cap(\rho\sqrt{K}\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{\infty}^{L}+\mathbf{t}_{\|})

(258)

where
\mathbf{t}_{\|}
is an abbreviation for the projection of
\mathbf{t}
onto the column space of
{\mathbf{G}}
, as defined above. Hence, from (
258
) we infer that

\bigl|\Lambda({\mathbf{F}})\cap(\rho\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}+\mathbf{t})\bigr|\leq\bigl|\Lambda({\mathbf{F}})\cap(\rho\sqrt{K}\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{\infty}^{L}+\mathbf{t}_{\|})\bigr|.

(259)

Since the lattice
\Lambda({\mathbf{F}})
can be expressed as an iterated union

\Lambda({\mathbf{F}})=\bigcup_{\alpha_{L}\in\mathbb{Z}}\dotso\bigcup_{\alpha_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}\left\{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\alpha_{\ell}\mathbf{f}_{\ell}\right\}

(260)

we can evaluate the cardinality
\bigl|\Lambda({\mathbf{F}})\cap(\rho\sqrt{K}\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{\infty}^{L}+\mathbf{t}_{\|})\bigr|
by an iterated sum as

\displaystyle\bigl|\Lambda({\mathbf{F}})\cap(\rho\sqrt{K}\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{\infty}^{L}+\mathbf{t}_{\|})\bigr|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha_{L}\in\mathbb{Z}}\dotso\sum_{\alpha_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathds{1}{\left\{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\alpha_{\ell}\mathbf{f}_{\ell}\in(\rho\sqrt{K}\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{\infty}^{L}+\mathbf{t}_{\|})\right\}}

(261)

where the indicator function
\mathds{1}\{\cdot\}
equals one if the statement in braces is true, and zero otherwise. The iterated sum (
261
) can be decomposed into partial summations

\displaystyle S_{1}(\alpha_{2},\dotsc,\alpha_{L})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathds{1}{\left\{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\alpha_{\ell}\mathbf{f}_{\ell}\in\bigl(\rho\sqrt{K}\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{\infty}^{L}+\mathbf{t}_{\|}\bigr)\right\}}

(262)

\displaystyle S_{\ell+1}(\alpha_{\ell+2},\dotsc,\alpha_{L})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha_{\ell+1}\in\mathbb{Z}}S_{\ell}(\alpha_{\ell+1},\dotsc,\alpha_{L}),\quad\text{for $\ell=1,\dotsc,L-1$}

(263)

such that the last sum
S_{L}=\sum_{\alpha_{L}\in\mathbb{Z}}S_{L-1}(\alpha_{L})
equals the cardinality
\bigl|\Lambda({\mathbf{F}})\cap(\rho\sqrt{K}\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{\infty}^{L}+\mathbf{t}_{\|})\bigr|
, which we seek to upper-bound. We will now prove by induction that the following upper bound holds:

S_{\ell}(\alpha_{\ell+1},\dotsc,\alpha_{L})\leq\prod_{k=1}^{\ell}\left\lceil\frac{2\rho\sqrt{K}}{\lVert\mathbf{g}_{k}\rVert_{2}}\right\rceil\times\mathds{1}{\left\{\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=\ell+1}^{L}\alpha_{\ell^{\prime}}\sum_{\ell^{\prime\prime}=\ell+1}^{L}\frac{\mathbf{f}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}}{\lVert\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}\rVert_{2}^{2}}\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}\in\bigl(\rho\sqrt{K}\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{\infty}^{L}+\mathbf{t}_{\|}\bigr)\right\}}.

(264)

For the first partial sum, we can prove the bound (
264
) as follows:

\displaystyle S_{1}(\alpha_{2},\dotsc,\alpha_{L})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathds{1}{\left\{\left(\alpha_{1}+\sum_{\ell=2}^{L}\alpha_{\ell}\frac{\mathbf{f}_{\ell}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{g}_{1}}{\lVert\mathbf{g}_{1}\rVert_{2}^{2}}\right)\mathbf{g}_{1}+\sum_{\ell=2}^{L}\alpha_{\ell}\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=2}^{L}\frac{\mathbf{f}_{\ell}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime}}}{\lVert\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime}}\rVert_{2}^{2}}\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime}}\in\bigl(\rho\sqrt{K}\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{\infty}^{L}+\mathbf{t}_{\|}\bigr)\right\}}

(265)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathds{1}{\left\{\left(\alpha_{1}+\sum_{\ell=2}^{L}\alpha_{\ell}\frac{\mathbf{f}_{\ell}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{g}_{1}}{\lVert\mathbf{g}_{1}\rVert_{2}^{2}}\right)\mathbf{g}_{1}-\frac{\mathbf{g}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{t}}{\lVert\mathbf{g}_{1}\rVert_{2}^{2}}\mathbf{g}_{1}\in[-1,1]\frac{\rho\sqrt{K}\mathbf{g}_{1}}{\lVert\mathbf{g}_{1}\rVert_{2}}\right\}}\times

\displaystyle\qquad{}\times\mathds{1}{\left\{\sum_{\ell=2}^{L}\alpha_{\ell}\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=2}^{L}\frac{\mathbf{f}_{\ell}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime}}}{\lVert\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime}}\rVert_{2}^{2}}\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime}}-\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=2}^{L}\frac{\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{t}}{\lVert\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime}}\rVert_{2}^{2}}\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime}}\in\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=2}^{L}[-1,1]\frac{\rho\sqrt{K}\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime}}}{\lVert\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime}}\rVert_{2}}\right\}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle\left\lceil\frac{2\rho\sqrt{K}}{\lVert\mathbf{g}_{1}\rVert_{2}}\right\rceil\mathds{1}{\left\{\sum_{\ell=2}^{L}\alpha_{\ell}\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=2}^{L}\frac{\mathbf{f}_{\ell}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime}}}{\lVert\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime}}\rVert_{2}^{2}}\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime}}\in\bigl(\rho\sqrt{K}\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{\infty}^{L}+\mathbf{t}_{\|}\bigr)\right\}}.

Here, Step
(a)
consists in expanding
\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\alpha_{\ell}\mathbf{f}_{\ell}
along the basis
{\mathbf{G}}
; in Step
(b)
we have exploited the orthogonality of
{\mathbf{G}}
and the geometry of
\tilde{{\mathbf{B}}}_{\infty}^{L}
to factorize the indicator function; in Step
(c)
we have upper-bounded the first indicator function by the ceiling term.
By comparison with (
264
), we confirm that the upper bound holds indeed for
\ell=1
.
If the bound (
264
) holds for some
\ell\geq 1
, we prove that it must also hold for
\ell+1
, as follows:

S_ℓ+1(
α
_ℓ+2,
…
,
α
_L)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha_{\ell+1}\in\mathbb{Z}}S_{\ell}(\alpha_{\ell+1},\dotsc,\alpha_{L})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{\ell}\left\lceil\frac{2\rho\sqrt{K}}{\lVert\mathbf{g}_{k}\rVert_{2}}\right\rceil\sum_{\alpha_{\ell+1}\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathds{1}{\left\{\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=\ell+1}^{L}\alpha_{\ell^{\prime}}\sum_{\ell^{\prime\prime}=\ell+1}^{L}\frac{\mathbf{f}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}}{\lVert\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}\rVert_{2}^{2}}\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}\in\bigl(\rho\sqrt{K}\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{\infty}^{L}+\mathbf{t}_{\|}\bigr)\right\}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{\ell}\left\lceil\frac{2\rho\sqrt{K}}{\lVert\mathbf{g}_{k}\rVert_{2}}\right\rceil\sum_{\alpha_{\ell+1}\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathds{1}\Biggl\{\alpha_{\ell+1}\left(\mathbf{g}_{\ell+1}+\sum_{\ell^{\prime\prime}=\ell+2}^{L}\frac{\mathbf{f}_{\ell+1}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}}{\lVert\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}\rVert_{2}^{2}}\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}\right)+\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=\ell+2}^{L}\alpha_{\ell^{\prime}}\sum_{\ell^{\prime\prime}=\ell+1}^{L}\frac{\mathbf{f}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}}{\lVert\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}\rVert_{2}^{2}}\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}

∈
(
