(266)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{\ell+1}\left\lceil\frac{2\rho\sqrt{K}}{\lVert\mathbf{g}_{k}\rVert_{2}}\right\rceil\mathds{1}\Biggl\{\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=\ell+1}^{L}\alpha_{\ell^{\prime}}\sum_{\ell^{\prime\prime}=\ell+2}^{L}\frac{\mathbf{f}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}}{\lVert\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}\rVert_{2}^{2}}\mathbf{g}_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}\in\bigl(\rho\sqrt{K}\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{\infty}^{L}+\mathbf{t}_{\|}\bigr)\Biggr\}.

The steps are similar as before: in Step
(a)
we expand along the basis
{\mathbf{G}}
; in Step
(b)
we factorize the indicator function; in Step
(c)
we upper-bound the first indicator function. This concludes the argument by induction. Therefore, the inequality (
264
) holds for
\ell=L-1
, hence

S_{L}=\bigl|\Lambda({\mathbf{F}})\cap(\rho\sqrt{K}\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{\infty}^{L}+\mathbf{t}_{\|})\bigr|\leq\prod_{k=1}^{L}\left\lceil\frac{2\rho\sqrt{K}}{\lVert\mathbf{g}_{k}\rVert_{2}}\right\rceil

(267)

which in combination with (
259
) concludes the proof of Lemma
27
.
∎

Let
{\mathbf{G}}=[\mathbf{g}_{1},\dotsc,\mathbf{g}_{K-L_{\mathsf{C}}}]\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times(K-L_{\mathsf{C}})}
denote the Gram–Schmidt orthogonalization of

\bm{\Omega}_{\perp}=\bigl[\bm{\omega}_{1}^{\perp},\dotsc,\bm{\omega}_{K-L_{\mathsf{C}}}^{\perp}\bigr]\in\mathbb{Z}^{K\times(K-L_{\mathsf{C}})}.

(268)

That is,
\mathbf{g}_{\ell}
is constructed from the vectors
(\bm{\omega}_{1}^{\perp},\dotsc,\bm{\omega}_{\ell}^{\perp})
by successive projections as described in (
253a
)–(
253b
).
Applying Lemma
27
on the lattice
\Lambda(\bm{\Omega}_{\perp})
intersected with a ball of radius
\rho
and arbitrary shift
-\mathbf{t}
, we get

\displaystyle\left|\Lambda(\bm{\Omega}_{\perp})\cap(\rho\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}-\mathbf{t})\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\prod_{\ell=1}^{K-L_{\mathsf{C}}}\left\lceil\frac{2\sqrt{K}\rho}{\lVert\mathbf{g}_{\ell}\rVert_{2}}\right\rceil

(269)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(2\sqrt{K}\rho)^{K-L_{\mathsf{C}}}}{\sqrt{\det(\bm{\Omega}\bm{\Omega}^{\mathsf{T}})}}\qquad\text{(for $2\sqrt{K}\rho\geq\max_{\ell\in[K-L_{\mathsf{C}}]}\lVert\mathbf{g}_{\ell}\rVert_{2}$)}

because
\prod_{\ell=1}^{K-L_{\mathsf{C}}}\lVert\mathbf{g}_{\ell}\rVert_{2}^{2}=\det({\mathbf{G}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\mathbf{G}})=\det(\bm{\Omega}_{\perp}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}_{\perp})=\det(\bm{\Omega}\bm{\Omega}^{\mathsf{T}})
.
Note that

\displaystyle\max_{\ell\in[K-L_{\mathsf{C}}]}\lVert\mathbf{g}_{\ell}\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle\max_{\ell\in[K-L_{\mathsf{C}}]}\lVert\bm{\omega}_{\ell}^{\perp}\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle\max_{\ell\in[K-L_{\mathsf{C}}]}\sqrt{\frac{\ell+3}{4}}\lambda_{2,\ell}(\bm{\Omega}_{\perp})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{K-L_{\mathsf{C}}+3}{4}}\lambda_{2,K-L_{\mathsf{C}}}(\bm{\Omega}_{\perp})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{K-L_{\mathsf{C}}+3}{4}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{K-L_{\mathsf{C}}}\lambda_{2,\ell}(\bm{\Omega}_{\perp})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{K-L_{\mathsf{C}}+3}{4}}2^{K-L_{\mathsf{C}}}\det(\bm{\Omega}_{\perp}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}_{\perp})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(e)}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle\sqrt{K}2^{K}\det(\bm{\Omega}\bm{\Omega}^{\mathsf{T}}).

Here, Step
(a)
holds because
\mathbf{g}_{\ell}
results from a (contractive) projection of
\bm{\omega}_{\ell}^{\perp}
, hence
\lVert\mathbf{g}\rVert_{2}\leq\lVert\bm{\omega}_{\ell}^{\perp}\rVert_{2}
for all
\ell
; Step
(b)
involves the fact that
\bm{\Omega}_{\perp}
is in reduced Korkin–Zolotarev form and the inequality (
43
); Step
(c)
makes use of the fact that the successive minima of
\bm{\Omega}_{\perp}
are lower-bounded by unity because
\bm{\Omega}_{\perp}
is an integer lattice; Step
(d)
involves Lemma
12
; Step
(e)
uses
\det(\bm{\Omega}_{\perp}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}_{\perp})=\det(\bm{\Omega}\bm{\Omega}^{\mathsf{T}})
and
L_{\mathsf{C}}\geq 0
as well as
3\leq 3K
.
From this inequality we can deduce a sufficient condition for the inequality (
269
) to hold, leading to a weaker version of (
269
), namely

\displaystyle\left|\Lambda(\bm{\Omega}_{\perp})\cap(\rho\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}-\mathbf{t})\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{(2\sqrt{K}\rho)^{K-L_{\mathsf{C}}}}{\sqrt{\det(\bm{\Omega}\bm{\Omega}^{\mathsf{T}})}}\qquad\text{(for $\rho\geq 2^{K-1}\det(\bm{\Omega}\bm{\Omega}^{\mathsf{T}})$).}

(270)

Evaluating (
270
) for
\rho=2\nu\tau
and noticing that the condition
2\nu\tau\geq 2^{K-1}\det(\bm{\Omega}\bm{\Omega}^{\mathsf{T}})
in (
270
) already implies the condition
\nu\tau\geq 1
from (
252
) (due to
K\geq 2
and
\det(\bm{\Omega}\bm{\Omega}^{\mathsf{T}})\geq 1
since
\bm{\Omega}
is an integer lattice), we can combine (
252
) and (
270
) to obtain

\displaystyle\vol\bigl(\nu^{-1}\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{i}}\cap\tau\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{(4\sqrt{K}\tau)^{K-L_{\mathsf{C}}}}{\sqrt{\det(\bm{\Omega}\bm{\Omega}^{\mathsf{T}})}}\nu^{-L_{\mathsf{C}}}\qquad\text{(for $\nu\tau\geq 2^{K-2}\det(\bm{\Omega}\bm{\Omega}^{\mathsf{T}})$).}

(271)

Based on (
250
), Lemma
6
and the bound (
271
), we can state the following lower bound on the entropy
H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y)
:

H(
CB
⌊
ν
u
⌋—Y)

(272)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H(\lceil\bm{u}\rfloor_{\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\nu}}|Y)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle h(\bm{u}|Y)-\max_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L_{\mathsf{C}}}}\log\bigl(\vol{\bigl(\nu^{-1}\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{i}}\cap\tau\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}\bigr)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle h(\bm{u}|Y)-\log\left(\frac{(4\sqrt{K}\tau)^{K-L_{\mathsf{C}}}}{\sqrt{\det(\bm{\Omega}\bm{\Omega}^{\mathsf{T}})}}\right)+L_{\mathsf{C}}\log(\nu)\qquad\text{(for $\nu\tau\geq 2^{K-2}\det(\bm{\Omega}\bm{\Omega}^{\mathsf{T}})$).}

On the other hand, we can derive an upper bound on
H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y)
by means of Lemmata
7
[Inequality (
30
)] and
8
, as follows:

\displaystyle H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y)-L_{\mathsf{C}}\log(\nu)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle H(\lfloor{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)-L_{\mathsf{C}}\log(\nu)+L_{\mathsf{C}}\log{\left(\frac{\lVert{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\rVert_{1}}{L_{\mathsf{C}}}\right)}

(273)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle H(\lfloor{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}\rfloor)+L_{\mathsf{C}}\log{\left(\frac{\lVert{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\rVert_{1}}{L_{\mathsf{C}}}\right)}.

Let us define the quantity

H_{0}({\mathbf{B}},M)=\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathscr{C}(M)}\left\{H(\lfloor{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\bm{u}\rfloor)+L_{\mathsf{C}}\log{\left(\frac{\lVert{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\rVert_{1}}{L_{\mathsf{C}}}\right)}\right\}

(274)

which is an upper bound on
\tilde{J}_{\nu}({\mathbf{B}},M)-L_{\mathsf{C}}\log(\nu)
.
In the optimization problem that defines the quantity
\tilde{J}_{\nu}({\mathbf{B}},M)
[cf. (
243
)], a matrix
{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathscr{C}(M)
is
strictly
suboptimal, in the sense that
H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y)>\tilde{J}_{\nu}({\mathbf{B}},M)
, if the following holds:

H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y)-L_{\mathsf{C}}\log(\nu)>H_{0}({\mathbf{B}},M).

(275)

We will now derive a sufficient condition on
{\mathbf{C}}
for being strictly suboptimal, so as to eventually reduce the domain of optimization in (
243
) to a finite set. For this purpose, consider that (
275
) certainly holds if [cf. (
272
)] the following two conditions on
\nu
and
\bm{\Omega}
are satisfied:

\displaystyle h(\bm{u}|Y)-\log\left(\frac{(4\sqrt{K}\tau)^{K-L_{\mathsf{C}}}}{\sqrt{\det(\bm{\Omega}\bm{\Omega}^{\mathsf{T}})}}\right)

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle H_{0}({\mathbf{B}},M)

(276a)

\displaystyle\nu\tau

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 2^{K-2}\det(\bm{\Omega}\bm{\Omega}^{\mathsf{T}}).

(276b)

(Recall that
\bm{\Omega}
is a function of
{\mathbf{B}}
and
{\mathbf{C}}
).
As these two conditions (
276a
)–(
276b
) involve the determinant
\det(\bm{\Omega}\bm{\Omega}^{\mathsf{T}})
, we now wish to translate these into sufficient conditions that involve
\det({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}})
.
Consider the following inequality, which relates
\det(\bm{\Omega}\bm{\Omega}^{\mathsf{T}})
and
\det({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}})
:

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}({\mathbf{B}}{\mathbf{B}}^{\mathsf{T}})^{L_{\mathsf{C}}}\det({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle\det({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}{\mathbf{B}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}})

(277)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\det({\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}))^{2}\det({\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}){\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}})^{\mathsf{T}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\det({\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}))^{2}\det(\bm{\Omega}\bm{\Omega}^{\mathsf{T}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle\det({\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{B}}))^{2}\det(\bm{\Omega}\bm{\Omega}^{\mathsf{T}}).

The inequality
(a)
is obtained by upper-bounding the positive semidefinite matrix
{\mathbf{B}}{\mathbf{B}}^{\mathsf{T}}
by the scaled identity matrix
\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathbf{B}}{\mathbf{B}}^{\mathsf{T}}){\mathbf{I}}
, where
\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathbf{B}}{\mathbf{B}}^{\mathsf{T}})=\max_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{L_{\mathsf{B}}}}\mathbf{x}^{\mathsf{T}}{\mathbf{B}}{\mathbf{B}}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{x}/\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}
denotes the maximum eigenvalue of
{\mathbf{B}}{\mathbf{B}}^{\mathsf{T}}
; the equality
(b)
results from applying the Smith normal decomposition (
245
) and recalling that
{\mathbf{S}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}})
is unimodular; Step
(c)
, in turn, is an application of the following auxiliary result.

Lemma 28
.

For any integer matrix
{\mathbf{B}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell\times k}
with
\ell\leq k
and full column rank, and for any right-invertible matrix
{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{r\times n}
(
r\leq n
) we have
\det({\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}))\leq\det({\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{B}}))
.

Proof:

Consider the Smith normal decomposition
{\mathbf{C}}=\tilde{{\mathbf{S}}}({\mathbf{C}})\tilde{{\mathbf{\Sigma}}}({\mathbf{C}})\tilde{{\mathbf{T}}}({\mathbf{C}})
and the reduced Smith normal decomposition
{\mathbf{B}}={\mathbf{S}}({\mathbf{B}}){\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{B}}){\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{B}})
. Since
{\mathbf{C}}
is right-invertible by assumption, we have
\tilde{{\mathbf{\Sigma}}}({\mathbf{C}})=[{\mathbf{I}}_{r\times r}\ \mynull_{r\times(n-r)}]
.
Since
\tilde{{\mathbf{T}}}({\mathbf{C}})
and
\tilde{{\mathbf{S}}}({\mathbf{C}})
are unimodular, on the one hand,
\det({\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{B}}))=\det(\tilde{{\mathbf{T}}}({\mathbf{C}}){\mathbf{B}})
and on the other hand,
\det({\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}))=\det(\tilde{{\mathbf{\Sigma}}}({\mathbf{C}})\tilde{{\mathbf{T}}}({\mathbf{C}}){\mathbf{B}})=\det([\tilde{{\mathbf{T}}}({\mathbf{C}}){\mathbf{B}}]_{[r]})
.
The product
\tilde{{\mathbf{T}}}({\mathbf{C}}){\mathbf{B}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell\times k}
has full column rank, much like
{\mathbf{B}}
.
Therefore, in the statement of Lemma
28
we can assume without loss of generality that
{\mathbf{C}}
is of the form
[{\mathbf{I}}_{r\times r}\ \mynull_{r\times(n-r)}]
and thus restate the lemma as the inequality

\det({\mathbf{\Sigma}}([{\mathbf{B}}]_{[r]}))\leq\det({\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{B}})).

(278)

To prove the latter, in turn, it will suffice to prove that, if
{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{(\ell-1)\times k}
denotes the matrix
{\mathbf{B}}
with one row removed, we have

\det({\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}))\leq\det({\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{B}})).

(279)

Without loss of generality, let
{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}=[{\mathbf{B}}]_{[2{:}\ell]}
, that is,
{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}
is obtained from removing the first row of
{\mathbf{B}}
. Let us denote the
\ell\times\ell
submatrices of
{\mathbf{B}}
as
{\mathbf{B}}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell\times\ell}
, of which there are
\binom{k}{\ell}
. As a direct consequence of the definition of elementary divisors (
26
), the determinant
\det({\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{B}}))
is equal to the (positive) greatest common divisor of all
\ell\times\ell
minors of
{\mathbf{B}}
, i.e.,

\det({\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{B}}))=\gcd\left(\det({\mathbf{B}}_{1}),\det({\mathbf{B}}_{2}),\dotsc\right).

(280)

Let us now develop each of these determinants along the elements of the first row of
{\mathbf{B}}_{i}
, that is,

\det({\mathbf{B}}_{i})=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}(-1)^{j+1}[{\mathbf{B}}_{i}]_{1,j}\det([{\mathbf{B}}_{i}]_{\setminus 1,\setminus j})

(281)

where
[{\mathbf{B}}_{i}]_{\setminus 1,\setminus j}
denotes the matrix
{\mathbf{B}}_{i}
with the first line and
j
-th column removed. Note that these submatrices
[{\mathbf{B}}_{i}]_{\setminus 1,\setminus j}
are in fact
(\ell-1)\times(\ell-1)
submatrices of
{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}
. Clearly, by iterating over all
(i,j)\in[\binom{\ell}{k}]\times[\ell-1]
, the submatrices
[{\mathbf{B}}_{i}]_{\setminus 1,\setminus j}
will reach all submatrices of
{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}
(each submatrix multiple times). Consequently, the greatest common divisor of the minors of
{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}
, which is

\det({\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}))=\gcd\Bigl(\det([{\mathbf{B}}_{1}]_{\setminus 1,\setminus 1}),\dotsc,\det([{\mathbf{B}}_{1}]_{\setminus 1,\setminus(\ell-1)}),\det([{\mathbf{B}}_{2}]_{\setminus 1,\setminus 1}),\dotsc\\
\dotsc,\det([{\mathbf{B}}_{2}]_{\setminus 1,\setminus(\ell-1)}),\dotsc\Bigr)

(282)

divides every
\det({\mathbf{B}}_{i})
, and is therefore a divisor of
\det({\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{B}}))
. Since
{\mathbf{B}}
has full row rank, we have
\det({\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{B}}))\neq 0
and therefore the claim
\det({\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}))\leq\det({\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{B}}))
follows, thereby concluding the proof of Lemma
28
.
∎

To complement (
277
), one can obtain inequality in reverse direction as follows:

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathbf{B}}{\mathbf{B}}^{\mathsf{T}})^{L_{\mathsf{C}}}\det({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\det({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}{\mathbf{B}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}})

(283)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\det({\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}))^{2}\det(\bm{\Omega}\bm{\Omega}^{\mathsf{T}})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\det(\bm{\Omega}\bm{\Omega}^{\mathsf{T}})

where the last inequality follows from
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}})
being an integer non-singular matrix.
Combining (
276a
)–(
276b
) with (
277
) and (
283
), and upon rearranging the terms in (
276a
), we obtain a new pair of sufficient conditions (on
{\mathbf{C}}
and
\nu
) that together imply (
276a
)–(
276b
), namely

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}({\mathbf{B}}{\mathbf{B}}^{\mathsf{T}})^{L_{\mathsf{C}}/2}\sqrt{\det({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}})}}{\det({\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{B}}))}

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle(4\sqrt{K}\tau)^{K-L_{\mathsf{C}}}e^{H_{0}({\mathbf{B}},M)-h(\bm{u}|Y)}

(284a)

\displaystyle\nu\tau

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 2^{K-2}\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathbf{B}}{\mathbf{B}}^{\mathsf{T}})^{L_{\mathsf{C}}}\det({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}}).

(284b)

In summary, we have shown that if
{\mathbf{C}}
is such that
\det({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}})
is so large as to satisfy (
284a
), then for all
\nu
large enough to satisfy (
284b
), the matrix
{\mathbf{C}}
is not a solution to the minimization problem. In other words, matrices
{\mathbf{C}}
satisfying (
284a
) can be excluded from the minimization domain, to such effect that the minimization domain can be reduced to a finite set and thus the order of limit-taking and minimization can be exchanged. Specifically, if we denote the constant

C_{0}({\mathbf{B}},M)\triangleq\frac{(4\sqrt{K}\tau)^{K-L_{\mathsf{C}}}\det({\mathbf{\Sigma}}({\mathbf{B}}))}{\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}({\mathbf{B}}{\mathbf{B}}^{\mathsf{T}})^{L_{\mathsf{C}}/2}}e^{H_{0}({\mathbf{B}},M)-h(\bm{u}|Y)}

(285)

then we have [cf. (
243
)–(
244
)]

\displaystyle\liminf_{\nu\to\infty}\bigl\{J_{\nu}({\mathbf{B}},M)-L_{\mathsf{C}}\log(\nu)\bigr\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\liminf_{\nu\to\infty}\bigl\{\tilde{J}_{\nu}({\mathbf{B}},M)-L_{\mathsf{C}}\log(\nu)\bigr\}

(286)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\liminf_{\nu\to\infty}\Bigl\{\inf_{{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{Z}}(M)}H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y)-L_{\mathsf{C}}\log(\nu)\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{Z}}(M)\colon\det({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}})\leq\Theta}\liminf_{\nu\to\infty}\bigl\{H({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y)-L_{\mathsf{C}}\log(\nu)\bigr\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{Z}}(M)\colon\det({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathsf{T}})\leq\Theta}\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}}(\bm{u}|Y)

for any
\Theta\geq C_{0}({\mathbf{B}},M)
. Taking the limit as
\Theta\to\infty
, we finally end up with

\displaystyle\liminf_{\nu\to\infty}\bigl\{J_{\nu}({\mathbf{B}},M)-L_{\mathsf{C}}\log(\nu)\bigr\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\inf_{{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{Z}}(M)}\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}}(\bm{u}|Y).

(287)

This completes the proof of (
237c
) and thus of Theorem
3
for absolutely continuous auxiliaries of bounded support. To remove this limitation, we will now use a “truncation” argument, similar to the “clipping” argument used in the proof of Theorem
2
. Based on an absolutely continuous auxiliary
\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
with distribution
P_{\bm{u}}
, let us define the truncated auxiliary
\bm{u}_{\tau}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
(for any
\tau
sufficiently large to satisfy
P_{\bm{u}}(\tau\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K})>0
) as

P_{\bm{u}_{\tau}}(V)=\frac{P_{\bm{u}}(V\cap\tau\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K})}{P_{\bm{u}}(\tau\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K})}\quad\text{(for all measurable $V$).}

(288)

In other words, if
f_{\bm{u}}
and
f_{\bm{u}_{\tau}}
denote the probability density functions of
\bm{u}
and
\bm{u}_{\tau}
, respectively, then
f_{\bm{u}_{\tau}}
is given by

f_{\bm{u}_{\tau}}(\mathbf{u})=\frac{f_{\bm{u}}(\mathbf{u})}{\int_{\tau\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}}f_{\bm{u}}(\mathbf{u}){\,\operatorname{d}}\mathbf{u}}\mathds{1}\bigl\{\mathbf{u}\in\tau\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}\bigr\}.

(289)

As already done in the proof of Theorem
2
, let us incorporate the dependency on the auxiliary distribution into the rate region notations, by writing
\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}};\bm{u})
and
\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T};\bm{u})
in lieu of
\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}})
and
\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T})
, respectively. By Theorem
3
, which thus far we have proven to hold for auxiliaries with bounded support, we can assert that
\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}};\bm{u}_{\tau})
is achievable. Therefore, the inner set limit
\varliminf_{\tau\to\infty}\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}};\bm{u}_{\tau})
is achievable. Upon writing out
\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}};\bm{u}_{\tau})
according to its definition (
54
) as

\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}};\bm{u}_{\tau})=\bigcap_{M\in\mathscr{M}^{\circ}_{\mathbb{Z}}(L_{\mathsf{B}})}\bigcup_{\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{B}(M^{*})}\bigcap_{\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{B}([{\mathbf{B}}]_{\mathcal{S}})}\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T};\bm{u}_{\tau})

(290)

and observing that this expression involves a finite number of set operations that are independent of
\tau
, we can argue that the inner set limit can be exchanged with the other set operations, so as to yield

\varliminf_{\tau\to\infty}\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}};\bm{u}_{\tau})=\bigcap_{M\in\mathscr{M}^{\circ}_{\mathbb{Z}}(L_{\mathsf{B}})}\bigcup_{\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{B}(M^{*})}\bigcap_{\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{B}([{\mathbf{B}}]_{\mathcal{S}})}\varliminf_{\tau\to\infty}\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T};\bm{u}_{\tau}).

(291)

The set on the right-hand side of the last equation can in turn be expressed as [cf. (
27b
)]

\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T};\bm{u}_{\tau})=\biggl\{(R_{1},\dotsc,R_{K})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{K}\colon\sum_{k\in\mathcal{T}}R_{k}<\\
h([\bm{u}_{\tau}]_{\mathcal{T}})+\inf_{{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{Z}}(M)}h({\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}})\bm{u}_{\tau}|Y_{\tau})-h({\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{B}})\bm{u}_{\tau}|Y_{\tau})\biggr\}

(292)

where
Y_{\tau}
denotes the channel output induced by the auxiliary
\bm{u}_{\tau}
. Specifically, for any value
\mathbf{u}
belonging to the support of
\bm{u}_{\tau}
, conditioned on
\bm{u}_{\tau}=\mathbf{u}
and given a modulation mapping
\mathbf{x}(\mathbf{u})
, the variable
Y_{\tau}
is distributed as
Y_{\tau}\sim P_{Y|\bm{x}}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}(\mathbf{u}))
.

Lemma 29

(Truncation lemma)
.

Let
(\bm{u},Y)\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\times\mathcal{Y}
and
(\bm{u}_{\tau},Y_{\tau})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\times\mathcal{Y}
denote pairs of random variables as defined above, where
\bm{u}\sim P_{\bm{u}}
is absolutely continuous with finite differential entropy
h(\bm{u})
and finite entropy
H(\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor)
, and where
\bm{u}_{\tau}\sim P_{\bm{u}_{\tau}}
denotes its truncated counterpart as defined in (
288
).
Given some real matrix
{\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times K}
with full row rank, we have that, as
\tau\to\infty
, the entropy
h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}_{\tau}|Y_{\tau})
converges to
h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}|Y)
uniformly in
{\mathbf{Q}}
, in the sense that there exists an upper bound on the absolute value of the difference of differential entropies

\bigl|h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}_{\tau}|Y_{\tau})-h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}|Y)\bigr|\leq\delta(\tau)

(293)

that does not depend on
{\mathbf{Q}}
, and satisfies
\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\delta(\tau)=0
.

Proof:

First note that it will suffice to prove simple convergence, i.e.,

\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\bigl|h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}_{\tau}|Y_{\tau})-h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}|Y)\bigr|=0.

(294)

In fact, the uniform convergence claimed in Lemma
29
immediately follows from a scale invariance of the entropy difference: for any full row rank
{\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times K}
, consider the factorization
{\mathbf{Q}}={\mathbf{R}}\tilde{{\mathbf{Q}}}
with a full-rank square diagonal matrix
{\mathbf{R}}\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times M}
and a matrix
\tilde{{\mathbf{Q}}}\in\mathbb{C}^{M\times K}
with rows normalized such that each row has an infinity norm equal to unity. In particular, such a factorization entails
\lVert\tilde{{\mathbf{Q}}}\rVert_{\infty}=1
. We can now express the difference of differential entropies as

\displaystyle h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}_{\tau}|Y_{\tau})-h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}|Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h(\tilde{{\mathbf{Q}}}\bm{u}_{\tau}|Y_{\tau})+\log\left|\det({\mathbf{R}})\right|-h(\tilde{{\mathbf{Q}}}\bm{u}|Y)-\log\left|\det({\mathbf{R}})\right|

(295)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h(\tilde{{\mathbf{Q}}}\bm{u}_{\tau}|Y_{\tau})-h(\tilde{{\mathbf{Q}}}\bm{u}|Y).

Hence, for any upper bound
\delta(\tau;{\mathbf{Q}})
that might depend on
{\mathbf{Q}}
, such that

\bigl|h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}_{\tau}|Y_{\tau})-h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}|Y)\bigr|\leq\delta(\tau;{\mathbf{Q}})

(296)

and
\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\delta(\tau;{\mathbf{Q}})=0
(for every
{\mathbf{Q}}
), one can define the uniform upper bound
\delta(\tau)=\max_{\tilde{{\mathbf{Q}}}}\delta(\tau;\tilde{{\mathbf{Q}}})
, where the maximum is taken over the compact set of full row-rank matrices
\tilde{{\mathbf{Q}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times K}
with rows having unit infinity norm. In the following, we shall thus assume that the rows of
{\mathbf{Q}}
have unit infinity norm, without loss of generality. In particular, this assumption implies
\lVert{\mathbf{Q}}\rVert_{\infty}=1
.

As a first proof step, we will show convergence of mutual informations, namely,

\lim_{\tau\to\infty}I({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}_{\tau};Y_{\tau})=I({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u};Y).

(297)

To this end, let us define the binary random variable

E_{\tau}=\mathds{1}\{\lVert\bm{u}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\tau\}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $\lVert\bm{u}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\tau$}\\
0&\text{if $\lVert\bm{u}\rVert_{\infty}>\tau$}\end{cases}

(298)

as well as the clipping operation

\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle_{\tau}=\mathds{1}\{\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\tau\}\mathbf{x}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{x}&\text{if $\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\tau$}\\
\mynull&\text{if $\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{\infty}>\tau$.}\end{cases}

(299)

In order to relate the mutual information term
I({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}_{\tau};Y_{\tau})
(based on the truncated variable
\bm{u}_{\tau}
) with the mutual information
I({\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau};Y)
(based on the clipped variable
\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}
), consider the following chain of equalities:

I(
Q
⟨
u
⟩
_
τ
; Y)

(300)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I({\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau},E_{\tau};Y)-I(E_{\tau};Y|{\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle I({\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau},E_{\tau};Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I(E_{\tau};Y)+I({\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau};Y|E_{\tau})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I(E_{\tau};Y)+I({\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau};Y|E_{\tau}=0)\P\{E_{\tau}=0\}+I({\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau};Y|E_{\tau}=1)\P\{E_{\tau}=1\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle I(E_{\tau};Y)+I({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}_{\tau};Y_{\tau})\P\{E_{\tau}=1\}.

The first equality
(a)
holds because the mutual information
I(E_{\tau};Y|{\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau})
is zero. In fact, its upper bound
H(E_{\tau}|{\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau})
vanishes since
{\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}
determines
E_{\tau}
almost surely. This is because
\bm{u}
is absolutely continuous and
{\mathbf{Q}}
has full row rank, such that, conditional on
{\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}=\mynull
(resp.
{\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}\neq 0
) we have
E_{\tau}=1
(resp.
E_{\tau}=0
) with probability one. The last equality
(b)
holds because conditioned on
E_{\tau}=0
, the variable
{\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}
equals to zero, hence
I({\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau};Y|E_{\tau}=0)=0
, and on the other hand because, conditioned on
E_{\tau}=1
, the variable
({\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau};Y)
has the same distribution as
({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}_{\tau};Y_{\tau})
.

Building upon on the equality (
300
), we can now upper-bound the mutual information
I({\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau};Y)
in terms of
I({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u};Y)
, as follows:

\displaystyle I({\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau};Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I(E_{\tau};Y)+I({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}_{\tau};Y_{\tau})\P\{E_{\tau}=1\}

(301)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I(E_{\tau};Y)+I({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u};Y|E_{\tau}=1)\P\{E_{\tau}=1\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle I(E_{\tau};Y)+I({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u};Y|E_{\tau})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u};Y)+I(E_{\tau};Y|{\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}).

Bearing in mind that
I(E_{\tau};Y|{\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u})
vanishes as
\tau\to\infty
(due to
\lim_{\tau\to\infty}H(E_{\tau})=0
), and taking the superior limit on both sides of (
301
), we get

\displaystyle\limsup_{\tau\to\infty}I({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}_{\tau};Y_{\tau})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\limsup_{\tau\to\infty}I({\mathbf{Q}}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau};Y)

(302)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle I({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u};Y).

Here, the first equality is obtained by using the equality (
300
) and recalling that
\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\P\{E_{\tau}=1\}=1
, which entails that
I(E_{\tau};Y)
, which is upper-bounded by the (binary) entropy
H(E_{\tau})
, tends to zero as
\tau\to\infty
.

To derive a matching inferior limit, we show that the pair
({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}_{\tau},Y_{\tau})
converges in distribution to
({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u},Y)
. By the data processing inequality and the chain rule of relative entropy, we can upper-bound the Kullback–Leibler divergence between
({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}_{\tau},Y_{\tau})
and
({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u},Y)
as follows:

\displaystyle D\bigl(P_{{\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}_{\tau},Y_{\tau}}\big\|P_{{\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u},Y}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle D\bigl(P_{\bm{u}_{\tau}}P_{Y|\bm{u}}\big\|P_{\bm{u}}P_{Y|\bm{u}}\bigr)

(303)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D\bigl(P_{\bm{u}_{\tau}}\big\|P_{\bm{u}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\tau\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}}\frac{f_{\bm{u}}(\mathbf{u})}{\P\bigl\{\bm{u}\in\tau\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}\bigr\}}\log\frac{1}{\P\bigl\{\bm{u}\in\tau\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}\bigr\}}{\,\operatorname{d}}\mathbf{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\log\P\bigl\{\bm{u}\in\tau\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{K}\bigr\}.

Taking the limit on both sides as
\tau\to\infty
, we conclude that
D\bigl(P_{{\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}_{\tau},Y_{\tau}}\big\|P_{{\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u},Y}\bigr)
tends to zero, hence
({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}_{\tau},Y_{\tau})
converges to
({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u},Y)
in Kullback–Leibler divergence, which implies convergence in distribution. Therefore, by the lower semi-continuity of mutual information
[
72
, Thm. 1]
,
[
73
, Thm. 19]
,

\liminf_{\tau\to\infty}I({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}_{\tau};Y_{\tau})\geq I({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u};Y).

(304)

Combining the superior limit (
302
) with the inferior limit (
304
), we obtain the limit

\lim_{\tau\to\infty}I({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}_{\tau};Y_{\tau})=I({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u};Y).

(305)

With this convergence of mutual information established, and since

h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}_{\tau}|Y_{\tau})-h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}|Y)=h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}_{\tau})-h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u})-I({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}_{\tau};Y_{\tau})+I({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u};Y)

(306)

it follows that a proof of (
294
) (and thus of Lemma
29
) reduces to proving

\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\bigl\{h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}_{\tau})-h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u})\bigr\}=0.

(307)

For this purpose, we first show that the function

\tilde{f}_{\tau}(\mathbf{w})\triangleq f_{{\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}|\lVert\bm{u}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\tau}(\mathbf{w})\P\{E_{\tau}=0\}

(308)

is monotone (non-decreasing) in
\tau
and for every
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
, we have the limit

\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\tilde{f}_{\tau}(\mathbf{w})=f_{{\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}}(\mathbf{w}).

(309)

Lemma 30
.

Consider an absolutely continuous random variable
\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
with probability distribution
\mu_{\bm{u}}
. For a set
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{K}
with
\mu_{\bm{u}}(U)>0
, denote as
\bm{u}_{U}\sim\mu_{U}
a random variable distributed as
\bm{u}
restricted on
\bm{u}\in U
, i.e.,
\mu_{U}(S)=\mu_{\bm{u}}(S\cap U)/\mu_{\bm{u}}(U)
for any measurable
S
. Let
g\colon\mathbb{R}^{K}\to\mathbb{R}^{M}
be some deterministic function such that
\bm{w}=g(\bm{u})
is absolutely continuous. Then
\bm{w}|\bm{u}\in U
is absolutely continuous with a density denoted as
f_{\bm{w}|U}
, and for any measurable sets
U\subset U^{\prime}
we have

\mu_{\bm{u}}(U)f_{\bm{w}|U}(w)\leq\mu_{\bm{u}}(U^{\prime})f_{\bm{w}|U^{\prime}}(w).

(310)

Proof:

Consider the variable pair
(\bm{u},\bm{w})
, which has a joint distribution
\mu_{\bm{u},\bm{w}}
. Clearly, for
U\subset U^{\prime}
and any
W\subset\mathbb{R}^{M}
, it holds that

\mu_{\bm{u},\bm{w}}(U\times W)\leq\mu_{\bm{u},\bm{w}}(U^{\prime}\times W)

(311)

or equivalently,

\mu_{\bm{u}}(U)\mu_{\bm{w}|\bm{u}}(W|U)\leq\mu_{\bm{u}}(U^{\prime})\mu_{\bm{w}|\bm{u}}(W|U^{\prime})

(312)

Clearly
\mu_{U}\ll\mu_{\bm{u}}\ll\lambda
and since
\bm{w}=g(\bm{u})
is absolutely continuous by assumption, we also have
\mu_{\bm{w}|\bm{u}}(\cdot|U)\ll\lambda
. The distributions
\mu_{\bm{w}|\bm{u}}(\cdot|U)
and
\mu_{\bm{w}|\bm{u}}(\cdot|U^{\prime})
thus have densities
f_{\bm{w}|U}
and
f_{\bm{w}|U^{\prime}}
, respectively, so can write the latter inequality as

\mu_{\bm{u}}(U)\int_{W}f_{\bm{w}|U}(w){\,\operatorname{d}}w\leq\mu_{\bm{u}}(U^{\prime})\int_{W}f_{\bm{w}|U^{\prime}}(w){\,\operatorname{d}}w.

(313)

Since this equality holds for every
W\in\mathcal{W}
, it follows that

\mu_{\bm{u}}(U)f_{\bm{w}|U}(w)\leq\mu_{\bm{u}}(U^{\prime})f_{\bm{w}|U^{\prime}}(w)

(314)

which proves the claim.
∎

Let us express
h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}_{\tau})
is terms of the function
\tilde{f}_{\tau}(\mathbf{w})
:

\displaystyle h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}_{\tau})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}|E_{\tau}=0)

(315)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}f_{{\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}|E_{\tau}=0}(\mathbf{w})\log f_{{\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}|E_{\tau}=0}(\mathbf{w}){\,\operatorname{d}}\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}\frac{\tilde{f}_{\tau}(\mathbf{w})}{\P\{E_{\tau}=0\}}\log\frac{\tilde{f}_{\tau}(\mathbf{w})}{\P\{E_{\tau}=0\}}{\,\operatorname{d}}\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log\P\{E_{\tau}=0\}-\frac{1}{\P\{E_{\tau}=0\}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}\tilde{f}_{\tau}(\mathbf{w})\log\tilde{f}_{\tau}(\mathbf{w}){\,\operatorname{d}}\mathbf{w}.

Furthermore, let us define the set

\mathcal{R}=\Bigl\{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}\colon f_{{\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}}(\mathbf{w})\geq\tfrac{1}{e}\Bigr\}

(316)

where
e
denotes the Euler constant. Clearly, the set
\mathcal{R}
is compact because
f_{{\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}}(\mathbf{w})
is a probability density function and as such, it is non-negative and integrates to unity.
The integral in (
315
) can be split as follows:

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}\tilde{f}_{\tau}(\mathbf{w})\log\tilde{f}_{\tau}(\mathbf{w}){\,\operatorname{d}}\mathbf{w}=\int_{\mathcal{R}}\tilde{f}_{\tau}(\mathbf{w})\log\tilde{f}_{\tau}(\mathbf{w}){\,\operatorname{d}}\mathbf{w}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{M}\setminus\mathcal{R}}\tilde{f}_{\tau}(\mathbf{w})\log\tilde{f}_{\tau}(\mathbf{w}){\,\operatorname{d}}\mathbf{w}.

(317)

When taking the limit as
\tau\to\infty
on both sides of (
317
), one can pull the limit operation inside the first right-hand side integral due to
\mathcal{R}
being compact, and also inside the second integral due to the Monotone Convergence Theorem
[
71
, Thm. 1.6.2]
, which is applicable because
\tilde{f}_{\tau}(\mathbf{w})
is monotone in
\tau
(for every
\mathbf{w}
) and
x\mapsto x\log(x)
is monotonely decreasing on
[0,1/e]
. This yields

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}\tilde{f}_{\tau}(\mathbf{w})\log\tilde{f}_{\tau}(\mathbf{w}){\,\operatorname{d}}\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{R}}f_{{\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}}(\mathbf{w})\log f_{{\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}}(\mathbf{w}){\,\operatorname{d}}\mathbf{w}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{M}\setminus\mathcal{R}}f_{{\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}}(\mathbf{w})\log f_{{\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}}(\mathbf{w}){\,\operatorname{d}}\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}).

In combination with (
315
) and the fact that
\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\P\{E_{\tau}=0\}=1
, we conclude that

\lim_{\tau\to\infty}h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}_{\tau})=h({\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}).

(318)

∎

By Lemma
29
, we have the limits

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to\infty}h([\bm{u}_{\tau}]_{\mathcal{T}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h([\bm{u}]_{\mathcal{T}})

(319a)

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to\infty}h({\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}})\bm{u}_{\tau}|Y_{\tau})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h({\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}})\bm{u}|Y)

(319b)

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to\infty}h({\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{B}})\bm{u}_{\tau}|Y_{\tau})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h({\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{B}})\bm{u}|Y).

(319c)

Regarding (
319b
), since Lemma
29
further asserts that the convergence is uniform, we can exchange the order of limit-taking and infimization can be exchanged, yielding

\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\inf_{{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{Z}}(M)}h({\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}})\bm{u}_{\tau}|Y_{\tau})=\inf_{{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{Z}}(M)}h({\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}})\bm{u}|Y).

(320)

Furthermore, by Lemma
5
we can revert all entropies back to the algebraic entropy notation:

\displaystyle h([\bm{u}]_{\mathcal{T}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}([\bm{u}]_{\mathcal{T}})

(321a)

\displaystyle h({\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}})\bm{u}|Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}}(\bm{u}|Y)

(321b)

\displaystyle h({\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{B}})\bm{u}|Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{B}}}(\bm{u}|Y).

(321c)

Combining (
319a
), (
319c
) and (
320
), and using algebraic entropy notation (
321
), we conclude that

\varliminf_{\tau\to\infty}\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T};\bm{u}_{\tau})=\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}},M,\mathcal{T};\bm{u})

(322)

and therefore, substituting back into (
291
), we finally end up with

\varliminf_{\tau\to\infty}\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}};\bm{u}_{\tau})=\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}};\bm{u}).

(323)

This finalizes the proof of Theorem
3
.

Appendix M
Proof of Lemma
19

By
[
60
, Lemma 7.18]
, we have

\displaystyle I(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor;\nu\bm{u}-\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)=\lim_{m\to\infty}I\bigl(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor;\big\lfloor m(\nu\bm{u}-\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)\big\rfloor\bigr).

The mutual information
I\bigl(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor;\big\lfloor m(\nu\bm{u}-\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)\big\rfloor\bigr)
can be upper-bounded as follows:

I
(
⌊
ν
u
⌋; ⌊m(
ν
u
-⌊
ν
u
⌋)⌋
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)+H\bigl(\big\lfloor m\nu\bm{u}-m\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\big\rfloor\bigr)-H\bigl(\big\lfloor m\nu\bm{u}-m\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\big\rfloor,\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)+H\bigl(\big\lfloor m\nu\bm{u}-m\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\big\rfloor\bigr)-H\bigl(\lfloor m\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)+n\log(m+1)-H\bigl(\lfloor m\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\bigr)

where step
(a)
follows because there is a one-to-one correspondence between
\bigl(\big\lfloor m\nu\bm{u}-m\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\big\rfloor,\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\bigr)
and
\lfloor m\nu\bm{u}\rfloor
and step
(b)
follows because each of the
n
coordinates of
\big\lfloor m\nu\bm{u}-m\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\big\rfloor
takes at most
m+1
values. Taking the limit as
\nu\to\infty
, we thus get an upper bound

\displaystyle I(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor;\nu\bm{u}-\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)+\lim_{m\to\infty}\bigl\{n\log(m+1)-H\bigl(\lfloor m\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\bigr)\bigr\}

(324)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor)-n\log(\nu)-h(\bm{u}).

Finally, taking the limit as
\nu\to\infty
we obtain the desired result

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}I\bigl(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor;\nu\bm{u}-\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

This concludes the proof.

Appendix N
Proof of Lemma
26

The statement of Theorem
3
distinguishes two mutually exclusive assumptions:

•

Case 1: Staircase mappings
x_{k}(u_{k})
with finite images;

•

Case 2: Linear mappings
x_{k}(u_{k})=\beta_{k}u_{k}
and a linear additive noise system equation.

The proof of this lemma requires distinct treatment for each of these assumptions, and will thus be structured in two parts.

N-A

Case 1: Staircase mappings with finite images

Let the
|\mathcal{X}_{k}|
locations of jump discontinuities of the staircase function
x_{k}(u_{k})
be denoted as
\upsilon_{1}^{(k)}<\upsilon_{2}^{(k)}<\dotsc
and let

\Upsilon_{\nu}^{(k)}=\left[\upsilon_{1}^{(k)}-\tfrac{1}{\nu};\upsilon_{1}^{(k)}+\tfrac{1}{\nu}\right]\cup\left[\upsilon_{2}^{(k)}-\tfrac{1}{\nu};\upsilon_{2}^{(k)}+\tfrac{1}{\nu}\right]\cup\dotso

(325)

denote the closed
1/\nu
-radius neighborhood of the set of discontinuity locations. Furthermore, let
\Upsilon_{\nu}=\Upsilon_{\nu}^{(1)}\times\dotso\times\Upsilon_{\nu}^{(K)}
be the Cartesian product of these neighborhoods. Let us define the binary random variable

E_{\nu}=\begin{cases}0&\text{if $\bm{u}\notin\Upsilon_{\nu}$}\\
1&\text{if $\bm{u}\in\Upsilon_{\nu}$}.\end{cases}

(326)

Note that
\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\P\{E_{\nu}=0\}=1
as a consequence of
\bm{u}
being absolutely continuous and
\Upsilon_{\nu}
having vanishing Lebesgue measure as
\nu\to\infty
. Moreover, note that the event
E_{\nu}=0
implies that
x(\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu})=x(\bm{u})
. As a consequence, we can choose to couple the channel output variables
Y
and
Y_{\nu}
in a way that, conditioned on
E_{\nu}=0
, we have
Y=Y_{\nu}
. Since mutual informations
I(E_{\nu};Y_{\nu})
,
I(E_{\nu};Y_{\nu}|{\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu})
,
I(E_{\nu};Y)
and
I(E_{\nu};Y|{\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu})
are all upper-bounded by
H(E_{\nu})
,
we infer that

\displaystyle\bigl|I(E_{\nu};Y_{\nu})-I(E_{\nu};Y_{\nu}|{\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu})\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle H(E_{\nu}),

(327a)

\displaystyle\bigl|I(E_{\nu};Y)-I(E_{\nu};Y|{\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu})\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle H(E_{\nu})

(327b)

whose left-hand sides tend to zero as
\nu\to\infty
because of
\lim_{\nu\to\infty}H(E_{\nu})=0
. By the chain rule for mutual information, we have

\displaystyle I(E_{\nu};Y_{\nu})-I(E_{\nu};Y_{\nu}|{\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y_{\nu})-I({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y_{\nu}|E_{\nu}),

(328a)

\displaystyle I(E_{\nu};Y)-I(E_{\nu};Y|{\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y)-I({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y|E_{\nu}).

(328b)

Combining (
327
) and (
328
), we infer that

\displaystyle\bigl|I({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y_{\nu})-I({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y_{\nu}|E_{\nu})\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle H(E_{\nu}),

(329a)

\displaystyle\bigl|I({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y)-I({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y|E_{\nu})\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle H(E_{\nu}).

(329b)

Let us now focus on the two conditional mutual information terms appearing in (
329
), which can be written out respectively as

\displaystyle I({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y_{\nu}|E_{\nu})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y_{\nu}|E_{\nu}=0)\P\{E_{\nu}=0\}

(330a)

\displaystyle{}+I({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y_{\nu}|E_{\nu}=1)\P\{E_{\nu}=1\},

\displaystyle I({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y|E_{\nu})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y|E_{\nu}=0)\P\{E_{\nu}=0\}

(330b)

\displaystyle{}+I({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y|E_{\nu}=1)\P\{E_{\nu}=1\}.

Due to the coupling between
Y
and
Y_{\nu}
that we have chosen, which imposes that
Y=Y_{\nu}
whenever
E_{\nu}=0
, it holds that
I({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y_{\nu}|E_{\nu}=0)=I({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y|E_{\nu}=0)
. Subtracting (
330a
) from (
330b
) and taking the absolute value, we obtain the equality

\Bigl|I({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y|E_{\nu})-I({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y_{\nu}|E_{\nu})\Bigr|\\
=\Bigl|I({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y|E_{\nu}=1)-I({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y_{\nu}|E_{\nu}=1)\Bigr|\cdot\P\{E_{\nu}=1\}.

(331)

As to the mutual information terms appearing on the right-hand side of (
331
), they can be bounded as follows:

\displaystyle I({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y|E_{\nu}=1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y|\bm{u}\in\Upsilon_{\nu})

(332a)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle I(\bm{u};Y|\bm{u}\in\Upsilon_{\nu})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{P_{U}}I(\bm{u};Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{P_{X}}I(X;Y)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\log|\mathcal{X}|

\displaystyle I({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y_{\nu}|E_{\nu}=1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y_{\nu}|\bm{u}\in\Upsilon_{\nu})

(332b)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle I(\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y_{\nu}|\bm{u}\in\Upsilon_{\nu})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{P_{U}}I(\bm{u};Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{P_{X}}I(X;Y)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\log|\mathcal{X}|

where
|\mathcal{X}|=\prod_{k=1}^{K}|\mathcal{X}_{k}|
. It follows that the left-hand side of (
331
) is upper-bounded by
\log|\mathcal{X}|\cdot\P\{E_{\nu}=1\}
, i.e.,

\bigl|I({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y|E_{\nu})-I({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y_{\nu}|E_{\nu})\bigr|\leq\log|\mathcal{X}|\cdot\P\{E_{\nu}=1\}.

(333)

Combining (
329
) and (
333
), we get

\displaystyle\bigl|H({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu}|Y)-H({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu}|Y_{\nu})\bigr|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigl|I({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y)-I({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y_{\nu})\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\log|\mathcal{X}|\cdot\P\{E_{\nu}=1\}+2H(E_{\nu}).

Since this upper bound does not depend on
{\mathbf{Q}}
and vanishes as
\nu\to\infty
, this concludes the proof for Case 1.

N-B

Case 2: Linear mappings and linear additive noise channel

Now assume that we fix a linear mapping
x_{k}(u_{k})=\beta_{k}u_{k}
. Let us define, conditioned on values
(U_{1},\dotsc,U_{K})=(u_{1},\dotsc,u_{K})
(in short
\bm{u}=u
) of the auxiliary variables, the outputs
Y
and
Y_{\nu}
as being

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}h_{k}\beta_{k}u_{k}+Z

(334a)

\displaystyle Y_{\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}h_{k}\beta_{k}\lfloor u_{k}\rfloor_{\nu}+Z

(334b)

respectively, with real-valued weights
\beta_{k}
, where
Z
is an independent (not necessarily Gaussian) additive noise. We choose the coupling of
Y
and
Y_{\nu}
to be such that they are generated by the same realization of
Z
. Specifically, this means that, conditioned on
\bm{u}=u
, their difference is given by

Y-Y_{\nu}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}h_{k}\beta_{k}(u_{k}-\lfloor u_{k}\rfloor_{\nu})

(335)

and is thus a linear function of the quantization error vector

\bm{d}_{\nu}=\bm{u}-\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu}.

(336)

We first bound the entropy difference from above as follows:

\displaystyle H\bigl({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu}\big|Y_{\nu}\bigr)-H\bigl({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu}\big|Y\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I\bigl({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y\bigr)-I\bigl({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y_{\nu}\bigr)

(337)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle I\bigl({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y,Y_{\nu},\bm{d}_{\nu}\bigr)-I\bigl({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y_{\nu}\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I\bigl({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};\bm{d}_{\nu}\big|Y_{\nu}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle I\bigl(\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};\bm{d}_{\nu}\big|Y_{\nu}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle I(\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};\bm{d}_{\nu}).

The second equality is due to
Y
being a deterministic function of
(Y_{\nu},\bm{d}_{\nu})
; the second inequality is the data processing inequality; the third and last inequality holds because
\bm{d}_{\nu}\rightarrow\bm{u}\rightarrow Y_{\nu}
forms a Markov chain.

Next, we bound the same entropy difference from below:

\displaystyle H\bigl({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu}\big|Y_{\nu}\bigr)-H\bigl({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu}\big|Y\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I\bigl({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y\bigr)-I\bigl({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y_{\nu}\bigr)

(338)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle I\bigl({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};Y\bigr)-I\bigl({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};\bm{d}_{\nu},Y\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-I\bigl({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};\bm{d}_{\nu}\bigl|Y\bigr)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle-I\bigl(\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};\bm{d}_{\nu}\bigl|Y\bigr).

Here, the first inequality is due to
Y_{\nu}
being a deterministic function of
(\bm{d}_{\nu},Y)
; the second inequality is the data processing inequality.

The upper bound (
337
) tends to zero as
\nu\to\infty
as a consequence of Lemma
19
. As for the lower bound (
338
), we have

\lim_{\nu\to\infty}I\bigl(\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};\bm{d}_{\nu}\bigl|Y\bigr)=\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\lim_{\nu\to\infty}I\bigl(\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};\bm{d}_{\nu}\bigl|Y=y\bigr)P_{Y}(\mathrm{d}y)=0

(339)

by the Dominated Convergence Theorem
[
71
, (1.6.9), p. 50]
. The latter can be applied because
I\bigl(\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};\bm{d}_{\nu}\bigl|Y=y\bigr)
has an integrable upper bound. To derive such a bound, reproducing the steps in the proof of Lemma
19
up to (
324
) first leads to

\displaystyle I\bigl(\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};\bm{d}_{\nu}\big|Y=y\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle H\bigl(\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu}\big|Y=y\bigr)-K\log(\nu)-h(\bm{u}|Y=y).

(340)

Then, the discrete entropy on the right-hand side of (
340
) can be upper-bounded as follows: let
k
and
k^{\prime}
denote
K
-dimensional integer vectors and define

p_{k,k^{\prime}}^{\nu}=\P\bigl\{k+\tfrac{k^{\prime}}{\nu}\leq\bm{u}<k+\tfrac{k^{\prime}+1}{\nu}\big|Y=y\bigr\}

(341)

where inequalities on vectors are to be interpreted entrywise. Note that

\sum_{k^{\prime}\in[0:\nu-1]^{K}}p_{k,k^{\prime}}^{\nu}=\P\bigl\{k\leq\bm{u}<k+1\big|Y=y\bigr\}.

(342)

By the log-sum inequality,

\displaystyle H\bigl(\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu}\big|Y=y\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{K}}\sum_{k^{\prime}\in[0:\nu-1]^{K}}p_{k,k^{\prime}}^{\nu}\log\frac{1}{p_{k,k^{\prime}}^{\nu}}

(343)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{K}}\left(\textstyle\sum_{k^{\prime}\in[0:\nu-1]^{K}}p_{k,k^{\prime}}^{\nu}\right)\log\left(\frac{\nu^{K}}{\textstyle\sum_{k^{\prime}\in[0:\nu-1]^{K}}p_{k,k^{\prime}}^{\nu}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K\log(\nu)+H\bigl(\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor\big|Y=y\bigr).

Combining the latter with (
340
), we obtain

I\bigl(\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu};\bm{d}_{\nu}\big|Y=y\bigr)\leq H\bigl(\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor\big|Y=y\bigr)-h(\bm{u}|Y=y).

(344)

This bound is integrable due to
I(\bm{u};Y)
,
H(\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor)
and
h(\bm{u})
being finite.

In summary, the upper and lower bound (
337
) and (
338
) on the entropy difference
H\bigl({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu}\big|Y_{\nu}\bigr)-H\bigl({\mathbf{Q}}\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor_{\nu}\big|Y\bigr)
both tend to zero. Since these bounds do not depend on
{\mathbf{Q}}
, this convergence is uniform. This concludes the proof for Case 2.

Appendix O
Proof of Theorem
5

This proof follows a similar logic as the proof of Theorem
2
(although the absence of the minimum-entropy term
J({\mathbf{B}},M)
drastically simplifies the proof).

We set
\mathbb{U}=\mathbb{Z}
and fix the pmf
p_{\bm{u}}(\mathbf{u})=\prod_{k=1}^{K}p_{U_{k}}(u_{k})
, the modulation mappings
\mathbf{x}(\mathbf{u})
as well as the matrix
{\mathbf{B}}_{[j]}\in\mathbb{Z}^{j\times K}
. Let
Y_{\mathsf{q}}
denote the channel output induced by the (finite-field) auxiliary
\phi_{\mathsf{q}}(\bm{u})
, in the context of Theorem
4
. We prove Theorem
5
in two steps: first, assuming that
\bm{u}
is finitely supported, and then, we relax this finiteness assumption.

If the support of
\bm{u}
is contained in the finite cube
\llbracket 2\tau+1\rrbracket^{K}
for some
\tau>0
, then for
\mathsf{q}>2\tau+1
we have
H(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}([\bm{u}]_{\mathcal{T}})))=H([\bm{u}]_{\mathcal{T}})
. Furthermore, for
\mathsf{q}\geq(2\tau+1)\lVert{\mathbf{B}}_{[j]}\rVert_{\infty}
, there is a one-to-one correspondence between
\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}}_{[j]}\bm{u})
and
{\mathbf{B}}_{[j]}\bm{u}
such that all instances of
Y_{\mathsf{q}}
can be replaced by
Y
, hence
H(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}}_{[j]}\bm{u})|Y_{\mathsf{q}})=H({\mathbf{B}}_{[j]}\bm{u}|Y)=\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{B}}_{[j]}}(\bm{u}|Y)
.
Consequently, the right-hand side of (
56
), as evaluated in the context of Theorem
4
, becomes equal to

H(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}(U_{k}))+H(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}}_{[j-1]}\bm{u})|Y_{\mathsf{q}})-H(\phi_{\mathsf{q}}({\mathbf{B}}_{[j]}\bm{u})|Y_{\mathsf{q}})\\
=H(U_{k})+H({\mathbf{B}}_{[j-1]}\bm{u}|Y)-H({\mathbf{B}}_{[j]}\bm{u}|Y)\quad\text{(for $\mathsf{q}\geq(2\tau+1)\lVert{\mathbf{B}}\rVert_{\infty}$).}

(345)

This proves Theorem
5
for finitely supported auxiliaries. To lift this restriction, now consider an auxiliary
\bm{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{K}
with arbitrary support but finite entropy
H(\bm{u})
. Using the clipping operation
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\tau}
as defined in (
299
) and denoting as
Y_{\tau}
the channel output induced by the clipped auxiliary
\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}
, we have the limits

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to\infty}H(\langle U_{k}\rangle_{\tau})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H(U_{k})=\mathcal{H}(U_{k})

(346a)

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to\infty}H({\mathbf{B}}_{[j-1]}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}|Y_{\tau})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H({\mathbf{B}}_{[j-1]}\bm{u}|Y)=\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{B}}_{[j-1]}}(\bm{u}|Y)

(346b)

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to\infty}H({\mathbf{B}}_{[j]}\langle\bm{u}\rangle_{\tau}|Y_{\tau})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H({\mathbf{B}}_{[j]}\bm{u}|Y)=\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{B}}_{[j]}}(\bm{u}|Y).

(346c)

Here, the last two limits follow from Lemma
25
. This concludes the proof of Theorem
5
.

Appendix P
Proof of Theorem
6

We set
(\mathbb{U},\mathbb{A})=(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{Z})
and fix the pdf
f_{\bm{u}}(\mathbf{u})=\prod_{k=1}^{K}f_{U_{k}}(u_{k})
, the modulation mappings
\mathbf{x}(\mathbf{u})
as well as the matrix
{\mathbf{B}}_{[j]}\in\mathbb{Z}^{j\times K}
. Let
Y_{\nu}
denote the channel output induced by the integer-valued auxiliary
\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor
, in the context of Theorem
5
.

By Lemma
26
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\Bigl\{H({\mathbf{B}}_{[j-1]}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y_{\nu})-H({\mathbf{B}}_{[j-1]}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y)\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(347a)

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\Bigl\{H({\mathbf{B}}_{[j]}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y_{\nu})-H({\mathbf{B}}_{[j]}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y)\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

(347b)

Hence,
Y_{\nu}
can be swapped out for
Y
in these limits, so we obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}H(\lfloor\nu U_{k}\rfloor)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h(U_{k})=\mathcal{H}(U_{k})

(348a)

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}H({\mathbf{B}}_{[j-1]}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y_{\tau})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h({\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{B}}_{[j-1]})\bm{u}|Y)=\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{B}}_{[j-1]}}(\bm{u}|Y)

(348b)

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}H({\mathbf{B}}_{[j]}\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y_{\tau})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h({\mathbf{T}}({\mathbf{B}}_{[j]})\bm{u}|Y)=\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{B}}_{[j]}}(\bm{u}|Y).

(348c)

This concludes the proof of Theorem
6
.

Appendix Q
Evaluation of
\mathscr{R}({\mathbf{A}})
for
(K,L)=(2,2)

We evaluate
\mathscr{R}({\mathbf{A}})=\bigcup_{{\mathbf{B}}}\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}})
for the special case
K=L=2
.
Let us denote
{\mathbf{B}}=\begin{bmatrix}b_{2,1}&b_{1,2}\\
b_{2,1}&b_{2,2}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{b}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\\
\mathbf{b}_{2}^{\mathsf{T}}\end{bmatrix}
. We distinguish two cases based on the rank of the matroid
M
.

The rank-
0
matroid
M=(\{1,2\},\emptyset)

In this case, the index sets
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{T}
are equal to
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{T}=\{1,2\}
, hence we obtain the sum-rate bound

\displaystyle R_{1}+R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(\bm{u})-\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{B}}(\bm{u}|Y)

(349)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(\bm{u})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I(\bm{u};Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I(\bm{x};Y).

The rank-
1
matroids

Since
{\mathbf{B}}
has full rank, either the diagonal entries
(b_{1,1},b_{2,2})
or the off-diagonal entries
(b_{1,2},b_{2,1})
are both non-zero, for otherwise
{\mathbf{B}}
would have an all-zero row or column. Since we can freely permute the rows of
{\mathbf{B}}
without affecting the value of
\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}})
, we shall assume without loss of generality that the diagonal entries
(b_{1,1},b_{2,2})
are non-zero. In the following, we make case distinctions based on the off-diagonals:

•

The off-diagonals are
b_{1,2}=b_{2,1}=0
:

–

Case
M=(\{1,2\},\{1\})
: the index sets are equal to
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{T}=\{2\}
, hence

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{2})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\mathcal{H}(U_{1}|Y)

(350)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I(U_{2};Y,U_{1}).

–

Case
M=(\{1,2\},\{2\})
: the index sets are equal to
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{T}=\{1\}
, hence

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{1})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\mathcal{H}(U_{2}|Y)

(351)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I(U_{1};Y,U_{2}).

–

Case
M=(\{1,2\},\{1\},\{2\})
: the index set
\mathcal{S}
is iterated over
\mathcal{S}\in\{\{1\},\{2\}\}
:

*

For
\mathcal{S}=\{1\}
, the index set
\mathcal{T}
is equal to
\mathcal{T}=\{1\}
, hence

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{1})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\inf_{c_{1}\neq 0,\,c_{2}\neq 0}\mathcal{H}_{[c_{1}b_{1,1}\ c_{2}b_{2,2}]}(\bm{u}|Y).

(352)

*

For
\mathcal{S}=\{2\}
, the index set
\mathcal{T}
is equal to
\mathcal{T}=\{2\}
, hence

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{2})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\inf_{c_{1}\neq 0,\,c_{2}\neq 0}\mathcal{H}_{[c_{1}b_{1,1}\ c_{2}b_{2,2}]}(\bm{u}|Y).

(353)

Since algebraic entropy is invariant against scaling of the coefficient matrix, in the infimum term in (
352
)–(
353
), the coefficients
(b_{1,1},b_{2,2})
can be set to
(1,1)
without loss of generality. In other words, we can set
{\mathbf{B}}
to the identity matrix.
The inequalities (
352
)–(
353
) are to be combined by a logical ‘or’ (due to union-taking over
\mathcal{S}
), and the result is to be combined with (
350
) and (
351
) by a logical ‘and’ operation (due to the intersection over matroids), hence we obtain, all in all,

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(U_{1};Y,U_{2})

(354a)

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(U_{2};Y,U_{1})

(354b)

\displaystyle\min\{R_{1}-\mathcal{H}(U_{1}),R_{2}-\mathcal{H}(U_{2})\}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\inf_{c_{1}\neq 0,\,c_{2}\neq 0}\mathcal{H}_{[c_{1}\ c_{2}]}(\bm{u}|Y).

(354c)

•

The off-diagonals are
b_{1,2}\neq 0
and
b_{2,1}=0
:

–

Case
M=(\{1,2\},\{1\})
: the index sets are equal to
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{T}=\{2\}
, hence we obtain the rate bound

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{2})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{b}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y).

(355)

–

Case
M=(\{1,2\},\{2\})
: The index set
\mathcal{S}
is equal to
\mathcal{S}=\{1\}
, and
\mathcal{T}
is iterated over
\mathcal{T}\in\{\{1\},\{2\}\}
, hence we obtain the pair of rate bounds

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{1})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\mathcal{H}(U_{2}|Y)=I(U_{1};Y,U_{2})

(356a)

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{2})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\mathcal{H}(U_{2}|Y).

(356b)

where the equality in (
356a
) follows from Lemmata
1
and
2
.

–

Case
M=(\{1,2\},\{1\},\{2\})
: the index set
\mathcal{S}
is iterated over
\mathcal{S}\in\{\{1\},\{2\}\}
. We make further case distinctions:

*

For
\mathcal{S}=\{1\}
, the index set
\mathcal{T}
is iterated over
\mathcal{T}\in\{\{1\},\{2\}\}
, hence we obtain the pair of inequalities

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{1})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\inf_{c_{1}\neq 0,\,c_{2}\neq 0}\mathcal{H}_{[c_{1}b_{1,1}\ c_{1}b_{1,2}+c_{2}b_{2,2}]}(\bm{u}|Y)

(357a)

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{2})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\inf_{c_{1}\neq 0,\,c_{2}\neq 0}\mathcal{H}_{[c_{1}b_{1,1}\ c_{1}b_{1,2}+c_{2}b_{2,2}]}(\bm{u}|Y).

(357b)

*

For
\mathcal{S}=\{2\}
, the index set
\mathcal{T}
is equal to
\mathcal{T}=\{2\}
, hence we obtain once more the inequality (
357b
). The pair of inequalities (
357a
)–(
357b
) is thus combined by a logical ‘or’ with (
357b
), hence what remains from this combination is only (
357b
).

The above three case distinctions on the matroid
M
are to be combined by a logical ‘and’ (due to intersections over matroids), thus leading to a set of four rate inequalities (
355
), (
356a
)–(
356b
) and (
357b
) (since (
357a
) was removed by union-taking over
\mathcal{S}
) that need to be simultaneously satisfied. Upon combining these four inequalities, we can reduce them to just two:

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(U_{1};Y,U_{2})

(358a)

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{2})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\inf_{(c_{1},c_{2})\neq(0,0)}\mathcal{H}_{[c_{1}\ c_{2}]}(\bm{u}|Y).

(358b)

To see why (
355
), (
356b
) and (
357b
) can be combined to (
358b
), observe that the infimum term in (
357b
) can be simply expressed as
\inf_{(c_{1},c_{2})\colon c_{1}\neq 0}\mathcal{H}_{[c_{1}\ c_{2}]}(\bm{u}|Y)
.

•

The off-diagonals are
b_{1,2}=0
and
b_{2,1}\neq 0
:

The derivations in this case are analogous to the previous case of
b_{1,2}\neq 0
and
b_{2,1}=0
, resulting in rate inequalities like (
358a
)–(
358b
), but with user indices swapped. That is,

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{1})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\inf_{(c_{1},c_{2})\neq(0,0)}\mathcal{H}_{[c_{1}\ c_{2}]}(\bm{u}|Y)

(359a)

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(U_{2};Y,U_{1}).

(359b)

•

The off-diagonals are
b_{1,2}\neq 0
and
b_{2,1}\neq 0
:

–

Case
M=(\{1,2\},\{1\})
: the index set
\mathcal{S}
is equal to
\mathcal{S}=\{2\}
, and
\mathcal{T}
is iterated over
\mathcal{T}\in\{\{1\},\{2\}\}
, hence we obtain the pair of rate bounds

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{1})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{b}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)

(360a)

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{2})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{b}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y).

(360b)

–

Case
M=(\{1,2\},\{2\})
: the index set
\mathcal{S}
is equal to
\mathcal{S}=\{1\}
, and
\mathcal{T}
is iterated over
\mathcal{T}\in\{\{1\},\{2\}\}
, hence we obtain the pair of rate bounds

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{1})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{b}_{2}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)

(361a)

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{2})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{b}_{2}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y).

(361b)

–

Case
M=(\{1,2\},\{1\},\{2\})
: the index set
\mathcal{S}
is iterated over
\mathcal{S}\in\{\{1\},\{2\}\}
:

*

For
\mathcal{S}=\{1\}
, the index set
\mathcal{T}
is iterated over
\mathcal{T}\in\{\{1\},\{2\}\}
, hence we obtain the pair of inequalities

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{1})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\inf_{c_{1}\neq 0,c_{2}\neq 0}\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}}(\bm{u}|Y)

(362a)

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{2})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\inf_{c_{1}\neq 0,c_{2}\neq 0}\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{B}}}(\bm{u}|Y).

(362b)

*

For
\mathcal{S}=\{2\}
, the index set
\mathcal{T}
is also iterated over
\mathcal{T}\in\{\{1\},\{2\}\}
and we obtain the same inequalities as (
362a
)–(
362b
).

The above three case distinctions on the matroid
M
are to be combined by a logical ‘and’ (due to the intersection over matroids), leading to

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{1})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\inf_{(c_{1},c_{2})\neq(0,0)}\mathcal{H}_{[c_{1}\ c_{2}]}(\bm{u}|Y)

(363a)

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{2})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\inf_{(c_{1},c_{2})\neq(0,0)}\mathcal{H}_{[c_{1}\ c_{2}]}(\bm{u}|Y).

(363b)

Due to the union-taking over matrices
{\mathbf{B}}
, the above four case distinctions on the off-diagonals of
{\mathbf{B}}
have to be combined by a logical ‘or’. Namely, the four groups of inequalities (
354a
)–(
354c
), (
358a
)–(
358b
), (
359b
)–(
359b
) and (
363a
)–(
363b
) have to be combined by logical ‘or’. As we shall see, the group of inequalities (
354a
)–(
354c
) (corresponding to a diagonal matrix
{\mathbf{B}}
) are implied by either of the latter three, that is, by either (
358a
)–(
358b
), (
359b
)–(
359b
) or (
363a
)–(
363b
). To confirm this, we first argue that the two side bounds (
354a
) and (
354b
) are implied by any of the aforementioned inequality pairs. This is because, for any
(c_{1},c_{2})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{1})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\inf_{(c_{1},c_{2})\neq(0,0)}\mathcal{H}_{[c_{1}\ c_{2}]}(\bm{u}|Y)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle I(U_{1};Y,U_{2})

(364a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{2})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\inf_{(c_{1},c_{2})\neq(0,0)}\mathcal{H}_{[c_{1}\ c_{2}]}(\bm{u}|Y)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle I(U_{2};Y,U_{1}).

(364b)

These two bounds are obtained when upper-bounding the infimum terms on the left-hand sides of (
364a
) and (
364b
) by setting
c_{1}=0
and
c_{2}\neq 0
in the former, and
c_{1}\neq 0
and
c_{2}=0
in the latter, respectively. As regards the third and last inequality (
354c
), clearly it is implied by either of the inequalities (
358b
), (
359b
), (
363a
) or (
363b
), because
\inf_{(c_{1},c_{2})\neq(0,0)}\mathcal{H}_{[c_{1}\ c_{2}]}(\bm{u}|Y)\leq\inf_{c_{1}\neq 0,\,c_{2}\neq 0}\mathcal{H}_{[c_{1}\ c_{2}]}(\bm{u}|Y)
. We conclude that in the union over
{\mathbf{B}}
, it suffices to consider diagonal matrices
{\mathbf{B}}
, which in turn can be reduced to the identity
{\mathbf{B}}={\mathbf{I}}
. Combining the rate inequalities (
354a
)–(
354c
) with the sum rate bound (
349
), we finally obtain the rate region

\displaystyle R_{1}+R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(U_{1},U_{2};Y)

(365a)

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(U_{1};Y,U_{2})

(365b)

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(U_{2};Y,U_{1})

(365c)

\displaystyle\min\{R_{1}-\mathcal{H}(U_{1}),R_{2}-\mathcal{H}(U_{2})\}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\inf_{c_{1}\neq 0,\,c_{2}\neq 0}\mathcal{H}_{[c_{1}\ c_{2}]}(\bm{u}|Y).

(365d)

Appendix R
Evaluation of
\mathscr{R}({\mathbf{A}})
for
(K,L)=(2,1)

We evaluate
\mathscr{R}({\mathbf{A}})=\bigcup_{{\mathbf{B}}}\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}})
for the special case
K=2
,
L=1
. The coefficient matrix
{\mathbf{A}}=\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}=[a_{1}\ a_{2}]\in\mathbb{A}^{1\times 2}
with
a_{1}\neq 0
and
a_{2}\neq 0
is a one-by-two row vector with non-zero entries. Since in the union operation in (
55
), the matrix
{\mathbf{B}}
is iterated over all matrices satisfying
\Lambda_{\mathbb{A}}({\mathbf{B}})\supset\Lambda_{\mathbb{A}}({\mathbf{A}})
, we infer that
{\mathbf{B}}
must run over all full-rank matrices
{\mathbf{B}}\in\mathbb{A}^{2\times 2}
as well as over all those
{\mathbf{B}}\in\mathbb{A}^{1\times 2}
that are scalar multiples of
\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}
(for which case it suffices to consider
{\mathbf{B}}=\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}
).
We make a case distinction based on the rank of
{\mathbf{B}}
.

R-
1
The matrix
{\mathbf{B}}
has rank
1

In this case,
{\mathbf{B}}
is a non-zero multiple of the coefficient vector
\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}
. Due to invariance of
\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}})
against scalar multiplication of
{\mathbf{B}}
, if will suffice to set
{\mathbf{B}}=\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}
. By virtue of the constraints on
(M,\mathcal{S},\mathcal{T})
laid out under Equation (
52
), we have that

•

the matroid
M
is the empty matroid
(\{1,2\},\emptyset)
;

•

the index set
\mathcal{S}
is the singleton set
\{1\}
;

•

the index set
\mathcal{T}
is iterated over
\{\{1\},\{2\}\}
.

The resulting rate region is thus

\displaystyle\mathscr{Q}(\mathbf{a})\triangleq\Bigl\{(R_{1},R_{2})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\colon R_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{1})-\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{2})-\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)\Bigr\}.

(366)

R-
2
The matrix
{\mathbf{B}}
has rank
2

For this case, the union
\bigcup_{{\mathbf{B}}}\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{B}})
was already computed in Appendix
Q
and shown to be equal to
\mathscr{Q}({\mathbf{I}})
, which is described by inequalities (
365a
)–(
365d
).

Appendix S
Proof of Lemma
18

As a first step, we show that if the inequality (
74
) holds, then
\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}
is also the
only
vector (with two non-zero entries) satisfying said inequality. As a consequence,
\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}
is then necessarily the
unique
minimizer that achieves the infimum in (
64b
)–(
64c
).

To prove this, let
\hat{\mathbf{a}}^{\mathsf{T}}=[\hat{a}_{1}\ \hat{a}_{2}]\in\mathbb{A}^{2}
denote some other coefficient vector with non-zero entries, that is linearly independent of
\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}=[a_{1}\ a_{2}]
. Then we have

\displaystyle d(\bm{u}|Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\frac{H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y)}{\log(\nu)}

(367)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\biggl\{\frac{H(a_{1}\lfloor\nu U_{1}\rfloor+a_{2}\lfloor\nu U_{2}\rfloor|Y)}{\log(\nu)}+\frac{H(\hat{a}_{1}\lfloor\nu U_{1}\rfloor+\hat{a}_{2}\lfloor\nu U_{2}\rfloor|Y,a_{1}\lfloor\nu U_{1}\rfloor+a_{2}\lfloor\nu U_{2}\rfloor)}{\log(\nu)}\biggr\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)+d_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)-\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\frac{I(\hat{a}_{1}\lfloor\nu U_{1}\rfloor+\hat{a}_{2}\lfloor\nu U_{2}\rfloor;a_{1}\lfloor\nu U_{1}\rfloor+a_{2}\lfloor\nu U_{2}\rfloor|Y)}{\log(\nu)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)+d_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y).

To prove the last equality, we argue that the mutual information
I(\hat{a}_{1}\lfloor\nu U_{1}\rfloor+\hat{a}_{2}\lfloor\nu U_{2}\rfloor;a_{1}\lfloor\nu U_{1}\rfloor+a_{2}\lfloor\nu U_{2}\rfloor|Y)
has a finite upper bound that is independent of
\nu
. In fact, by Lemma
8
and the data-processing inequality,

I(
^

a

_1 ⌊
ν
U_1⌋+
^

a

_2 ⌊
ν
U_2⌋; a_1 ⌊
ν
U_1⌋+ a_2 ⌊
ν
U_2⌋— Y)

(368)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\log(\lVert\hat{\mathbf{a}}\rVert_{1})+I(\lfloor\hat{a}_{1}\nu U_{1}+\hat{a}_{2}\nu U_{2}\rfloor;\lfloor a_{1}\nu U_{1}+a_{2}\nu U_{2}\rfloor|Y)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\log(\lVert\hat{\mathbf{a}}\rVert_{1})+I(\hat{a}_{1}U_{1}+\hat{a}_{2}U_{2};a_{1}U_{1}+a_{2}U_{2}|Y).

It now suffices to prove the finiteness of the mutual information term on the right-hand side of (
368
).
We make a case distinction depending on the setting:

Theorems
1
and
2

In these cases,
(U_{1},U_{2})
is discrete and the mutual information term can be upper-bounded by discrete entropies as

\displaystyle I(\hat{a}_{1}U_{1}+\hat{a}_{2}U_{2};a_{1}U_{1}+a_{2}U_{2}|Y)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle H(\hat{a}_{1}U_{1}+\hat{a}_{2}U_{2}|Y)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle H(U_{1})+H(U_{2})

which is finite by assumption.

Theorem
3

In this case,
(U_{1},U_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
is absolutely continuous and
I(U_{1},U_{2};Y)
is assumed finite. The mutual information term can be expressed as

I(
^

a

_1 U_1 +
^

a

_2 U_2 ; a_1 U_1 + a_2 U_2 — Y)

(369)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h(\hat{a}_{1}U_{1}+\hat{a}_{2}U_{2}|Y)+h(a_{1}U_{1}+a_{2}U_{2}|Y)-h(\hat{a}_{1}U_{1}+\hat{a}_{2}U_{2},a_{1}U_{1}+a_{2}U_{2}|Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h(\hat{a}_{1}U_{1}+\hat{a}_{2}U_{2}|Y)+h(a_{1}U_{1}+a_{2}U_{2}|Y)-h(U_{1},U_{2}|Y)-\log\bigl|\det\bigl(\bigl[\hat{\mathbf{a}}\ \mathbf{a}\bigr]\bigr)\bigr|.

Given that
H(\lfloor\bm{u}\rfloor|Y)
,
h(U_{1}|Y)
,
h(U_{2}|Y)
and
h(U_{1},U_{2}|Y)
are finite, by Lemma
9
, the first two differential entropy terms
h(\hat{a}_{1}U_{1}+\hat{a}_{2}U_{2}|Y)
and
h(a_{1}U_{1}+a_{2}U_{2}|Y)
are finite too.
This closes the previous argument. We conclude that in all cases,

d(\bm{u}|Y)=d_{\mathbf{a}}(\bm{u}|Y)+d_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}(\bm{u}|Y).

Hence, by the data-processing inequality,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\liminf_{\nu\to\infty}\Bigl\{H(\lfloor\nu\bm{u}\rfloor|Y)-d(\bm{u}|Y)\log(\nu)\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\liminf_{\nu\to\infty}\Bigl\{H(a_{1}\lfloor\nu U_{1}\rfloor+a_{2}\lfloor\nu U_{2}\rfloor|Y)+H(\hat{a}_{1}\lfloor\nu U_{1}\rfloor+\hat{a}_{2}\lfloor\nu U_{2}\rfloor|Y,a_{1}\lfloor\nu U_{1}\rfloor+a_{2}\lfloor\nu U_{2}\rfloor)

- d_
a
^
T
(
u
—Y)log(
ν
) - d_
^

a

^
T
(
u
—Y)log(
ν
)
}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\liminf_{\nu\to\infty}\Bigl\{H(a_{1}\lfloor\nu U_{1}\rfloor+a_{2}\lfloor\nu U_{2}\rfloor|Y)+H(\hat{a}_{1}\lfloor\nu U_{1}\rfloor+\hat{a}_{2}\lfloor\nu U_{2}\rfloor|Y)

- d_
a
^
T
(
u
—Y)log(
ν
) - d_
^

a

^
T
(
u
—Y)log(
ν
)
}

(370)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)+\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y).

As a consequence,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{1})-\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{1})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)

(371a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{2})-\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{2})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y).

(371b)

The sum of the right-hand sides of this pair of inequalities equals

I(\bm{u};Y)+2\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)

which due to (
65
) is upper-bounded by the sum rate
I(\bm{u};Y)
.
Hence
\mathscr{Q}(\hat{\mathbf{a}}^{\mathsf{T}})
, which is delimited by the left-hand side quantities of (
371a
)–(
371b
), is contained in
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}
.
Therefore,
\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}
is the only coefficient vector such that
\mathscr{Q}(\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}})
is
not
contained in
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}
, while for any other (linearly independent) vector
\hat{\mathbf{a}}^{\mathsf{T}}
, we have
\mathscr{Q}(\hat{\mathbf{a}}^{\mathsf{T}})\subseteq\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}
. Consequently, if the inequality (
74
) holds, then
\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}
is the
unique
maximizer of the set
\mathscr{Q}(\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}})
, i.e., the unique minimizer of
\mathbf{c}^{\mathsf{T}}\mapsto\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{c}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)
over vectors
\mathbf{c}^{\mathsf{T}}
with non-zero entries [cf. (
73a
)–(
73b
)]. That is,

\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}=\argmin_{c_{1}\neq 0,\,c_{2}\neq 0}\mathcal{H}_{[c_{1}\ c_{2}]}(\bm{u}|Y).

(372)

Since, as argued in (
63
), (
64a
)–(
64c
) and Lemma
17
, the condition (
65
) entails a non-empty set
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}\cap\overline{\mathscr{R}^{\prime}}
, i.e., the set described by the inequalities (
64a
)–(
64c
). By substracting (
64b
) from (
64c
), and (
64c
) from (
64a
), it becomes clear that any element from
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}\cap\overline{\mathscr{R}^{\prime}}
will satisfy

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{1})-\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)

(373a)

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{2})-\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)

(373b)

and thus, we have
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}\cap\overline{\mathscr{R}^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathscr{Q}(\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}})
. As a consequence [cf. (
72
), (
63
)],

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}(\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}\cup\mathscr{Q}(\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}})

(374)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}\setminus(\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}\cap\overline{\mathscr{R}^{\prime}})\cup\mathscr{Q}(\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}\cup\mathscr{Q}(\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}).

This proves (
75
) and thus the first part of Lemma
18
.

By contrast, if
\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}
satisfies the reverse of the inequality (
74
), then we distinguish two cases:

Case
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}\subsetneq\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}

By Lemma
17
, there exists a vector
\mathbf{c}^{\mathsf{T}}
such that
2\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{c}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)<\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)
. As argued before [cf. (
372
)], this coefficient vector is the unique minimizer of
\tilde{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathsf{T}}\mapsto\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)
, hence

\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{c}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)=\min_{\tilde{c}_{1}\neq 0,\,\tilde{c}_{2}\neq 0}\mathcal{H}_{[\tilde{c}_{1}\ \tilde{c}_{2}]}(\bm{u}|Y).

(375)

Yet since we have assumed, on the other hand, that
2\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)\geq\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)
, it follows that
\mathbf{a}
and
\mathbf{c}
are distinct, linearly independent vectors. Re-using the bounding steps performed in (
370
) for a pair of linearly independent coefficient vectors, we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)+\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{c}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y).

Similarly as in (
371a
)–(
371b
),
the latter allows us to write down the following two inequalities:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{1})-\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{1})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{c}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)

(376a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{2})-\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(U_{2})-\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)+\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{c}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y).

(376b)

in which
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{c}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)
may be written as the minimum (
375
).
Hence the set
\mathscr{Q}(\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}})
, which is delimited by the quantities on the left-hand sides of (
376a
)–(
376b
), is contained in
\mathscr{R}^{\prime}
[cf. (
62
)]. Given that
2\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)\geq\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)
, we also have that
\mathscr{Q}(\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}})\subset\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}
. Taken together, we have
\mathscr{Q}(\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}})\subseteq\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}\cap\mathscr{R}^{\prime}=\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}(\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}\cup\mathscr{Q}(\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}})

(377)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}.

Case
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}=\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}

By Lemma
17
, there exists no vector
\mathbf{c}^{\mathsf{T}}
such that
2\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{c}^{\mathsf{T}}}(\bm{u}|Y)<\mathcal{H}(\bm{u}|Y)
. Therefore, all sets
\mathscr{Q}(\mathbf{c}^{\mathsf{T}})
(for all
\mathbf{c}^{\mathsf{T}}
with non-zero entries) are subsets of
\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}=\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{MAC}}
. As in the previous case, it follows that
\mathscr{R}(\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}})=\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{LMAC}}
.

This proves (
76
) and thus concludes the proof of Lemma
18
.
